# Runtime V2 Debt Ledger

This is the durable debt ledger for Runtime V2 work. Add new debt here when it
is discovered. Close debt only with evidence: commit, task, command, or linked
document.

## Rules

- Do not hide debt in `NOTES.md` only. `NOTES.md` is a handoff log; durable debt
  belongs here.
- Every debt item must have an owner: an epic, task, or explicit future
  decision point.
- Every runtime task must state whether it touches any open debt item.
- Closing debt requires evidence and a dated note.
- Raising a legacy LOC ceiling in `.loc-legacy-allowlist` is a debt decision and
  must update this file or the owning task evidence.

## Open Debt

**The Status column is authoritative, not this heading.** A closed row keeps its
place in this table rather than moving, so that the causal text explaining why
the debt existed stays where the next reader of that area will find it — the
same reason the Owner Overrides section below leaves historical owner cells
alone. As a result this table holds every row, and 38 of the ones in it are
already `Closed`. Scoping work by reading the section title, or by reading a row
without its Status cell, will therefore over-count what is outstanding; that
mistake has been made on this ledger more than once. Read the Status cell, and
when a row names an epic in its Owner cell, check that epic's own scope section
before treating the row as yours — a row can name an epic and still be excluded
by it.

| RV2-DEBT-031 | The SLOT budget landed (2026-08-28 transport rulings): one data credit per pointer envelope, an independent control reserve holding only release-and-teardown traffic, and two live counters, `data_credit_stalls` and `control_reserve_stalls`. The classes split on what a message does, which removed the live contract failure where sixteen completions filled the reserve and refused the next ack. The dead placeholder went with it — `RT_TRANSPORT_MSG_CREDIT_CONTROL` and the old `credit_stalls` are deleted, and `payload_len` left the pointer path because the transport owns none of the bytes behind the pointer. What remains deferred: a BYTE credit, which is a measurement only over a buffer the transport itself owns and so waits for a serialized or handed-over path; whether a completion or reply takes an ordinary data slot or a reply slot reserved when its request was admitted; and the producer park — a sender at the bound still sees QUEUE_FULL and `rt_remote_spawn_enqueue_with_drain` still drains once and retries. **CLOSED 2026-09-03 by Wave E step E4.** The reply-slot reservation is decided: a request that expects a data-lane reply reserves one slot on its own shard's lane before it is admitted anywhere, ordinary data admission counts reservations as occupied, the reply spends the reservation and can never be refused, and a pending resolved without a reply gives it back exactly once (`reply_reserved`, `rt_remote_admission`). The producer park replaces the caller-visible QUEUE_FULL: a request that finds its target's lane -- or its own lane's reservation -- exhausted parks its task on the shard's slot key (`WAKER_TRANSPORT_SLOT`, `rt_remote_admit.c`, `SP_TRANSPORT_DATA_SLOT_TASK_PARKED` armed there) and answers PENDING; a data pop or a released reservation on that lane wakes it; no crossing emitter has a QUEUE_FULL arm any more. Witnesses: `anchored-saturation-parks-the-producer-and-a-freed-slot-wakes-it` (`parks=1 wakes=1 data_stalls=0/2 reserved=0/0`, the window reached once), the rewritten refusal rows across spawn/immediate/select/await/drop, and Rule 13 `TestRuntimeV2TransportSaturationParkNegativeControl` (drain-once-and-refuse restored: `saturated data lane did not park the producer`). The byte credit is not built and not deferred any further: the 29.08 ruling (`docs/RUNTIME_V2.md` §8) says a pointer transport owns no bytes to measure one over, and the model text now says what the transport does instead. Control messages keep drain-once-and-retry, because their issuers cannot park. | Closed 2026-09-03 (E4: reply slot reserved at admission, producer parks; byte credit void by ruling) | Epic 23b Wave E (E4) | A transport-owned buffer arrives and byte credits are measured over it, the reply-slot reservation is decided, and the producer park replaces the caller-visible QUEUE_FULL — or a re-baseline records slot-budget-plus-refusal as the permanent contract with measured saturation evidence. |

Local `ch.close()` INSIDE anchored blocks already closes the channel VALUE for everyone (that is channel semantics, not registry semantics) — the deferred piece is only the registry-level force-release. | Open | Shared-handle follow-up (design review first) | The capability lands with its own review and race rows, or the review records release-only as the permanent registry contract. |

The deferred tail is Model B (arms as anchored micro-ops over the proven cancel-inflight discipline) as the honest multi-owner slow path, then Model A (cross-shard waiter registration) ONLY behind a profile signal AND a dedicated waiter-store hardening vertical — remote select must never be the first consumer of a waiter-store change (Epic 17 fork resolution + recorded B-vs-A divergence, `17-remote-select.md`). The reusable selector lifecycle seam is recorded in `17-tasks/05-stabilization-closeout.md`; any tail vertical starts from that table and the Task 2 cancel-gate defect record. | Open | Profile-gated: a workload where the single-owner restriction binds | Model B ships the multi-owner slow path behind the recorded seam, or the restriction is re-recorded as permanent language surface with review. |
| RV2-DEBT-035 | Small-int inline representation (fixnum) eliminated the pervasive int/uint allocation leak: values within the inline range (int ±2^62, uint 0..2^63−1) never touch the heap, and `parse_uint_string` no longer leaks its per-digit scratch, so hot integer loops are allocation-balanced (`while i<1_000_000 { i = i+1 }` valgrind: 16,000,016 allocs = 16,000,016 frees, definitely lost 0). The residual is the heap-bignum TAIL: values outside the inline range stay boxed `SurgeBigInt`/`SurgeBigUint`, and because int/uint are `Copy` the drop system never frees them, so a genuinely-big int retained to scope exit leaks. **CONFIRMED FROM A SECOND DIRECTION 2026-08-14, while closing RV2-DEBT-215/216: this residual is what blocks Range bound release, and the blocker is stronger than "nobody wrote the call".** A range with bounds ≥ 2^62 reports its two bound boxes as INDIRECTLY lost through the range (256 bytes in 16 blocks over eight iterations); the range is now freed, so those bytes move to DEFINITELY lost, unchanged in size. `rt_range_free` cannot release them because a heap bignum int has NO exported lifecycle at all - `bi_free` is a `static inline` in `rt_bignum_internal.h` with no declaration in `rt.h`, and `IsRefCountedScalar` answers true only for `float`. Any fix for this row therefore has to EXPORT a lifecycle before any holder can give a bound back, and `rt_range_free` is the first waiting caller. The VM already does release both bounds (`internal/vm/heap.go:198-209`), so this is a lane disagreement and not only a leak. Arithmetic that consumes a big intermediate demotes/frees it (`bi_finish`), so the tail only leaks values kept past their last consuming op. | Open | Fixnum RC phase (order-A step 2): reference-count heap bignums, atomic RC for MT-native | Heap bignums outside the inline range are reference-counted (or made droppable) and freed at last use / scope exit; a big-int loop shows definitely-lost 0. |
| RV2-DEBT-036 | Source int/uint literals carrying text lower to `rt_bigint_from_literal`/`rt_biguint_from_literal` (runtime base-10 string parse) re-materialized on every use, so a hot loop condition (`i < 1000000`) re-parses its constant each iteration. Now leak-free (parse scratch freed, result demoted to inline) but costs O(digits) transient alloc/free per use (~16 alloc+free/iter in the counter loop). MEASURED 2026-07-23 (evidence moved here from the withdrawn RV2-DEBT-071): the cost is two heap allocations PER DECIMAL DIGIT, every time the literal is evaluated. `parse_uint_string` (`runtime/native/rt_bignum_parse.c` ~76-101) walks the digits doing `bu_mul_small` then `bu_add_small`, each allocating a fresh `SurgeBigUint` and freeing the previous. In a loop this is per-iteration: `while i < 100000 { i = i + 1; }` costs 14 allocations per iteration — 12 for the 6-digit literal in the condition, 2 for the 1-digit literal in the body — giving `1,400,015 allocs` over 100,000 iterations. CONTROL: hoisting both literals into bindings takes the identical program to `17` allocations for the whole run, i.e. zero per iteration. Nothing leaks (allocs and frees balance exactly); this is pure churn. The fix is constant-folding an in-range literal to a tagged word at COMPILE time instead of parsing a decimal string at runtime. SEQUENCING NOTE from the RV2-DEBT-068 design review: this should land BEFORE any copy-on-write benchmarking, because it dominates the baseline — measuring reclamation cost against a baseline shaped by literal churn would make the numbers mean something other than what they appear to. CLOSED 2026-07-23. Fix: `emitConst` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_term.go`) folds an in-range int/uint literal to a tagged fixnum WORD at compile time — `0 -> null`, otherwise `inttoptr (v<<1)\|1` per rt_bignum_tag.h — with zero runtime materialization, replacing the per-use `rt_big*_from_literal` decimal parse. The text is the source of truth for the in-range test (`numlit.ParseInt64/ParseUint64`), because `IntValue` is 0 on int64 overflow; out-of-range literals keep the parse path unchanged. Measured: the 100,000-iteration counter loop drops from 1,400,015 allocations to 3 for the whole run (zero per iteration). Correctness verified across small/negative/zero/out-of-range/uint/arithmetic and at the exact fixnum boundaries (2^62-1 inline vs 2^62 heap; 2^63-1 inline uint vs 2^63 heap), LLVM matching the VM byte-for-byte on every case. Gate: `TestRuntimeV2InRangeLiteralsFoldToFixnum` asserts a hot literal-bearing loop's allocation traffic stays flat, with a negative control (reverting the fold reproduces the per-iteration churn). SIDE EFFECT worth recording as a method note: three heap-census tests had encoded this literal churn into their expected free/alloc counts — `TestRuntimeV2DropSelectSendArm` (select windows 2->0, 3->1), `TestRuntimeV2DropScopeExit` (scope-end 6->4, view-order 9->5), and `TestLLVMNativeBufferedChannelAllocatesSingleBlock` (buffered-vs-unbuffered delta +2->+0, revealing the whole historical difference was the `1:uint` re-parse and the ring buffer is genuinely co-allocated into the one rt_channel block). Each was recalibrated to the clean baseline, not silenced: the reductions were proven balanced churn (alloc fell with free; valgrind A/B on the completing scope-exit program shows byte-identical 1,320B/28blk pre and post, all from rt_heap_stats/rt_range, none from bi_alloc). This is exactly the review's Phase -1 warning — literal churn dominated these baselines, so folding it changes what every such census means. | Open | Closed (in-range literals fold to inline words at compile time) | Small literals that fit i64/u64 emit `rt_bigint_from_i64`/`rt_biguint_from_u64` (inline, zero-alloc), and/or loop-invariant literal materialization is hoisted; the counter loop reaches near-zero allocs/iter. |
| RV2-DEBT-038 | Arbitrary-precision `float` (WidthAny) has the same never-freed heap shape the int leak had before fixnum: every bigfloat op allocates a `SurgeBigFloat` (mantissa biguint + struct) that the drop system never frees (`float` is `Copy`). SCOPED 2026-07-15: (1) Inline-as-f64 (the fixnum analog) is OUT — `float` keeps full arbitrary precision (`1.0/3.0` prints ~250 digits, matching the VM), so an f64 inline would change output and break the vm↔llvm differential; only exactly-f64-representable dyadics could inline (too narrow). (2) The parity-safe fix is to make WidthAny `float` a RECLAIMED type (freed on scope exit) while KEEPING value semantics (no move errors). That needs the ownership/borrow model extended to a heap-Copy scalar: a borrowed read (`rt_bigfloat_add(a,b)` — `const void*` params) must NOT clone, a stored duplicate (`let b = a`) MUST clone, every owner freed once — the SAME general mechanism DEBT-035 (heap int) needs. A naive "clone every copy" over-clones (leaks clones handed to borrowing ops); a naive "flip `float` to non-Copy" segfaults LLVM and hangs the VM even on `let a = 1.0; print(a)`, because move/drop/clone for a bigfloat exists in NEITHER backend yet. GROUNDWORK LANDED: `rt_bigfloat_clone`/`rt_bigfloat_free` runtime primitives + builtins registration. Remaining is a milestoned epic: [M1] native drop leaf (emitInstrDrop→rt_bigfloat_free)+typeOwnsHeap+move/clone; [M2] VM VKBigFloat drop/clone/move; [M3] sema auto-clone so value semantics survive (no move errors); [M4] flip IsCopy(WidthAny float)=false + async/channel/capture paths; verify no double-free/leak + differential at each. | Open (groundwork landed; milestoned epic) | Ownership-model reclamation for heap-Copy `float` (shared design with DEBT-035) | Bigfloat values are freed on scope exit; a bigfloat loop shows definitely-lost 0; vm↔llvm output unchanged; no double-free (valgrind). |
| RV2-DEBT-049 | An `@entrypoint` async main body can silently not execute (or stop mid-body) with process exit 0. Observed 2026-07-17 under valgrind on the select probes, BIMODAL across identical invocations: full runs allocate normally, truncated runs exit 0 with runtime-baseline-only heap traffic and missing program output. ESCALATED same day: reproduced NATIVELY (no valgrind) in bursts — during a bad window the same async-main binary fails 4-6 consecutive runs (exit 0, zero stdout), then passes 20/20 minutes later; host-state/timing dependent (WSL2, 32 CPUs, low load). Mechanism captured by `SURGE_TRACE_EXEC=1` on a failing-window run: `ctrl_create=1`, `tasks_user=1`, `tasks_running=1`, `tasks_done=0`, `worker_sleep=32`, `wake_called=0`, `park_committed=0`, `reason=exit` — the entrypoint task is created and published, all 32 workers go to sleep without ever polling it, no wake is issued, and the process exits 0; `strace` confirms `exit_group(0)` with zero stdout writes. This is a startup lost-wake (task published ↔ worker sleep race), same hazard family as the PARKED-store protocol races, on the worker-pool bring-up path. Sync-main binaries are unaffected in the same windows. Matters for Epic 20: census/e2e rows on async programs can silently "pass" or spuriously fail; harnesses must assert an execution witness and may explicitly retry ONLY on the exact empty-output+exit-0 signature with a loud log naming this row. | Open | Runtime liveness follow-up (startup wake protocol); Epic 20 census tasks carry execution witnesses + labeled signature retries meanwhile | Minimal repro + root cause of the startup lost-wake; the exit path cannot report 0 while a created user task was never polled; census harness rows assert an execution witness; the labeled retries are removed. |
| RV2-DEBT-050 | An async `@entrypoint`'s return value does not become the process exit code: `@entrypoint async fn main() -> int { return 42; }` exits 0 on the LLVM/native backend (verified 2026-07-17; a SYNC `fn main() -> int` propagates its code correctly — the drop-reclamation e2e suite relies on it). The async entry path (task result → `__surge_start` → `rt_entry`) drops the result bits instead of converting them to the exit status. Consequence for harnesses: async e2e rows cannot signal failure through exit codes and must use stdout markers (execution witness + FAIL markers), which `TestRuntimeV2DropSelectSendArm` does. Found during Epic 20 Task 3 while wiring the select-send reclamation rows. | Open | Runtime entry-path follow-up (can ride any Epic 20 runtime task or a small standalone fix) | An async entrypoint returning `int` sets the process exit status like the sync form; a golden/e2e row covers both forms; async harness rows may then drop their stdout-marker workaround. |
| RV2-DEBT-054 | A boxed/composite `let` re-declared each pass through a `while` loop body leaks on the LLVM backend — no for-loop involved: `while outer < 2000 { let arr = [1,2,3]; ... }` leaks 2000 x 24B (the array header/backing of each pass; a stored `Range<T>` value leaks the same way). Found 2026-07-19 during the RV2-DEBT-040 fix (reproduced with the for-loop removed entirely; the iterator census keeps this confound out of its window by declaring iterables once, outside the measured loop). Distinct, general drop-emission gap: scope-exit obligations do not fire per-iteration for while-body locals (Epic 19's scope-exit synthesis covers function/block scope ends — the loop back-edge is not an emission point). | Open | Drop-emission follow-up (same family as Epic 19 scope-exit work) | A droppable `let` inside a while body is reclaimed on every iteration (back-edge or block-end emission); a census row pins d(N) independence; the RV2-DEBT-040 census rows can then drop their declare-outside workaround note. |
| RV2-DEBT-056 | A 3-element array of struct values leaks its element boxes on the LLVM backend: 48 bytes / 3 blocks, CONSTANT regardless of how often the array is iterated or read (matches the struct-literal array construction, not any loop) — the array's own drop does not reclaim struct element boxes, or the literal's element temporaries never acquire obligations. Found 2026-07-19 during the RV2-DEBT-040 review round (the struct[] census probe cancels it in its differential). Epic 19's composite work covered array-of-droppables for strings/views — the struct-element case appears to sit outside that coverage. | Open | Drop-emission follow-up (verify against Epic 19's array-of-droppables rows, then fix or fold into RV2-DEBT-054's family) | An array of struct values reclaims element boxes exactly once when the array drops; a leaf census row pins it; the struct[] iterator probe can then assert strict zero instead of relying on differential cancellation. |
| RV2-DEBT-061 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing, newly found 2026-07-20 while building RV2-DEBT-060's e2e coverage: `on ch { ... }` (the anchored/immediate-on channel-operation surface) carries an intermittent, RARE (roughly 10-25% of runs across two independent repros) invalid-free / invalid-write under valgrind on the LLVM backend, in the immediate-on retry/completion path — NOT in any far-channel handle or object code. Two stack shapes seen: (a) `rt_free` called from a compiled `drop.typeN` (the crossing state's own composite drop glue) invoked via `__surge_drop_call` from `rt_remote_task_pending_release` -> `rt_remote_task_pending_consume` -> `rt_immediate_on_finish_retry` -> `rt_immediate_on_execute_anchored`, freeing a block already freed elsewhere ("0 bytes inside a block of size N free'd"); (b) a size-1 invalid write inside `rt_immediate_on_dispatch_execute` (`rt_immediate_on.c:271`, called from `rt_remote_task_dispatch_message` -> `rt_remote_spawn_drain_inbound_locked` -> `rt_worker_main` on a DIFFERENT thread) landing inside a block `rt_remote_task_pending_release` (`rt_remote_task_pending.c:123`) had just freed — looks like a worker-thread write racing a pending's refcounted release on another thread. CONFIRMED PRE-EXISTING AND UNRELATED to RV2-DEBT-060/061's own work: reproduces on unmodified pre-Epic-21 HEAD (commit 7e56a513) at a similar rate (2/8 manual runs) with a program that only spawns tasks doing `on ch { ch.send(...)/ch.recv(...) }` in a loop — no far-channel handle-drop or channel-object-free code involved at all in the crash traces. Confirmed NOT caused by either the handle-drop fix (RV2-DEBT-060, `emitInstrDrop`'s far-channel case) or an earlier abandoned attempt to also drop far-channel composite fields via `emitDropValue` (removed after this investigation — see RV2-DEBT-060's closure note) — both variants showed the identical intermittent rate, matching clean HEAD. Repro not yet reduced to a minimal, deterministic form; the two programs used during investigation both mix real crossing traffic (send/recv through `on ch {...}`) with repeated `.share()`, at 4 iterations, under `valgrind --leak-check=full`. RV2-DEBT-060's own gate test (`TestRuntimeV2DropFarChannelHandleAndObjectValgrindZero`) deliberately avoids `on ch {...}` (create+share+drop only) specifically to stay race-free and not flake on this bug. ROOT-CAUSE LEAD (2026-07-20, found by codex during the Epic 21 Task 6 design review, lead-verified by direct read — a lead, not a confirmed fix): the publication-accepted handoff write `pending->state_owned = 0` (`rt_immediate_on.c` — this is shape (b)'s own crash-site line, ~271, already cited above) executes AFTER `state->lock` is released (unlocked a few lines earlier); it is a plain, unsynchronized write. `rt_remote_task_pending_release`'s free path reads `pending->state_owned`/`body_state` with no lock at all. A caller-side release racing the owner's handoff around this window has no synchronization between the two: a stale `state_owned==1` read during the race window would double-drop `body_state` via `__surge_drop_call` (matches shape (a) exactly), and the unsynchronized write itself can land in memory a concurrent release just freed (matches shape (b) exactly, same line the original crash evidence already pointed to). Not fixed here — Epic 21 Task 6 needs 053b/059 proven independently of this race, not entangled with fixing it. Candidate fix direction for whoever picks this up: move the `state_owned = 0` transfer under `state->lock`, in the same re-check window the surrounding code (`~242-254`) already uses for the analogous status check. PARTIAL PROGRESS 2026-07-23, row stays OPEN. A code-confirmed CONTRACT VIOLATION was found and repaired at the two sites this row's stack traces name, but the intermittent signature itself was NOT reproduced, so the repair is unproven against it. The publication-accepted handoff contract (`rt_remote_spawn_internal.h`) states its own safety invariant explicitly: "No path sees both releases because the dispatch lane still holds a pending reference when it clears the flag: the plain store is ordered before that reference's acq_rel refcount drop." The SPAWN family satisfies that by construction — its reference only leaves with the ACK, enqueued after the store. `rt_immediate_on_dispatch_execute` and `rt_far_channel_dispatch_select` do NOT: both hand the in-flight reference to the owner registration BEFORE `rt_remote_spawn_publish_body_task`, and publishing makes the body runnable, so another thread can complete it, consume that registration, drop the last reference, and free the pending before `pending->state_owned = 0` executes. That is exactly this row's failure shape (b) — a size-1 invalid write at `rt_immediate_on.c:271` landing in a block `rt_remote_task_pending_release` had just freed — and shape (a) is the same window read from the other side (a stale `state_owned` read double-dropping `body_state`). FIX APPLIED: both dispatches now take an explicit pending reference before publication and release it after the store, restoring the invariant the contract already claims. NOT PROVEN: a probe built to this row's own recipe — `on ch` send/recv through `.share()`d sibling leases, four iterations, under valgrind — did not reproduce the race in 50 baseline runs across `SURGE_SHARDS/THREADS` of 2/2, 2/4, 4/8 and 8/8, versus this row's recorded 10-25%, so the probe shape does not reach the window and cannot discriminate fixed from unfixed. The repair is kept because it is a four-line restoration of a documented invariant that the code demonstrably breaks, not because it was measured. SYNC POINT ADDED 2026-07-23, AND THE HYPOTHESIS DID NOT HOLD. `SP_IMMEDIATE_ON_AFTER_PUBLISH` now exists (allowlisted in `rt_sync_point.h` and `check_sync_points.sh`) exactly where this row's analysis said the window is: between `rt_remote_spawn_publish_body_task` and the `state_owned` store. It IS reachable — a harness row parked the dispatch there and the reached-count fired. But the free-inside-the-window shape did NOT reproduce, in either of two attempts: with the dispatch held at that point, (1) letting the published body complete and the caller resolve, and (2) additionally cancelling the caller inside the window to force the teardown sweep, both left the pending's reference count above zero, so the free path never ran there and no drop occurred. Decisive detail: BOTH attempts behaved identically with the repair applied and with it reverted, so the row was a test that could not fail and was REMOVED rather than kept as a false proof; the sync point itself is kept, since it is the correct window and is now available to whoever picks this up. CONSEQUENCE FOR THE REPAIR'S STANDING: it restores an invariant the handoff contract explicitly states and the code demonstrably broke, and that justification is unchanged — but two independent attempts now fail to show the invariant's violation is REACHABLE, so the repair should not be described as fixing this row's observed corruption. It is entirely possible the observed intermittent invalid-free has a different cause than the publication window, and the next investigation should treat the diagnosis as unconfirmed rather than as a lead already banked. What is now ruled out, and worth not repeating: draining the other references while parked at the window, with and without a forced caller cancel. | Open | Immediate-on / anchored crossing correctness (rt_immediate_on.c, rt_remote_task_pending.c) — needs its own root-cause investigation, not row-sized inside Epic 21 Task 7 | A minimal, reliably-reproducing repro exists (deterministic sync-point forcing, per this codebase's established technique, rather than a probabilistic loop); the race is root-caused (state-drop double-free vs. a genuine cross-thread write-after-free in the retry/dispatch path — the two stack shapes may be one root cause or two, and the root-cause lead above suggests they may be the SAME cause); a fix lands with a negative control; a gate row exercises `on ch {...}` under valgrind without flaking. |
| RV2-DEBT-062 | The recursive composite drop-glue walk (`typeOwnsHeapRec`/`emitDropGlueBody`/`emitFieldDropAt`, `emit_drop_glue.go`) silently skips handle-shaped types — confirmed for `Task<T>` and `Channel<T>` — nested as a struct/tuple/union field or array element: `typeOwnsHeapRec`'s default case explicitly returns false for anything that isn't string/dynamic-array/struct/tuple/union (`emit_drop_glue.go` ~93-96, "Enums..., maps..., references, pointers, scalars: no owned heap the drop glue reclaims here"), so a field of one of these handle types is never visited, and the box it points to (a small runtime-owned wrapper — 24B for a local `Task<T>` handle observed) is never freed by the generic walk. Found 2026-07-20 while building RV2-DEBT-059's local repro: `TestRuntimeV2CancelledSuspendStateReclaimed`'s abandoned-state box packs a live `Task<T>` local (from `checkpoint().await()`) into its payload, and after 059's fix correctly frees the OUTER state box, exactly this residual remains (24B direct, git-stash A/B confirmed: 32B direct + 24B indirect before 059, 24B direct + 0 indirect after — the 32B outer box is the only thing 059's fix reclaims). A second, independent repro using `own Channel<int>` instead of `Task<T>` hit the SAME class at a different size (24B direct + 48B indirect, git-stash A/B: 32B+72B before, 24B+48B after) — confirms this isn't Task<T>-specific. These handle types are known to have their OWN dedicated reclaim mechanism elsewhere in the compiler (e.g. the far-channel case in `emitInstrDrop`, RV2-DEBT-060's closure note) for ordinary top-level scope-exit `InstrDrop`, but that mechanism is never consulted by the recursive composite walk when the same handle type shows up NESTED inside a larger value being dropped via `requireDropGlue`. This plausibly affects every OTHER existing consumer of `requireDropGlue`'s recursive descent too (crossing state via `registerCrossingDropState`, results via `registerCrossingDropResult`, and now RV2-DEBT-059's `registerAbandonedStateDrop`), not just the new mechanism that found it — any composite carrying a live Task<T>/Channel<T>/similar-handle field, dropped via any of these paths, would leak that field's own handle box. Not fixed here (out of scope for RV2-DEBT-059, which only needed the outer box). **CLOSED 2026-09-03.** The walk now routes every handle-shaped leaf nested in a composite to its own release: the local task handle (`rt_task_handle_drop`, the arm added with RV2-DEBT-198's residual), the local channel (`rt_channel_handle_drop`, D3b C1) and, last, the far channel handle (`rt_far_channel_handle_drop`, Wave E step E3). Evidence: `TestRuntimeV2CancelledSuspendStateReclaimed` -- the row's own `Task<T>` repro -- reads `definitely_lost=0B/0blk indirectly_lost=0B/0blk` under valgrind; the far-select cancel copy control (`TestRuntimeV2FarSelectCancelNonCopySendArm/valgrind`), which pinned this row's shape as a 48-byte-in-2-blocks baseline -- the two far-channel tokens a cancelled task's abandoned frame held as fields -- reads 0 bytes in 0 blocks since E3's arm, and the pin was re-taken to strict zero rather than left describing a leak that is gone. The drop-glue agreement gate names every leaf family the glue reaches and refuses an empty body for a droppable composite, so a new handle-shaped family cannot re-open this gap silently. | Closed 2026-09-03 (every handle-shaped leaf routes to its own release; both repros strict zero) | LLVM backend drop-glue generality (`emit_drop_glue.go`) | `typeOwnsHeapRec`/`emitFieldDropAt` (or an equivalent dedicated path) recognizes handle-shaped types nested in a composite and routes each to its correct release call; the two known repros (`TestRuntimeV2CancelledSuspendStateReclaimed`'s Task<T> case, and the sibling Channel<T> case referenced in its comment) both go to strict zero; any other `requireDropGlue` consumer carrying a live handle-typed field is audited for the same gap. |
| RV2-DEBT-063 | `run_ready_one` (`runtime/native/rt_async_poll.c`, the single-worker synchronous poll path reached via `rt_task_await`/`run_until_done` whenever `rt_worker_count() <= 1`) has its own inline duplicate `poll_outcome` switch (two copies in the same function) that, on `POLL_DONE_CANCELLED`, calls `mark_done()` directly — it never routes through `apply_poll_outcome()`, unlike every other poller in the codebase (`rt_worker_turn.c`'s three call sites, `poll_ready_child_inline` in `rt_async_task.c`). `apply_poll_outcome`'s `POLL_DONE_CANCELLED` case is where a cancelled task with its OWN scope still holding active children gets deferred (re-parked on `scope_key`, `task->cancel_pending = 1`) instead of completing immediately — structured concurrency's guarantee that a parent never finishes before its children do. `run_ready_one` skips that deferral entirely: in single-worker mode (`SURGE_SHARDS=1`/no extra threads, the synchronous `.await()` path), a task cancelled while its own scope has active children completes immediately regardless, which is not just a leak variant of RV2-DEBT-059/062 but a correctness gap — a parent can be freed/observed-done while its children are still running. Found 2026-07-20 during the RV2-DEBT-059 design consult (independently confirmed by direct code read plus a second AI trace that reached the identical conclusion without seeing the first). Not reproduced with a minimal failing test yet; the mechanism is confirmed by code inspection (`rt_async_poll.c`, `run_ready_one`'s two switches vs. `apply_poll_outcome`'s scope-deferral branch), not by an observed failure. Not fixed here — out of scope for RV2-DEBT-059/062, which only needed the abandoned-state-box and nested-handle drop paths respectively; this is a scheduling-correctness gap in a different function entirely. **CLOSED 2026-08-28 by the fix this row itself proposed.** `run_ready_one`'s two hand-copied outcome switches are gone; it routes through `apply_poll_outcome` like every other poller, and `rt_async_poll.c` drops from 391 to 363 physical lines (301 effective). The leak half is measured and gated: a task cancelled at its first suspension point never gave back the scope block its own poll had opened -- valgrind `in use at exit` over 190 extra cancelled rounds grew by 190 blocks and 12,160 bytes, exactly 1.00 block and 64.0 bytes per cancelled task, against a flat uncancelled twin. With the fix both round counts read the same figure, and the row runs inside `runtime-v2-heap-check`. The DEFERRAL half is fixed by construction rather than by a behavioural row, and that is stated rather than glossed: with one applier there is no second switch that can skip the re-park. A behavioural row was attempted and DELETED rather than adjusted -- cancelling a parent cancels its children, so a child of a cancelled parent never reaches an observable point, and the deferral is an ordering no program can witness. Writing a row that asserts something else in its place would have been worse than saying this. Two things this uncovered and did not fix, recorded so they are not read as part of the close: a second retention of 200 blocks at 368 bytes each under four workers, allocated by `spawn_internal_task_locked` via the checkpoint path, which is a different object and unattributed; and the fact that this row's deferral branch was UNREACHABLE while scopes were not counting their children, so any earlier test of it would have passed against a poller that never took it. | Closed | Closed 2026-08-28 | A minimal repro proves a parent-with-active-children completes before a child in single-worker mode today; `run_ready_one` routes `POLL_DONE_CANCELLED` (and ideally all outcomes, for symmetry with every other poller) through `apply_poll_outcome()`; the repro is fixed and gated. |
| RV2-DEBT-068 | A bignum payload delivered into a channel is never reclaimed: `typeOwnsHeap` returns false for a bignum `int`/`uint`/`float` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go` ~63-97 falls through to the `default:` case), so the `channel_on::<T>`/`make_channel::<T>` lowering computes drop-fn id 0 for such a T and RV2-DEBT-064-era buffer drain at `rt_channel_free` has nothing to dispatch. Measured 2026-07-23 while building `TestRuntimeV2FarSelectConstArmEvaluatedOnce`: a single `int` literal outside the fixnum inline range, sent through a far channel, leaves exactly `36 bytes in 1 block` definitely-lost, stack `rt_bigint_from_literal` <- `bi_alloc` (`rt_bignum_int.c`). That row pins the count at one deliberately — it gates the crossing block's const-operand evaluation, not this reclamation gap — so the gap is visible and bounded rather than hidden. Distinct from the resolved pervasive int/uint leak (small-int inline/fixnum work): this is specifically the drop-glue classification refusing to treat a heap bignum as heap-owning. SCOPE CORRECTED 2026-07-23, and it is much WIDER than the channel-payload framing above: the same 36 bytes leak from a PLAIN LOCAL with no channel, no crossing and no async involved — `@entrypoint fn main() -> int { let big: int = <literal beyond the fixnum range>; ... }` leaks 36 bytes in 1 block, stack `bi_alloc` (`rt_bignum_int.c`). So this is not a channel defect at all; heap-backed bignums are simply never dropped anywhere in the language. The reason is structural rather than an oversight: an `int` word is either an inline fixnum (odd tag) or a heap bignum pointer (even, non-NULL), so whether a value owns heap depends on the VALUE, not the type — which is exactly what `typeOwnsHeap` classifies. Making it true for `int`/`uint`/`float` turns EVERY integer in every program into a droppable, each drop needing a runtime fixnum-tag check before it may free. That is a language-wide ownership decision with real codegen and performance consequences, not a local fix, and it should be scoped and reviewed as its own vertical rather than folded into a channel row. Relatives: the resolved pervasive-leak work (fixnum inlining) deliberately made the common case allocation-free instead of solving drops, and RV2-DEBT-035 (refcounting) is the other candidate mechanism. DIRECTION CHOSEN 2026-07-23 by the project owner, after a survey of how other languages solve this: COPY-ON-WRITE VALUE TYPES, i.e. Swift's model — the heap block carries a reference count, `let b = a` keeps both usable with no move errors and no surface-language change, a borrowing read never clones (the arithmetic entry points already take `const void*`), drop obligations extend to these scalars, and the block frees at zero. This is the same direction RV2-DEBT-038 independently sketched for `float`. Two framing facts that decided it. FIRST, the survey: every language offering a seamless dynamic int has a GARBAGE COLLECTOR — Ruby, OCaml, Lisp and Smalltalk use the identical tagged fixnum/bignum representation Surge uses and simply GC the heap half; the languages without a GC all drop one leg instead, either making big numbers an explicit non-Copy type (Rust's `BigInt`, C++ with GMP+RAII) or fixing the width (Go, C, Java, Swift's trapping `Int`). The combination Surge wants — seamless dynamic int, value semantics, no GC — is implemented by nobody, so this is a choice of which leg to release, not a search for a trick. Swift's copy-on-write containers are the proven precedent for value semantics over a heap payload without a GC. SECOND, and this is what makes it viable here where it would not be elsewhere: the reference count can plausibly be NON-ATOMIC, which removes the apparent conflict with RUNTIME_V2.md's rule that request-path code must not touch shared refcounts. The argument is that a bignum reaches another shard only through an EXPLICIT crossing (`on`, `spawn on`, far channels), so every crossing can deep-copy and no block is ever shared across shards. That claim is LOAD-BEARING AND NOT YET VERIFIED — it is the first thing the pending adversarial review must break or confirm, since a single path that publishes a pointer without cloning forces an atomic counter and reopens the conflict. SEQUENCING NOTE: `float` is the worse and more urgent half (unbounded, ~2 blocks per operation, no inline form possible) while `int` leaks only retained large values, so float may be the smaller vertical that proves the mechanism. The alternative the owner rejected was ratifying the leak; rejected on the stated rule that a pre-existing defect surviving the refactor is worse than an incomplete V2 feature, and that engineering expense alone is not grounds to defer a real bug. | Open | Drop-glue type classification | `typeOwnsHeap` recognises heap bignums, a bignum channel payload reclaims exactly once, and the const-arm row's residual tightens from 36 bytes to zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-069 | Remote-select commit recording has a window the shutdown/cancel sweep can steal: `record_select_commit` (`runtime/native/rt_far_channel_select.c` ~290-308) only writes `select_committed_index` when the pending is still `PENDING`, but `rt_select_poll`'s commit has already released its own critical section by then. If `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending` (`rt_remote_task_wait.c`) resolves the pending inside that window, the index stays `NO_COMMIT`, RV2-DEBT-064's winner gate correctly does NOT fire (the status is no longer OK), and the release loop then drops the payload of the arm the owner ALREADY delivered into the channel — a double free reachable only through shutdown racing a committing select. Found by the RV2-DEBT-064 adversarial review; pre-existing relative to that fix and not introduced by it. REPRODUCED DETERMINISTICALLY 2026-07-23, and the obvious fix is REFUTED. Recipe (rebuildable in minutes; the row itself was not kept, since it would be permanently red): in the publication harness, give `select_exec_state` a `send_drop_fn_ids[2]` array and thread it into `rt_far_channel_select`; make `__surge_drop_result_call` count instead of `exit(97)` so arm drops are assertable; set up a one-arm SEND select on a capacity-1 far channel so the send commits immediately; arm `SP_FAR_SELECT_AFTER_COMMIT_BEFORE_REPLY:block`; from the driver, wait for the window, take an extra pending reference, call `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending(ex, DESTINATION_SHUTDOWN)` DIRECTLY (going through `rt_executor_request_shutdown` deadlocks — shutdown waits for workers to quiesce and the worker you need is the parked one), then open the point and await. Result: `arm_drops=1`, `select_committed_index=UINT64_MAX` (NO_COMMIT) — the committed arm's payload is dropped although the channel already owns it, exactly as this row predicted. WHY THE NAIVE FIX FAILS: removing the `status == PENDING` condition from `record_select_commit`'s match does NOT help. Instrumentation shows the function is entered (`RSC: entered`) but its candidate loop prints nothing — `state->pending_head` is already EMPTY, so the pending has been unlinked from the registry before the body resumes; a driver-held reference keeps the memory alive but not the listing. A registry scan therefore cannot find it at that point, whatever the status test says. FIX DIRECTION (identified, not implemented): the body must not re-scan. `rt_anchored_channel_select` already calls `rt_remote_task_select_binding_current` at body entry, BEFORE any sweep can land; extend that lookup to hand back the pending with an added reference, hold it across `rt_select_poll`, record the commit through the pointer directly, and release. Note the same registry scan with the same `status == PENDING` condition appears TWICE in that file (the binding lookup and the commit record), which is what makes a textual patch to one of them silently hit the wrong site. Second, smaller item from the same review, recorded here rather than as its own row: `rt_remote_task_pending_set_reply` (`rt_remote_task_pending.c` ~187-201) is not status-gated and writes `result_kind`/`result_bits` unconditionally — "exactly one reply edge" is documented but never asserted, so a second reply edge would flip the kind after the winner gate's peek. An assert or status guard there is cheap. **CLOSED 2026-09-03 (Wave E, E6).** The body holds a reference on its pending from its binding to the moment the winner's cell is marked moved and the commit is recorded, and it records the commit through that pointer, not a registry scan (`rt_remote_task_select_binding_take`, `record_select_commit(pending, winner)`); the pending itself is found through the body task's own registration pointer (`rt_task.remote_owner_pending`) rather than a scan keyed on a PENDING status the sweep changes or a listing the caller's consume removes. Row `commit-vs-shutdown-sweep-keeps-the-record` (`TestRuntimeV2RemoteSelectAbandonEdges`, forced `SP_FAR_SELECT_AFTER_COMMIT_BEFORE_REPLY`, `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending` run in the window): the index reads the winner, the send lands once, no arm payload drops, no pin leaks. Rule 13 `RV2_DEBT_069_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` (the scan back) reads NO_COMMIT. The second item: `rt_remote_task_pending_set_reply` writes only the first edge and refuses a second, whose caller gives back the capability it minted. | Closed | Remote-select commit/teardown race | The commit index survives a teardown that lands in the window (or the window is closed), proven with a forced sync point; the reply edge asserts its single-write contract. |
| RV2-DEBT-072 | A boxed composite NESTED inside another never frees its inner box. `emitFieldDropAt` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go`) skips a field whose `typeOwnsHeap` is false, which is exactly the shape of a struct/tuple whose own fields hold no heap — so `type Nested = { inner: Pair, label: int }` leaks one 16-byte block per construction even though the OUTER box is now reclaimed. Discovered 2026-07-24 while closing the top-level case; the outer half is fixed and gated by `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed`, which pins struct and tuple at strict zero and deliberately omits the nested shape. Closing this means making `typeOwnsHeap` true for boxed composites generally, which switches drop glue on for nearly every type at once and must be sequenced AFTER RV2-DEBT-073 (the Copy aliasing question), since a `@copy` composite would otherwise be dropped through two aliases. CLOSED 2026-07-27 by Epic 23 step 6. The fix is not in the field walk at all: `typeOwnsHeap` now answers true for a VALUE COMPOSITE before it reaches that walk, so a nested composite is reclaimed because it owns its box, not because a field of it happens to own heap. The narrower structural question the walk asks — "does a field own heap" — was the wrong one for this shape and stays only for deciding which FIELDS a drop must free. Measured: 16 nested constructions in a loop report `All heap blocks were freed -- no leaks are possible`. | Open | Drop glue field walk | Closed (a value composite owns its box) | A nested composite's inner box is freed; a nested-shape row in `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed` passes at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-073 | A `@copy` composite leaks its box, once per construction, and CANNOT simply be made droppable. Measured 2026-07-24: `@copy type Q = { a: int, b: int }` loses 256 bytes in 16 blocks over 16 constructions; with `float` fields it also loses the fields (1,920 bytes indirect). Reproduced unchanged at `9b849310`, i.e. this predates Epic 22 entirely and is not a refcounting artifact. The obstruction is representation, not classification: a boxed composite is duplicated by copying the box POINTER, so `let q = p` gives two bindings one box, and marking the type droppable would double-free. Sema's `ownsHeap` reports false for it (Copy wins), so no drop is even emitted today. Two ways out, both real decisions: unbox small Copy composites into registers so a copy is a value copy, or clone the box on every Copy-composite copy (the Swift path, and the same shape the arbitrary-precision scalars solve by counting). Blocks RV2-DEBT-072. CLOSED 2026-07-27 by Epic 23 Phase 1 (steps 1-6), which took the second way out the row names — clone the box on every Copy-composite copy — behind a permanent boundary rather than as a point fix. `OperandCopyValue` carries the consuming-copy intent that `OperandCopy` could not; both backends implement it (a fresh object on the VM, generated `@clone.typeN` glue on the native side, alloc + memcpy + fix up only the members whose bits are not the value); `ownsHeap` then flipped so the box is reclaimed. Neither half works alone, which is why they landed together: droppable-while-aliasing double-frees, independent-while-undroppable leaks. Measured: build/copy/drop and nested shapes report strict zero definitely-lost under valgrind, and copy independence holds on both backends from one source. The blocked row RV2-DEBT-072 closed with it. Phase 2 replaces the boxed clone with inline storage behind the same boundary; the contract tests do not change. | Open | Copy composite representation | Closed (type-directed copy + owned box) | A `@copy` struct is reclaimed exactly once per value with no double free; a `@copy` row in `TestRuntimeV2CompositeBoxIsReclaimed` passes at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-074 | `compare` on a DEREFERENCED BORROW frees the referent, so the real owner's later drop is a double free — on the native backend, a use-after-free and usually a SIGSEGV. `compareScrutineeReleaseSafe` (`internal/hir/normalize_compare_release.go`) already refuses to release a borrowed scrutinee, but it tested only the scrutinee's TYPE: `*r` on an `&FmtArg` has type `FmtArg`, because the deref already stripped the reference, so the guard saw a bare union and concluded the compare owned the box. FILED 2026-07-27 WITH THE WRONG ATTRIBUTION and corrected the same day; the original row blamed "writing through a `&mut` composite parameter, then reading through it, in a function that returns a value", with four negative controls that appeared to isolate that conjunction. THE NEGATIVE CONTROLS WERE NOT CLEAN — they were silently corrupt: valgrind shows the identical Invalid read + Invalid free in the "passing" controls, which merely failed to segfault because the freed block happened to read back as a benign discriminant. Method note worth keeping: a crash/no-crash bisection over a heap-corruption bug attributes to whatever perturbs the allocator, not to the cause; the controls must be run under valgrind, not judged by exit code. The real blast radius is far larger than the original row implied — `core/format.sg`'s `append_fmt_arg` takes `arg: &FmtArg` and does `compare *arg`, so EVERY formatted print with a non-string argument was a double free, down to a five-line `let y = 100; print(f"{y}")` with no composites anywhere. CLOSED 2026-07-27. Fix: the guard now also inspects the scrutinee EXPRESSION (`scrutineeReadsThroughBorrow`), refusing release for a deref (and through an `own` wrapper), matching the file's stated errs-toward-false policy. Measured: the minimal int repro and the `&mut`-composite repro both go from SIGSEGV to correct output matching the VM, valgrind Invalid-read/Invalid-free counts drop to zero, and showcase `21_bigint_stress` goes from SIGSEGV to byte-identical with the VM. The 19-20 byte `rt_string_from_bigint` leak visible in these programs is PRE-EXISTING and unchanged (measured identical before and after). Gate: a `borrowed` probe in `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` compares through `&Outcome` at n=1 and n=2000 and pins the alloc/free differential; negative control confirmed (reverting the guard makes it fail with a crash). Full `./internal/...` failure set is unchanged from baseline (33 vs 32 rows, the single difference being `TestMTStructuredConcurrency`, which flaked in the baseline run and passes 5/5 on re-run). WHY THE VM NEVER SAW IT: the VM's heap is reference-counted, so the extra release only decremented a count — which is exactly why the VM/LLVM differential could not catch this class and a census had to. | Open | Compare lowering (HIR normalize) | Closed (guard tests the scrutinee expression, not only its type) | A compare through a borrowed scrutinee frees nothing; the census probe balances at n=1 and n=2000; valgrind reports no invalid read or free for formatted printing. |
| RV2-DEBT-075 | A compare arm binding a REFCOUNTED-SCALAR payload out of a BORROWED union releases one reference too many, corrupting the heap. Split off from RV2-DEBT-074 on 2026-07-27 after that fix unblocked the path far enough to expose it. Repro is four lines: `let y: float = 1.5; print(f"{y}");` — no crash, correct output, and valgrind reports an Invalid read; showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` takes the same path and dies with `malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detected` after printing nine correct lines. MECHANISM, read off the MIR of `append_fmt_arg`'s `FmtFloat` arm: `L23 = tag_payload copy L3.FmtFloat[0]` extracts a BARE ALIAS (no retain — `lowerTagPayloadExpr` in `internal/mir/lower_expr_misc.go` is the one extraction site that does NOT call `retainExtractedValue`, unlike `lowerFieldAccessExpr` and `lowerIndexExpr`), then `L22 = retain L23` gives the binding its own reference (+1), then `drop L23` releases the temp (-1), then `drop L22` releases the binding (-1) — net -1 against a union whose real owner still holds it. THE OBVIOUS FIX IS WRONG AND WAS TRIED AND REVERTED: adding `retainExtractedValue` to `lowerTagPayloadExpr` balances the borrowed case and breaks the OWNED case, where `drop L23` is not a bug but the ownership TRANSFER the envelope-release model depends on (the payload's reference moves to the binding and the envelope release deliberately does not touch it). With the unconditional retain, `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` regresses from strict zero to 72 bytes in 3 blocks. So the retain must be CONDITIONAL on whether the scrutinee transfers ownership — the same predicate `compareScrutineeReleaseSafe` computes in HIR — and MIR's `lowerTagPayloadExpr` cannot see that today: both cases lower to an indistinguishable `tag_payload copy L3`. That is the design question this row carries, and it is the same shape as Epic 23's central finding (an extraction that aliases before the ownership decision is made), so the two should be solved with one mechanism rather than two. CLOSED 2026-07-27. Fix: the ownership answer is CARRIED DOWN from the compare instead of guessed at the extraction. `TagPayloadData` gains `SubjectBorrowed`, set from `!releaseSafe` where the tag arm is built and propagated through `lowerTagPayloadPattern` so a nested pattern inherits it (a payload read out of a borrowed union is itself borrowed, however deep it nests); `lowerTagPayloadExpr` retains only when that flag is set. Every site that copies `TagPayloadData` (mono clone/subst/traverse/dce, hir normalize, buildpipeline, driver) mutates a struct copy and reassigns, so the new field rides along with no change. Measured: `print(f"{1.5}")` valgrind-clean (Invalid count 0); showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` goes from `malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detected` to byte-identical with the VM; all 30 showcases now build, run and exit clean on the native backend, versus 2 crashing before RV2-DEBT-074. Gate: two probes in `TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced` that must BOTH stay balanced, `borrowed-float` (compare through `&Measure`) and `owned-float` (compare on an owned `Reading(...)`), at n=1 and n=2000. NEGATIVE CONTROLS BOTH WAYS, since a one-directional control would have passed the over-broad fix: removing the retain entirely crashes the borrowed probe; making it unconditional fails the owned-float probe AND regresses `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` to 72 bytes in 3 blocks. Full `./internal/...` failure set is baseline minus one known flake. **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-11 at `5ebb0cec`: this row is LIVE, and its SYMPTOM HAS CHANGED.** It is recorded above as "no crash, correct output, and valgrind reports an Invalid read". Today the same four lines — `let y: float = 1.5; print(f"{y}");` — make the VM print `panic VM3301: use-after-free: local "x" used after drop` at `core/format.sg:61:12` and exit 1, while the NATIVE backend prints `1.5E+0` and exits 0. So it is a detected fault now rather than a silent one, and it is a backend disagreement, which it was not when filed. Pre-existing rather than caused by anything in this session: a compiler built from `5ebb0cec` before today's changes gives the identical panic. The payload type is the discriminator exactly as the row says — `let y: int = 3; print(f"{y}")` prints `3` on both. **Read the Status column, not the prose:** the text ends "CLOSED 2026-07-27" and a close condition is worded as though met, while Status says Open. Open is right, because the repro fails; whether the carried-down ownership answer never covered this path or something after it regressed has NOT been established, and that is the first thing to settle. **Why no gate sees it:** `testdata/golden/vm_strings/fstring_basic.sg` interpolates one string and two ints, and no fixture in that directory mentions `float` at all, so the behavioural corpus is green on both backends while ordinary formatting of a float is broken. A float-interpolating fixture lands with the fix. **MECHANISM NARROWED 2026-08-11 by reading the MIR, and it is NOT the missing retain this row was filed on — that fix is present and correct.** `lowerTagPayloadExpr` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_misc.go:31`) does retain, and only when `SubjectBorrowed`, exactly as the closure paragraph describes. What the MIR of `append_fmt_arg` shows is a DOUBLE DROP of the arm's payload binding: `bb5` reads `L23 = tag_payload copy L3.FmtFloat[0]`, retains twice (`L23 = retain L23`, `L22 = retain L23`), drops the temp, does the work, and ends with `drop L22` — and then the compare's MERGE block `bb6` immediately does `L2 = move L24; drop L22` again. Two drops of one binding, which is the net -1 this row predicted, arriving from a place it did not: the arm's scope exit and the merge both claim it. The other arms do not show it because they do not have the obligation at all — `registerComparePayloadDroppables` skips a payload of a BORROWED union unless it takes its own reference, and only a reference-counted scalar does, so `float` is the one payload type that gets an obligation here and the one that gets it twice. That also explains why the symptom changed: the double drop was always there, and the VM's use-after-free detector is what now names it instead of letting it corrupt quietly. NEXT STEP is therefore narrow — find which of the two channels registers the merge-block drop and stop it claiming a binding the arm already released; do not touch the retain. The reference arithmetic settles which of the three drops is the wrong one, so nobody has to guess: the union holds 1; `retain` on the temp makes 2; the binding's own `L22 = retain L23` makes 3; `drop L23` returns to 2; the arm's `drop L22` returns to 1, which is exactly the union's own reference and the correct resting state; and the merge block's second `drop L22` takes it to 0 and frees a value the union still points at. So the arm's drop is right and the merge's is the surplus. `wrapArmDrops` (`internal/hir/lower_expr.go:54`) is the channel that emits the arm's — it wraps the result in `{ ret <value> after dropping ... }` from `ArmDropsExpr`, and sema hands that list over exactly once. The surplus therefore comes from whatever ALSO treats the pattern binding as a droppable of the wrapping block's scope, which is where to look first. (Surfaced 2026-08-11 by the adversarial verification of RV2-DEBT-191, which reported it as a regression of a CLOSED row; that reading was wrong — 075 was SPLIT OFF on 2026-07-27, not closed — but the defect is real. This row also has SIX table columns where the table has five; left as found rather than reshaped, since which of the two close-condition texts is intended is not obvious.) | Open | Compare arm payload extraction (HIR/MIR boundary) | Closed (the compare's ownership answer is carried to the extraction) | A float payload bound out of a borrowed union balances its references: `print(f"{1.5}")` is valgrind-clean, showcase `23_roundtrip_parse_format` matches the VM, and `TestRuntimeV2FloatReclamationValgrindZero` stays at strict zero. |
| RV2-DEBT-076 | Every crossing route REFUSES a `@copy` value composite instead of carrying it, as the deliberate safe state Epic 23 step 3 installed on 2026-07-27. A struct, tuple, union or fixed array is a value by the language's own definition but is still stored in a heap box, so shipping its word would hand two shards the same box — cross-shard aliasing today, and a cross-shard double free the moment step 6 makes the box droppable. Rather than ship that, `TriviallyTransportableBits` now excludes the combination (`Result.IsCopyValueComposite`: composite AND duplicable — a move-only composite still ships, because it transfers the box to exactly one shard, and a Copy scalar still ships, because its word is the value), and each route reports the representation as the cause rather than the misleading "not plain-copy data": `on`/`spawn on` and `blocking` captures at sema (SEM3168), and the crossing result, far-task-await result and remote channel element at the transport guard (FUT7020), with remote-select SEND arms inheriting the channel's element decision. THIS IS A DELIBERATE SOURCE BREAK: programs that compile today stop compiling. That is sanctioned for the 0.1.X -> 0.2.X refactor and is the honest trade — those programs are already silently sharing one box between two shards. The fix is Epic 23 step 7: install a deep copy at each boundary, which the type-directed clone of steps 4-5 makes available; Phase 2's inline representation then removes the need for the boundary clone entirely, since a composite crosses as inline bits plus its refcounted-scalar fields. Gated by three negative fixtures (`spawn_on_negative_copy_composite_capture`, `_spawn_on_negative_copy_composite_result`, `_channel_negative_copy_composite_element`) and one POSITIVE neighbour (`channel_positive_move_only_composite_element`) proving the refusal is narrow; negative control confirms all three go red when the predicate is disabled. NOTE a regression found and fixed inside this step: the first cut also refused `Placement`, which is declared as a struct but is `@intrinsic` and represented at runtime as a tagged word with no box, and whose entire purpose is to cross. Fixed in `Interner.IsValueComposite` rather than by special-casing the crossing sites, because the predicate was wrong about the type, not the sites. CLOSED 2026-07-28 by Epic 23 step 7. Every route carries a `@copy` value composite again, and the routes did NOT need the same treatment — reading the transport showed three shapes where the row assumed one. A CAPTURE is duplicated at its operand (`lower_expr_crossing.go`, now a consuming read for every capture mode, which also settles a disagreement: blocking captures always read consumingly and crossings did not), so the destination's state holds a box of its own. A RESULT is a TRANSFER — the body builds the box, the caller receives it, there is never a second holder — so it needed permission and no copy at all; adding one would have allocated a box per reply. A channel ELEMENT is duplicated at the send. The transport axis stopped excluding composites, and the sema capture refusals were removed. NOTE what step 7 had to add beyond lifting refusals: an unpacked COPY capture is now reclaimed at the crossing body's returns (`rewriteSpawnOnPollReturns`). The shallow envelope release rests on the stated assumption that "the captures were unpacked into locals, so only the box is dead", which was true while a capture was an alias and stopped being true once the state held a copy. That drop is restricted to COPY captures on purpose: an OWNED capture may be consumed by the body, move tracking is sema's and this synthetic local was never sema's to track, so dropping it unconditionally double-freed what the body handed on — measured as `free(): double free detected in tcache 2` across the migration suite before the restriction. Measured after: composite captured across `spawn on` is independent of the sender (body sees the pre-mutation value, sender keeps its own), strict-zero definitely-lost under valgrind, no memcheck error. Negative controls both directions: reverting the capture to a borrowing read fails independence, removing the capture drop leaks 32 bytes in 2 blocks. Full `./internal/...` diffed against pristine HEAD adds exactly one failure for the whole epic, the ownership-axis invariant test, updated to pin the new shape. STILL REFUSED, and out of this epic's scope: a composite carrying an arbitrary-precision scalar, because the crossing copy RETAINS such a field rather than deep-copying it and the count is not atomic — that refusal is Epic 22's. | Open | Crossing transport | Closed (each route gives the far side an owner) | Each route carries a `@copy` value composite again, by deep-copying at the boundary (step 7) or by inline representation (Phase 2); the three negative fixtures become positives and the positive neighbour still passes. |
| RV2-DEBT-080 | A `blocking { }` body abandons a constant 219 bytes, INDEPENDENT of iteration count — 1 block at n=1 and 1 block at n=8, so it is not a per-execution leak and NOT the same mechanism as the crossing `ret` defect that Epic 24 step 0 fixed. Measured 2026-07-28 on the native backend with a `@shard_movable` struct carrying a 200-character string moved into a `blocking` body that reads it and returns an int. Blocking bodies still have all drop-obligation recording suppressed in `dropObligationsSuppressed` (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go`); crossings had the same suppression lifted when RV2-DEBT-034 was found to have closed, but blocking was deliberately left alone because its release path is a separate shallow free on the pool side (`runtime/native/rt_async_blocking.c`) and was not probed. So the suppression there is a PARKED question, not a proven boundary: it may be hiding the same class of defect the crossing side had, and the constant-size loss above is a hint that something else is also wrong. Unprobed: whether the loss scales with the number of distinct blocking sites rather than executions, and whether it is a pool-shutdown artifact. **ATTRIBUTED AND REWORKED 2026-08-26 (D6-tail a-1..a-4).** The 219 bytes were the reproducer's capture, abandoned by a release that could only free the state BLOCK (`rt_free` over a pointer and two integers); it was constant because that body CONSUMED the capture, and a body that only READS one loses it once per execution. a-1 (`dcdcb2da`): unpacking a capture declares the transfer (`MoveOut`). a-2 (`0a3fa567`): the job's state is one `rt_value_cell` adopted from the block the compiler reserved, claimed by the worker immediately before the body runs and destroyed through its own descriptor at the last release -- initialized: walked then freed; spent: freed only -- with two negative-control toggles (`RV2_DEBT_080_NEGATIVE_CONTROL`, `RV2_DEBT_080_WALK_ALWAYS_NEGATIVE_CONTROL`). a-3: `dropObligationsSuppressed` is deleted; `typeExprBlocking` pushes a function root, registers the transferring captures as the body's droppables before the walk (`registerBlockingBodyOwnership`) and marks the caller's bindings moved after it, exactly as `typeExprAsync` does; pinned by `TestBlockingBodyRecordsItsCaptureAndLocalAtReturn` (red before: `early-exit obligations: []`) and its three must-not rows, and by four valgrind rows at eight executions each (`TestRuntimeV2BlockingBodyLocalIsReclaimed`, `...ReadCaptureIsReclaimed`, `...ConsumedCaptureIsReleasedOnce`, `...CopyCompositeCaptureIsReclaimed`) in `runtime-v2-heap-check`, `runtime-v2-carrier-sanitizer-check` and `requiredSanitizerCoverage`. a-4: three sync points in `rt_async_blocking.c` (`SP_BLOCKING_POP_BEFORE_STATUS`, `SP_BLOCKING_STATE_BEFORE_BODY`, `SP_BLOCKING_SHUTDOWN_BEFORE_DRAIN`) and the section-7 rows in the lifecycle harness with a counted state descriptor (`TestRuntimeV2LifecycleDebt080*` in `runtime-v2-lifecycle-check`): cancel before claim (walked once) with the shallow-free negative control reading zero drops, cancel after claim (freed only) with the walk-always negative control reading `blocking captures destroyed twice`, poll-cancelled while queued, shutdown draining a queued job by CANCELLATION -- the owner's ruling: a job still queued when the pool's shutdown is published is settled CANCELLED by the worker that pops it and never run (storage model section 13, "shutdown drains or transfers every ... blocking job"); before a-4 the worker ran it -- and callback reentry (the last release performed under the control lock is refused by `rt_value_refuse_if_locked`). Stale generation has no row on purpose: the job is reference counted, one reference each for the awaiting task and the pool, and is freed only when both have released, so no late event can meet reused storage under its address. Residue, not this row's: a submission that lands after the last pool worker has exited (shutdown observed with an empty queue) is neither run nor cancelled -- the worker that would cancel it is gone -- and the task-side reference of a parked awaiter at executor shutdown is released by nobody, which is true of every parked task kind at shutdown today. | Open | Blocking body drop obligations (sema suppression + pool-side release); closes on the lead's green run of the a-3 valgrind rows and the a-4 lifecycle rows named above | The 219-byte loss is attributed to a named site; blocking bodies either record drop obligations like crossing bodies or the suppression is justified by a stated ownership contract that a test asserts; a blocking body that builds and holds a droppable at its exit is proven leak-free by allocation census plus valgrind. |
| RV2-DEBT-082 | A FAR-HANDLE capture crossing an `on` boundary has no stated owner, and the two layers disagree. MIR lowers every capture as a consuming read into the shipped state (`internal/mir/lower_expr_crossing.go`), but sema leaves the caller's binding live: Epic 24 step 0b marked `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` captures moved and deliberately left `CrossingCaptureMoveFarHandle` alone, because the ANCHORED form uses the destination handle INSIDE the body it was captured for (`on ch { ch.send(1); ... }`), so consuming it would reject every anchored channel operation — pinned by the `anchored_far_channel_still_usable` row in `internal/sema/crossing_capture_move_test.go`. That exclusion is a workaround for symbol-granular move tracking, not an ownership model. As it stands the handle is either TWO owners (if the transport is a move, which the lowering says it is) or a hidden lease (if the transport shares, which nothing states). Raised by adversarial review 2026-07-28: the comment claiming the lease travels with the handle is an assertion, not an implementation. Candidate resolutions: a distinct anchor/lease capture mode that is explicitly not a move; or marking the caller's binding moved AFTER body analysis while the body uses a separate anchor capability. Note the second becomes cheaper once Epic 24 makes move tracking place-granular. No leak or invalid access has been measured on this path — `share()` leases (Epic 16) and the affine handle lifecycle appear to cover it in practice — but nothing pins that, and no probe was written. **CLOSED 2026-09-03 by Wave E step E3, with the first candidate: the anchor is a LEASE, spelled by its own capture mode.** What the probe found first: `let held = ch;` inside `on ch { ch.send(1); ... }` compiled without a word on `1fdd6383`, and under valgrind produced no invalid access -- the body's binding consumed the CALLER's handle (the shipped copy was the caller's own box pointer, the body's drop released it, sema's body-side move marked the caller's symbol moved), so the "two owners" were really one owner chosen by whichever side moved first; incoherent rather than corrupting. Now: (1) the anchor of an `on far_handle` block crosses as `CrossingCaptureAnchorLease` (verdict `CrossingCaptureAnchorLeased`): `checkOnCaptures` observes no move, `registerCrossingBodyOwnership` registers nothing for it, MIR lowers it as a borrowing read, and the body's copy of the token is a lease view it never dereferences and never drops -- the body reaches the channel only through the owner-side pin `rt_far_channel_pin` takes at dispatch and gives back at the reply edge; (2) `checkAnchorLeaseUses` refuses any use of the anchor inside the block other than as the receiver of its channel operation (`SEM3210`, `SemaOnAnchorLeaseMisuse`; fixture `on_negative_anchor_lease_misuse.sg`; `TestAnchorLeaseMisuseIsRejected`); (3) a far handle captured into a NON-anchored crossing is a real move: `observeMove` at the crossing (a later use is `SEM3130`, pinned by the wildcard-body row `far_handle_read_after_capture`, which a rule relying on the body's own use accepts), and the body owns the lease (`registerCrossingBodyOwnership` registers far captures). Runtime: the pending records `anchor_pinned`, every unpin path goes through `rt_immediate_on_anchor_unpin` (once per pin), and `rt_far_channel_unpin` refuses a non-channel token and an entry with no pin left (it used to subtract from an unsigned counter and park the entry at UINT32_MAX in flight). Controls, both valgrind strict zero at shards 1 and 2: `TestRuntimeV2FarHandleHasOneOwnerAcrossACrossing/anchored` (create, share, blocks on both handles, scope exit) and `/move_in` (a handle moved into a `spawn on` body that drops it). The anchored row `anchored_far_channel_still_usable` stays green and `anchor_still_usable_after_the_block` is new. | Closed 2026-09-03 (E3: the anchor is a lease in its own capture mode; SEM3210 for its misuse) | Epic 23b Wave E (E3) | A far-handle capture has ONE stated owner, spelled in the capture mode rather than in a comment; the anchored form stays legal; and a far handle captured into a crossing is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind, with an anchored-op program and a plain move-in program as the two controls. |
| RV2-DEBT-092 | The mono dump lists every core generic instance TWICE: `--emit-mono` on `testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.sg` prints 8 type rows for 4 distinct types (`Task::<bool>`, `Task::<int>`, `TaskResult::<bool>`, `TaskResult::<int>`), each pair carrying the SAME resulting `type#`. Found 2026-07-30 while root-causing the RV2-DEBT-070 remainder, and the two share a mechanism: one copy is keyed on core's own declaration symbol (`OrigSym` 63/64, low and stable) and the other on the SYNTHETIC per-file symbol that `injectCoreExports` mints for the same export (`internal/symbols/resolve_module_members.go`, `syntheticSymbolForExport`). Monomorphization keys instances by symbol, so one generic reached through two symbols instantiates twice. Both copies agree on the TypeID, so this is duplicated WORK and a confusing dump rather than a known miscompile — but it doubles the mono type table for every core generic, and any pass that counts or walks instances sees each one twice. NOT a determinism defect: the ordering was fixed with RV2-DEBT-070 and the duplicate rows are now stable. Deliberately NOT fixed there, because deduplicating means changing how mono keys instances, which reaches codegen and deserves its own measurement. | Open | Mono instance keying | One generic reached through both its declaration symbol and a synthetic import symbol produces ONE mono instance, shown by the dump listing 4 rows for the 4 distinct types. |
| RV2-DEBT-094 | The native e2e corpus does not gate a commit, in TWO independent ways. First, `make test` runs `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1 go test ./...`, and every LLVM e2e test calls `ensureLLVMToolchain` -> `skipTimeoutTests`, which skips on any non-zero value of that variable — so `internal/vm` reports `ok` in `make check` having run none of its native suite, including every reclamation census. Second, even run directly the package exceeds `go test`'s default 10-minute timeout (measured 619s to completion), so a plain `go test ./internal/vm/` reports a TRUNCATED failure set that looks like a regression and is not; `-timeout 40m` is required. Not theoretical: RV2-DEBT-093 shipped a double free with `make check` green, and the two rows that would have caught it had been red on the tree since. A third trap sits next to these: the e2e tests exec `./surge`, so rebuilding the binary while the suite runs produces spurious failures indistinguishable from real ones. ALSO measured: the `TestLLVMParity` subtest set that fails VARIES run to run (13 subtests on one tree, 22 on the same tree with a change stashed, the smaller a strict subset of the larger), so the parity rows are themselves flaky under parallel load and cannot be read as a signal without a same-session baseline. Consequence: `make check` is a gate for compile-time behavior and says nothing about memory safety, while reading as if it covers both. Deliberately NOT fixed on discovery — the suite takes minutes and making it unconditional is a CI-shape decision, not a code fix. The owner has flagged leak-hunting tooling as a thing to design properly later rather than now, and this row is the concrete instance to design against. | Open | Runtime gate coverage | A memory-safety regression in `compare`, drop or crossing lowering cannot reach a commit with a green gate — either the native e2e corpus runs in the commit gate, or a fast proxy for it does. |
| RV2-DEBT-102 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing, found by Epic 25's full-corpus ownership verifier: a heap-backed optional copied from a borrowed recursive field can remain an alias while a loop carries the binding into a later consuming `compare`. The original `recursive_handles.sg` spelling used `let mut id: NodeId? = start`, then `id = *n.next` and consumed `id` on the next iteration; MIR gave the loop-carried binding an aliasing definition and a later release, so the verifier correctly refused to certify it. Epic 25 did not change the compiler for this non-blocking general case: the sema-only fixture was equivalently rewritten to consume `start` once, carry the scalar `NodeId`, and borrow `*n.next` only as the current compare subject. That removes the unsafe dependency without hiding a corpus finding. | Open | Post-Epic-25 loop ownership / drop-lowering follow-up | A minimal executable loop proves the current alias/release failure on both backends; borrowed heap-backed projections are cloned/retained or rejected before entering a loop-carried consuming binding; the original optional-carry shape is verifier-clean and memcheck-clean without a fixture rewrite. |
