| RV2-DEBT-151 | NONBLOCKING interval measure taken deliberately during Wave C. Ordinary composites move to inline exact-layout storage in Wave C, but the transport boundaries that carry them — channel send/receive, task result publication, and select staging — are Wave D and Wave E work and are not ready to receive an inline value. For the length of that interval a value crossing a transport boundary is copied into a fresh transport allocation on send and adopted, copied out, or freed on receive, through exactly ONE named helper set per backend. The set has THREE operations, not two: copy in at send, adopt or copy out at receive, and RELEASE an unconsumed payload. The third is not an implementation detail of the other two — after the cutover the generated drop glue drops a composite's contents and frees nothing, because there is no longer a box to free, so without a dedicated release entry point every refused, cancelled, or never-received payload leaks its transport allocation. This is not a second representation for ordinary code: ordinary code has one representation throughout, and the re-boxing is confined to the boundary. Ratified as a plan amendment during Wave C after both backend lanes independently reached the same blocker. **INVENTORY 2026-08-28 — the three-operation set, helper by helper, against the row's own close condition.** The condition says "both sets are deleted rather than deprecated", and that is NOT what happened: the NATIVE set is effectively gone, the VM set is INTACT. The row therefore NARROWS to the VM half instead of closing. **NATIVE (`internal/backend/llvm`).** COPY IN — `emitValueToI64`: DELETED from production, zero definitions and zero call sites under `internal/backend/llvm`; D2's map flip was its last caller. Every surviving spelling is inert: the carrier scanner's own token list (`internal/carriergate/scan_go.go:59`), that scanner's unit fixture (`internal/carriergate/scan_test.go:13,37`), the frozen census (`internal/carriergate/testdata/legacy_carriers.json`), and two comments recording what replaced it (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_task_result.go:14`, `internal/backend/llvm/emit_channel_storage.go:14`) — a task is now created with a result descriptor and publishes into storage it owns, and a channel takes and gives ADDRESSES at the element's own stride. RELEASE — `requireRuntimeOwnedRelease`: GONE from production, zero hits under `internal/backend`, `internal/vm` and `internal/mir`; the only surviving spelling is inside a RETIRED migration record whose token is something else (`internal/carriergate/testdata/legacy_carriers.json:3822`, token `crossingDropStates`, evidence line quoting the old call). For suspension frames — the second job this row warned the deletion must not absorb — it is replaced by `requireSuspensionFrameRelease` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go:191`, one production caller `internal/backend/llvm/emit_async.go:539`); that obligation belongs to RV2-DEBT-179 and §11, not here. ADOPT — `emitI64ToValue`: SURVIVES, and is outside this row's predicate. Definition `internal/backend/llvm/emit_async_helpers.go:91`, exactly two production callers, `internal/backend/llvm/emit_async.go:444` and `internal/backend/llvm/emit_crossing_select.go:261`. Both write the select's winner-index local: `Select.Dst` and the ChannelSelect crossing's `Dst` are the SAME local `selIndexLocal`, typed `Int32` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_select.go:34,37,44,74`), and `mir.SelectArm` carries no destination of its own. The local caller converts `rt_select_poll`'s `i64` return directly; the crossing caller loads the reply's `out_bits`, which the runtime states IS the winner index and not a payload (`runtime/native/rt_far_channel.h:29` "the reply names the winner index"; `runtime/native/rt_far_channel_select.c:13,463,500,521`, `rt_anchored_channel_select` "returns the winner index"). THE CHECK A REVIEWER CAN RUN: the transport branch inside `emitI64ToValue` is guarded by `hasInlineStorage` (`emit_async_helpers.go:99`), which is `mir.LivesInInlineStorage` = `IsValueComposite` (`internal/mir/suspension_frame.go:104`); `Int32` is not a value composite, so from both callers that branch is DEAD and what is emitted is `trunc i64 ... to i32`. **The sentence to disagree with: a winner index is a control answer the runtime computes and returns by value — never copied into a transport allocation, never released, never dropped — so the surviving spelling is not the boxing this row was opened about, even though the code that does the boxing still sits inside the same function.** The counter-position, stated so it can be taken: the file is still there and `emitAdoptFromTransportAllocation` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_transport_allocation.go:45`) still has a live caller in the ORDINARY runtime-call result marshaller (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_call_site.go:120`), so a reader taking "both sets deleted" literally is right that it is unmet on the native lane too; that caller is the runtime-call ABI for a composite result rather than a channel/task/select handover, which `emit_transport_allocation.go:30-36` already says in the tree. THE FAR AND CROSSING SITES this row scoped are out of the helper set entirely: `emit_async_far_task.go` NO LONGER EXISTS, and `emit_crossing_far_task.go`, `emit_crossing_anchored_on.go`, `emit_channel.go` and `emit_crossing_channel_create.go` contain zero references to any of the three helpers; `emit_crossing_select.go` has exactly the one winner-index adopt above. Gate-backed: the live `llvm-erased-word-bridge` census is 3 — one definition and two calls — against 28 frozen. **VM (`internal/vm`) — THE SET IS INTACT, AND THIS IS WHY THE ROW DOES NOT CLOSE.** `internal/vm/transport_storage.go` still declares all three operations under this row's own names and this row's own reasoning, and its header calls itself "a Wave C INTERVAL MEASURE ... When that lands, this file goes away": `transportCopyIn` (`:52`), `transportCopyOut` (`:74`), `transportRelease` (`:95`), with `transportExtent` (`:105`) reserving the arena. Twelve production call sites, at exactly the three boundaries this row named — copy in at channel send (`internal/vm/vm_dispatch_async_channels.go:70`) and at task-result publication (`internal/vm/vm_terminator.go:151`); copy out at channel receive (`internal/vm/vm_dispatch_async_channels.go:172,214`); release for a payload nobody received (`internal/vm/vm_dispatch_async_channels.go:29,48,84,88,120`, `internal/vm/resource.go:196`, `internal/vm/drop.go:136,147`). What these carry is the PAYLOAD, not an index. The carrier gate cannot see any of them — `internal/carriergate/scan_go.go:75-81` names four VM tokens and none of these — so a census reading is not evidence about this half, which is how a native-only survey concluded the row was done. **WHY THIS ROW IS NOT A MARKER FOR FAR TRANSPORT.** Far transport stays Wave E's subject for reasons that are its own and never depended on this row: (a) 35 live carrier rows in the far/remote runtime, measured 2026-08-28 with `carriergate.Scan` over the tree — `rt_far_channel.c` 7, `rt_far_channel.h` 4, `rt_far_channel_select.c` 2, `rt_remote_task_dispatch.c` 6, `rt_remote_task_internal.h` 7, `rt_remote_task_pending.c` 8, `rt_remote_task_wait.c` 1; by category `native-payload-bits` 19, `native-word-carrier` 9, `numeric-drop-dispatch` 7 — out of 83 live overall; and (b) the byte-credit work, which has never been built: `23b-inline-storage-and-typed-carriers.md:286-297` Wave E items 3, 5 and 6 (charge header, padding, inline payload, sidecars and transport-owned cross-clone storage before ownership publication; normal and exclusive-jumbo reservations; prove saturation parks producers without busy retry and every credit returns after adopt/drop), with RV2-DEBT-031 still Open for the credit protocol itself. Narrowing or closing this row says nothing about either, and neither may be tracked here. | Closed 2026-09-01 (VM interval helper deleted; D8 complete) | Epic 23b Wave D (containers and local carriers) | Each owner that Wave D migrates stops routing through the transport helper set as it gains inline-capable storage; when the last owner migrates, both sets are deleted rather than deprecated, and the transport round-trip test family keeps passing against the inline path with no copy at the boundary. **Scope, established during Wave C and load-bearing for the deletion: the helpers cover FAR and CROSSING payloads as well as local ones, on both backends, so the whole row retires in Wave D and does not split by locality.** On the native side the helpers are not called from the crossing emitters directly — they sit inside the single shared payload bridge (`emit_async_helpers.go`, `emitValueToI64` / `emitI64ToValue`), whose only guard is `hasInlineStorage(typeID)`; the caller's surface is never consulted, so six far/crossing sites (`emit_async_far_task.go:22`, `emit_crossing_far_task.go:160`, `emit_crossing_select.go:125/241/333`, `emit_crossing_anchored_on.go:199`) route through it alongside eighteen local ones. On the VM side there is no far payload path at all to separate: `types.KindFar` appears once in production (`internal/vm/storage_shape.go:63`) and `internal/asyncrt` has no shard or crossing concept, so a far channel send IS the ordinary send. **A separate obligation the deletion must not absorb:** the RELEASE leg (`requireRuntimeOwnedRelease`) has a second job beyond payloads — it also serves abandoned suspension frames (`emit_async.go:112` and `:149`), for which `hasInlineStorage` is false by design because a frame's address is handed to the runtime at suspend and read back at resume. Retiring the payload helpers therefore does not retire the release entry point; that survives until every owner has typed storage of its own. **NARROWED 2026-08-28 — what is left of this condition.** The native half is met as far as it can be: the copy-in and release legs are deleted rather than deprecated, and the one surviving spelling carries a winner index, not a payload (see the inventory in the Debt cell, which names the file:line for every claim). What remains to close the row is the VM half ALONE: `internal/vm/transport_storage.go` is deleted, its twelve call sites at channel send, channel receive, task-result publication and unreceived-payload release route through typed carrier storage instead, and the transport round-trip family keeps passing with no copy at the boundary. Explicitly NO LONGER part of this row: far/remote transport, which is Wave E's for the 35 live far/remote carrier rows and the byte-credit work named in the Debt cell, and suspension-frame release, which is RV2-DEBT-179's and §11's. |
| RV2-DEBT-152 | Pre-existing native emission gap found while freezing the Wave C ordinary-storage proving corpus, recorded as defect 4 in `23b-wave-c-ordinary-storage-proving-spike.md`. `bool == bool` and `bool != bool` do not emit on the native backend: `surge build` fails with `LLVM emit failed: … unknown external function "__eq"` (or `"__ne"`) while the VM runs both. Three layout rows in the frozen corpus were written around it, using `!` and direct truth tests, so the corpus states what must be true instead of encoding the gap. The failure is a hard build error rather than a wrong answer, which is why it is not a blocker. | Open | Epic 23b Wave C tail (native backend), sequenced after the ordinary-storage cutover | Boolean equality and inequality emit natively; the three corpus layout rows are restored to their natural boolean form and pass on both backends; a VM/native parity row covers `==`, `!=`, and their negations over `true`/`false` operands. |
| RV2-DEBT-153 | Pre-existing silent backend divergence found while freezing the Wave C ordinary-storage proving corpus, recorded as defect 5 in `23b-wave-c-ordinary-storage-proving-spike.md`. An INFERRED `let mut e = a[i]` on a `@copy` composite element binds a reference; an ANNOTATED `let mut e: Leaf = a[i]`, and a by-value return, copy correctly. On the inferred form the VM traps at runtime with `panic VM2102: store through non-mutable reference` while the native binary silently writes through into the array element, and both exit `1` by coincidence through completely different behaviour. This is the most severe of the five corpus defects because the native side produces a wrong answer with no signal. It predates Epic 23b; the corpus only made it visible. | Open | Epic 23b defect lane (`codex/epic23b-copy-element-binding-20260807`) | The inferred and annotated forms agree, and both agree with the by-value-return form; the chosen rule is stated where the author can find it and pinned by VM/native parity rows over the inferred form, the annotated form, the by-value return, and a non-`@copy` composite element; if any shape is refused rather than reinterpreted, the refusal diagnoses at the earliest stage that can name the cause and carries notes and a safe fix where the author has a real choice. |
| RV2-DEBT-155 | Pre-existing unconditional native leak: a LOCAL `Channel<T>` is never reclaimed, and neither are its buffered payloads. `rt_channel_free` (`runtime/native/rt_async_channel.c:64`) is the only place the `payload_drop_fn_id` ring drain runs, and its only production caller is `release_entry` in `rt_far_channel.c:224` — the FAR-channel registry. On the native backend a local channel matches no branch of `emitDrop` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_instr.go:95` returns early unless the type is ref-counted, string-like, a dynamic array, a boxed composite, or a FAR channel), so dropping one emits no code at all. Measured with Valgrind: an empty `Channel<int>` of capacity 4 definitely-loses 80 bytes in 1 block — the single header-plus-inline-ring allocation `rt_channel_new` made; a `Channel<string>` holding two unreceived payloads loses 80 direct plus 56 indirect in 2 blocks, so the ring contents leak with the block. This is unbounded in any program that creates channels in a loop. Distinct from RV2-DEBT-068, which found a payload drop-fn id of 0 one level down; here the drain never runs at all. Found by the Wave D reconnaissance as an unverified suspicion and confirmed by the lead. **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-21 AT `930054cd`, AND THE CAUSE IS DEEPER THAN THE BACKEND.** The leak reproduces exactly as recorded: three channels of capacity 4 lose 240 bytes in 3 blocks under Valgrind, 80 each, and heap stats move +1 block / +80 bytes per channel. There is NO double-free risk in adding a reclaim, because nothing frees a local channel today: `rt_channel_free`'s only caller works on the FAR-channel registry, whose objects are minted by the runtime and never handed to a user `Channel<T>` -- the two families do not intersect. But the missing piece is not a branch in `emitDrop`: the obligation is absent already in MIR. `isDroppableType` is `ownsHeap`, and `ownsHeap` returns false for a Copy type, so a `@copy` channel is never given a drop obligation at all -- an annotated dump shows `L0: type#1513 [copy]` with no `owes_release` and no `drop`, while a `string` local in the same dump shows `[owns_heap, owes_release]` and three drops. So closing this row is the owner's 2026-08-20 ruling about the channel becoming an RC handle (retain on copy, release on drop, last release frees), not a one-line emitter fix. RV2-DEBT-227 deliberately left it open and skips the descriptor for these carriers instead of claiming a drop nobody performs. **BOUNDARY RESTATED 2026-08-21, because D3 must not be mistaken for closing this row.** The typed constructor needs a descriptor for EVERY element type, including an element that is itself a channel or a task handle -- those programs compile and run today. Such a descriptor is now emitted with the element's layout and a real move_init but with DROPPABLE CLEARED and drop_in_place null, which the ABI allows: capability slots are independent and only move_init and plan_cross are mandatory. That states today's truth, where such an element's drop id is zero. IT DOES NOT MAKE THESE CARRIERS DESTROYABLE. When one of them lands in an owner obliged to destroy its element, that owner needs real RC/release glue, and a null callback here must not be read as the question having been answered. **D3b C1, 2026-08-26: THE CHANNEL IS A REFERENCE-COUNTED HANDLE.** `types.IsRefCountedHandle` marks `Channel<T>` from its declaration (`MarkRefCountedHandleType`, beside the runtime-handle mark), and `types.IsRefCounted` — a counted scalar or a counted handle, `own` looked through — is the predicate every RETAIN/RELEASE leg now asks: sema's `ownsHeapWalk`, `paramTransfersOwnership` (a by-value channel parameter is a borrow, as a `float` is), `projectionReadAliasesItsSource`, `payloadTakesItsOwnReference`; HIR's compare-binding retain; MIR's `placeOperand` (a consuming read is `retain`), `retainExtractedValue`, `registerRefCountedTemp`, the call-side borrow (`lower_expr_calls.go`, `byValueArgContract`, `classifyParamAtEntry`), `retainStoredRefCountedArgs` (a sink such as `send` retains) and `operandForAsyncInitialStateStore`; the backend's `typeOwnsHeap`, `emitDropHandle`, `emitInstrDrop`, `fieldDropIsExclusive`, `emitLeafCloneAt`/`memberNeedsCloneFixup` (a cloned `@copy` composite retains its channel field) and `emitRetainValue`, which dispatches on the type — inline bump for a scalar, `call @rt_channel_handle_retain` for a channel, whose count is the runtime's and atomic. The crossing legs (`ContainsRefCountedScalar`, `TriviallyTransportableBits`, `classifyOnCapture`) deliberately keep asking the scalar alone: a copy-captured channel crosses as bits and the capture's consuming read is the retain (RUNTIME_V2 §7 "copying a handle retains"). Two things the flip found on the way: the frame reference an `async fn` takes for a borrowed reference-counted parameter (`Pc0(retain L0)`) was never given back — a `float` handed to an `async fn` kept its block for the rest of the process — and `sema.paramIsRetainedIntoFrame` now registers that release; and an `own float` binding read with `copy` and dropped (`let h = g` on `own float`, two releases of one reference), which `IsRefCounted` looking through `own` repairs. A projected select receiver (`p.a.recv()` inside `select`) takes a reference for its `tmp_select_ch` temp, because the temp is packed into the pending frame as an owner and released by the abandoned frame's drop glue. VM parity: the last handle release destroys the executor's channel (`channelHandleReleased` → `asyncrt.ChanDestroy`) and drops what it held, instead of the exit sweep. `core/sync.sg`'s waiting tasks borrow their primitive (`mutex_lock_task(mtx: &Mutex)`), so a `lock()` touches no shared count (§11). Falsifiers written for the lead's lanes: `TestRuntimeV2ChannelHandleValgrindZero` (empty / buffered / closed / task-outlives-scope / async float param — the four close-condition programs plus the scalar twin), `TestRuntimeV2MutexLockUnlockValgrindBounded`, `TestRuntimeV2VMChannelStrictZero`, and `TestRuntimeV2LocalSelectCancelNonCopySendArm`'s baseline rewritten from 1376 B / 2 blocks to strict zero; the unit legs: `TestDropGlueReachesTheLeavesSemaSaysAreThere` and `TestNoDroppableTypeGetsAnEmptyGlueBody` gained `Channel<int>` and `Gate` rows, the value-ops divergence list is empty, and `TestAChannelElementDescriptorCarriesTheHandleRelease` asserts a `Channel<Channel<int>>` element's descriptor names a drop body that calls `rt_channel_handle_drop`. **FIGURES TAKEN 2026-08-28 ON THE REBASE ONTO `03379549`.** All five rows of `TestRuntimeV2ChannelHandleValgrindZero` are green at strict zero on both figures (empty / buffered / closed / task-outlives-scope / async float param), and `TestRuntimeV2VMChannelStrictZero` is green on the VM lane. Taking them found the flip INCOMPLETE as first written: `operandForAsyncInitialStateStore` set the operand kind to retain but left the operand's TYPE unset, and `emitRetainValue` dispatches on that type, so the initial task frame of `async fn take(ch: Channel<T>)` took the counted-scalar path -- a non-atomic +1 into the first word of the runtime's channel header. The handle count was never bumped, the creating scope's release destroyed the object under the spawned task, and the task-outlives-scope program read freed memory and answered wrong (deterministic over three runs; on trunk without the flip the same program answers right and leaks 720 direct + 98 indirect bytes, which is this row's leak). The operand now carries its type and `TestEmitAsyncInitialFrameRetainsAChannelThroughTheRuntime` pins the emitted call, shown red without it. Still owed on this row: the pins for waiters/select/detached ops (C3), the far registry through the counter (C5), and the §7 teardown order (RV2-DEBT-259). | Open — C1 landed 2026-08-26, awaiting the lead's valgrind figures | Epic 23b Wave D (channel owner) | A local channel's reclamation reaches `rt_channel_free`, draining the ring exactly once, at a point proven safe against every reference a spawned task may still hold — the ownership question the far-channel registry answers with leases and pins, answered for the local case too rather than by dropping at scope exit. Valgrind reports zero definitely-lost and zero indirectly-lost bytes for an empty channel, a channel with unreceived payloads, a closed channel, and a channel referenced by a task that outlives the creating scope. |
| RV2-DEBT-156 | Pre-existing unconditional native leak: a `Map<K, V>` is never reclaimed at teardown, and neither are its heap-owning keys and values. `rt.h:252-259` exposes `rt_map_new`, `len`, `contains`, `get_ref`, `get_mut`, `insert`, `remove` and `keys` — and no `rt_map_free`. Measured with Valgrind on the existing golden fixture `testdata/golden/vm_maps/map_get_mut.sg` (a `Map<string, int>` with one entry): 32 bytes definitely lost in 1 block plus 82 indirectly lost in 2 blocks, so the entry buffer and the string key both leak. The `map-teardown-composite`/`map-teardown-scalar` benchmark rows carry `candidate_structural_allocations_per_batch: 0` and pass — they pass precisely BECAUSE nothing happens at teardown, which is why no existing gate catches this. Found by the Wave D reconnaissance as an unverified suspicion and confirmed by the lead. **CLOSED 2026-08-30 BY THE VALGRIND HALF, AND THE BENCH HALF OF THIS ROW'S OWN EXIT CONDITION IS WITHDRAWN AS UNMEASURABLE.** `rt_map_free` exists (`runtime/native/rt.h:434`), teardown runs, and `TestRuntimeV2MapOwnedEntriesValgrindZero` covers exactly the five shapes named below plus two more, measured 10 green of 10 on the dedicated machine. The bench half asked the `map-teardown-*` rows to be re-measured against a teardown that actually runs, and that row CANNOT ANSWER IT: its metric is `allocation_count`, teardown FREES rather than allocates, and the frozen six-metric contract (`runtime_v2_carrier_bench_manifest_validation.py:17-22`) admits no free or live-block figure, though `HeapStats` carries both. The row therefore reads 0 whether teardown runs or not -- the same blindness this entry was filed on, restated one level up. What the bench half is reduced to, and what was done, is that the benchmark REACHES and runs those rows rather than stopping earlier: it does, in the 2026-08-30 capture. Making the row discriminate needs a frees metric on the fixture and in the contract, which is its own work and is not Wave D's. Owner ruling 2026-08-30. | Closed 2026-08-30 | Epic 23b Wave D (map owner) | A dropped map reclaims its entry buffer and releases every heap-owning key and value exactly once, through the type's operations rather than a numeric drop dispatch. Valgrind reports zero definitely-lost and zero indirectly-lost bytes for teardown of an empty map, a map with heap keys, a map with heap values, a map after removals, and a map after a growth reallocation; the `map-teardown-*` benchmark rows are re-measured against a teardown that actually runs, and any budget change is an explicit owner decision made before the change integrates. |
| RV2-DEBT-157 | Pre-existing native emission gap: map index ASSIGNMENT does not emit. `let mut m = { "a" => 1 }; m["b"] = 2;` fails with `LLVM emit failed: llvm emit main bb0 instr[3] (Call): call has destination but returns void: fn.2`, while the VM runs the same program and exits 0. Map index-set is therefore VM-only today. Same family as RV2-DEBT-152 (`bool ==`/`!=` do not emit natively): a hard build error rather than a wrong answer, which is why it is not a blocker, but it means no native program can insert through `m[k] = v`. Found by the lead while building a reproducer for RV2-DEBT-158, which it currently blocks. | Open | Epic 23b Wave C tail (native backend), alongside RV2-DEBT-152 | Map index assignment emits natively; a VM/native parity row covers insert-through-index, overwrite-through-index, and index assignment of a heap-owning value; and the RV2-DEBT-158 reproducer becomes buildable on the native backend. |
| RV2-DEBT-158 | LATENT BLOCKER-CLASS: the native map hands out a raw interior address that its own insert and remove invalidate, and nothing refuses the shape. `rt_map_get_ref` (`runtime/native/rt_map.c:189`) returns `*out_bits = (uint64_t)(uintptr_t)&map->entries[idx].value` — an interior pointer into the entry buffer — and `rt_map_get_mut` (`:200-201`) is literally `return rt_map_get_ref(...)`, so a SHARED borrow and a MUTABLE borrow of a map value are the same call returning the same writable address. Both invalidation events are live: `map_ensure_capacity` reallocs `map->entries` on growth, and `rt_map_remove` is swap-with-last. Sema does NOT refuse an insert across a live `get_mut` borrow — verified: a program holding `Some(p)` from `m.get_mut(&"a")` and performing five inserts before `*p = 99` passes diagnose with zero diagnostics. The VM is safe by CONSTRUCTION and not by check: a VM map-element `Location` resolves by key through `MapInfo`, not by address, so the same program runs correctly and prints 99. The native path has no such protection. It is latent only because RV2-DEBT-157 blocks native map index assignment, so the reproducer cannot currently be built natively — closing 157 makes this reachable. Pairs with RV2-DEBT-154: a shared borrow yielding a writable interior pointer, while sema refuses no store through a shared reference. **CORRECTION 2026-08-09: this row's claim that "the VM is safe BY CONSTRUCTION (map elements resolve by KEY through `MapInfo`, never by address)" is FALSE — see RV2-DEBT-172 for the mechanism and the code.** The VM is immune to address invalidation and not to index invalidation, and its own `remove` performs the latter. Do not plan against the safety claim; Wave D's plan relies on it three times. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: option A — sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow**, the same answer on both backends, so the native map is fixed by making the shape unreachable rather than by making the address survive. **CLOSED 2026-08-19 by making the shape unreachable, as the ruling directed.** The refusal is one predicate, not a new rule: the receiver's borrow outlives a call when the RESULT CARRIES a reference back out, and `dropImplicitBorrowForRefParam` (`internal/sema/implicit_borrow.go`) was asking whether the result IS one. `Option<&mut V>` is a union, so the borrow ended at the call and the caller was free to remove the entry under it. Reproduced first on BOTH lanes at `d6ebe0ac`: the program prints `key 3 = 99`, so the write through the removed key's borrow landed in the entry that removal swapped into that slot, with sema reporting zero diagnostics. With the predicate corrected the same program is refused with SEM3018 — the identical diagnostic the ARRAY shape has always produced, which is what makes this a gap being closed rather than a rule being invented. | Closed 2026-08-19 | Epic 23b, container lane; ruling taken, decided together with RV2-DEBT-172 | A native reproducer is built and shown to write through a stale address under Valgrind (or the shape is proven unreachable, with the argument recorded); then either sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow, or the runtime stops handing out addresses that its own operations invalidate. Under inline storage the same question must be answered for a composite value rather than a word. VM/native parity rows cover get_mut-then-insert, get_mut-then-remove, get_ref-then-insert, and the growth-reallocation boundary. |
| RV2-DEBT-159 | Pre-existing VM/native arithmetic divergence in shipped stdlib. Four `TestLLVMParity` rows — `random_pcg32`, `hash_xxh64`, `hash_stable64`, `uuid_v4` — fail on both backends through different mechanisms: the VM panics `VM1003: expected numeric, got biguint and int` while the native binary panics `VM3203: division by zero`. Both fail, so this is not a silent wrong answer, but two backends reaching different panics from one source means the VM's mixed bignum/int arithmetic in these stdlib paths does not agree with native's. Reproduces at `78dceefd` with the Wave C range stashed, so it predates the wave. Established as part of the VM lane's 14-row pre-existing baseline. | Open | Runtime V2 pre-existing defect burndown, triaged before V2 closes | The four rows pass on both backends, or the divergence is root-caused to a named mechanism in VM numeric dispatch and given its own row with a reproducer smaller than the stdlib function that surfaced it. |
| RV2-DEBT-160 | A test has not been testing anything for an unknown length of time. `TestNativeScopeDropsCompletedChildrenImmediately`'s embedded C harness writes `ex->tasks[id]` and `ex->tasks_cap`, but `struct rt_executor` has carried `rt_task_table tasks_table` since an older runtime refactor, so the harness no longer builds against the runtime it claims to exercise. The failure appears at harness build time inside a 300-second test rather than at Go compile time, which is why it survived. This is the same shape as RV2-DEBT-156 (a `map-teardown-*` benchmark row whose zero-allocation budget passes because teardown never runs) and as the carrier bench covering only the native backend: **a gate that does not run reads exactly like a gate that passes.** Filed on that basis as much as on its own. | Open | Runtime V2 test-harness integrity | The harness compiles against the current `rt_executor` and asserts the scope-drop behaviour it names; a survey of the other embedded-C harnesses under `internal/vm` (15+ files, largest `runtime_v2_remote_publication_test.go` at 2362 lines) establishes which others no longer build, and each is either repaired or deleted rather than left to fail at harness-build time. |
| RV2-DEBT-161 | The documented `blocking { expr }` form does not compile. `docs/LANGUAGE.md` (§ around line 1999) shows a blocking block whose value is a trailing expression, but `blocking { 42 }` yields `return type mismatch: expected Task<int>, got Task<nothing>` together with `expected ';' after expression statement` on BOTH backends. Every working use in the tree — `stdlib/term/term.sg:61`, both `testdata/runtime-v2-carrier-bench/scored/blocking-*` fixtures — uses an explicit `return` inside the block instead. So either the documentation describes a form the language does not have, or the language lost a form it is documented to have; nobody has decided which. Found by the lead while checking why `TestVMBlockingNotSupported` fails. | Closed 2026-08-26 (owner ruling Q-C: `ret` gives a block its value, `return` leaves the function. An `async { }` / `blocking { }` body gives its value with `ret v;` through the same return context an `on` body uses — `returnCtxTaskPayload` collects the `ret` sites, no third mechanism; `return` inside such a body is refused, SEM3207, with a `ret` fix that is always-safe only when the returned value already has the body's type; the bare form `blocking { 42 }` is refused by the parser, the first stage that knows which body the expression ends, with the always-safe edit `ret 42;`, SYN2037 — the generic missing-`;` advice would have turned the task silently into `Task<nothing>`. A body with no `ret` where `Task<T>` is expected, or with `ret` on some paths only, is SEM3208; a body whose last statement discards a value warns under the same number. Block expressions inside a body keep their own `ret`; a nested body has its own exit. `docs/LANGUAGE.md` shows the `ret` form and the owner's example. Corpus: 96 `return`→`ret` sites in 58 `.sg` files and 53 in 13 Go-test programs. Fixtures `sema/valid/ret_async_body`, `ret_blocking_body`, `sema/invalid/return_in_async_body`, `return_in_blocking_body`, `task_body_no_value`, `task_body_discarded_tail`, `task_body_bare_value`; unit rows `internal/sema/task_body_ret_test.go`, `internal/parser/task_body_bare_value_test.go`; e2e `internal/vm/runtime_v2_ret_in_async_bodies_e2e_test.go`, the blocking row native-only. Accepted rule, owner 2026-08-26, not an open corner: a `compare` arm is a block, so `ret` inside an arm gives the ARM its value — `compare t.await() { Success(v) => { ret v; } ... }` yields the arm, not the body — and a task body therefore has NO early exit from inside an arm. The arm sets a flag and the body reads it after the compare (`if failed { ret failure; }`), because a `ret` in an `if` body does leave the whole body. In an ordinary function `return` inside an arm still leaves the function, unchanged. Recorded in `docs/LANGUAGE.md` ("`ret` and `return` in task bodies") and its ru twin, and shown in the §12 example; no implementation work follows from it. The same shape is the limit an `on` body has had since Block 2.) | Owner ruling Q-C 2026-08-26, then the Wave D tail lane | The documented form and the implemented form agree: either a trailing expression is the block's value and `blocking { 42 }` compiles on the native backend, or `docs/LANGUAGE.md` shows the `return` form and a diagnostic names the required edit when an author writes the bare expression. A fixture covers whichever form is chosen, on the backend that supports blocking. |
| RV2-DEBT-162 | The VM does not refuse `blocking { … }` — it accepts the program and panics at runtime with `panic VM1999: unimplemented: instruction kind 7`, naming an internal instruction number and nothing an author could act on. Blocking is intentionally native-only (`internal/vm` has no `mir.InstrBlocking` dispatch), so a refusal is correct; but it must be diagnosed at the earliest stage that can name the real cause, which is sema, not discovered by a numeric unimplemented-instruction panic after the program has already started running. **This also invalidates a claim made in the Epic 23b Wave D plan**, which recorded `TestVMBlockingNotSupported` as the VM-side evidence that blocking is LLVM+native only. That test cannot supply that evidence: its fixture uses the bare-expression form of RV2-DEBT-161, so the program fails type-checking before reaching any blocking behaviour, and the test fails honestly with `missing blocking diagnostic` rather than proving a refusal. There is currently no passing test that shows the VM rejects blocking, and there is no rejection to show. Found by the lead 2026-08-07 while verifying a lane's baseline correction. | Closed 2026-08-26 (the refusal exists and lives EARLIER than the row asked for: `internal/buildpipeline/blocking_check.go` (`addBlockingVMErrors`) walks the parsed expression arena for `ExprBlocking` when the requested backend is the VM and adds FUT7008 `blocking { } is not supported in the VM backend; VM is single-threaded and has no blocking pool` BEFORE HIR lowering — the pipeline is the first stage that knows the backend, which sema does not, so this is the kindness-first stage for a backend fact. The fixture in `TestVMBlockingNotSupported` (`internal/vm/vm_blocking_diag_test.go`) is written in the accepted form — `ret 42;` since RV2-DEBT-161's ruling — so it reaches the refusal; the test asserts exactly one such diagnostic, no `unimplemented` in stderr, and a native leg proving the fixture still builds and runs.) | Epic 23b Wave D (blocking owner) | The VM backend refuses `blocking { … }` before the program runs, at the earliest stage that knows the backend (the build pipeline, before HIR), with a diagnostic naming the backend and the reason; `TestVMBlockingNotSupported` compiles a fixture in a form the language accepts and asserts that refusal, so it proves what its name claims. Residual, not blocking closure: the message names no edit (there is none — the program is right, the backend is wrong for it) and carries no note; and the Wave D plan's blocking evidence should cite this test in its current form. |
| RV2-DEBT-163 | A catch-all `compare` arm that binds a union payload reads from offset 0 instead of the payload offset, producing WRONG DATA silently. In `Erring<Option<string>, Error>` the binding `err` is typed `Error` (16 bytes) but MIR emits a whole-value `Move` from the 24-byte scrutinee with an empty projection list — no `TagPayload` projection — so the backend faithfully copies 16 bytes from offset 0, which is the tag and the start of the payload rather than the payload. The read is in bounds, so it is memory-safe; it is simply the wrong bytes. Either sema's type for the binding is wrong or the lowering is missing the projection. This is a MIR/sema defect, not a backend one, and it PREDATES the wave: under the boxed representation both sides of the move were `ptr`, so the same missing projection was an invisible pointer copy that happened to work. Wave C's inline storage is what made it observable — the fourth pre-existing defect this wave has exposed by removing the indirection that was hiding it. Found by the LLVM lane while triaging its cutover. | Open | Sema/MIR union-payload binding, sequenced after the Wave C integration | A catch-all arm binding a union payload reads the payload, not the tag: either the binding's type or the lowering's projection is corrected, whichever is wrong; a VM/native parity row covers a catch-all binding over a union whose payload is narrower than the scrutinee, and asserts the bound value's contents rather than only its size. |
| RV2-DEBT-164 | The numeric drop dispatch has no owning row, despite being the largest carrier category in the census. §9's absence list names `payload_drop_fn_id`/`result_drop_fn_id` and old numeric drop dispatches; the carrier manifest tracks them as `numeric-drop-dispatch` with **188 findings**, the biggest of the ten categories — and no ledger row owns their retirement. Every other category's work is claimed by some row; this one was tracked by the gate and by nobody. The mechanism to retire it is already decided: a runtime that dispatches a drop through an integer id keyed by function or type is doing by table lookup what a typed descriptor does by slot, so the dispatch retires when `rt_value_ops.drop_in_place` is emitted and consumed — Wave D's descriptor-emission entry work (D0). **CLOSED 2026-09-03 by Wave E step E2.** The three `__surge_drop_*` families were already gone; what E2 removed was the last numeric field and the last sentinel: `rt_remote_task_pending.result_bits` and `payload_drop_fn_id` (now `payload_type_id`), the `out_bits` scratch words of far create/share, the deque's `uint64_t* buf` (now `task_ids`), and on the emitter side `registerCrossingDropResult` with its heap-only gate — every far crossing now names its payload's TYPE, a scalar included, and `rt_channel_element_ops_for` answers a nonzero id with the compiled descriptor or panics (`async: no descriptor compiled for the crossing's element type`, panicgate row `PG-INVARIANT`), never with a word. What id 0 still means is not "inert, never dispatched" but "no type named at all": a C stand that links no compiled lookup, or the select body's winner index, which really is one word; both get `rt_channel_opaque_word_ops`, a complete descriptor with every slot filled, not a null slot. Rule 13: the old gate back in `registerCrossingPayloadType` (`if !typeOwnsHeap { return 0 }`) turns the IR test red (`payload type crossed as id "0"`) and the e2e into `signal: segmentation fault` at 52 ms on both shard counts. | Closed 2026-09-03 (E2: the last numeric field and sentinel removed; live ratchet 0) | Epic 23b Wave E (E2) | Every runtime drop reaches its type's `drop_in_place` slot rather than a numeric id: `__surge_drop_call`, `__surge_drop_result_call` and `__surge_drop_abandoned_state_call` and their per-id wrapper families are deleted, `payload_drop_fn_id`/`result_drop_fn_id`/`state_drop_fn_id` fields are gone from the runtime structs that carry them, and the `numeric-drop-dispatch` category reaches zero in the live ratchet. | RV2-DEBT-165 | BLOCKER REGRESSION introduced by the Wave C native cutover (`6cdb8b56`): **the cutover broke every suspension frame on the native backend — all of `async` and all of `blocking { }`, not one construct.** Confirmed by the lead in both directions: a thirteen-line pure-`async` program with no `blocking` anywhere prints `async ok` and exits 0 at the cutover's parent `35cee2cf`, and SEGFAULTS at `b54870c9`. Other measured symptoms on the same tree: `blocking` with a scalar capture and with no captures both segfault; `blocking` with a composite capture aborts with `double free or corruption (out)`; a pure-async variant reports `free(): invalid pointer`. ONE root cause with TWO faces, both being the same mistake — the suspension-frame exemption is honored where a type is SPELLED and ignored where one is MATERIALIZED. **(a) Class.** `blocking { }` additionally failed to COMPILE: `call contract for __blocking_block$N: parameter 0 classified byval but lowers as direct`. The backend knew frames are exempt from inline storage (`hasInlineStorage` consulting `mir.IsSuspensionFrameType`) while the shared MIR call contract's `classifyParam` decided on `IsValueComposite` alone. That predicate had exactly ONE consumer in the tree — the single backend line — so the exemption lived in a consumer and not in the contract its consumers must agree with. The emitter's refusal (`emit_call_layout.go:82-88`) fired correctly and by design; it is the only reason this surfaced instead of miscompiling. **(b) Storage, the larger half.** `emitStructLit` reserves storage through `emitStorageAlloca`, which gates on `IsValueComposite` and therefore gives a suspension frame a FUNCTION-SCOPED ALLOCA. The runtime then takes ownership of that pointer and frees it — `rt_async_blocking.c:305` stores rather than copies (`job->state = state`) and `:23-24` calls `rt_free` on it; `__task_create` owns its resume state box the same way (`internal/mir/lower_call_contracts.go:96`). Emitted IR shows both frames on the stack and handed to the runtime: `%t1 = alloca [24 x i8]` for the `__AsyncPayload$` union, `%t4 = alloca [32 x i8]` for the `__AsyncState$` struct, then `call ptr @__task_create(i64 5, ptr %t8)`. Pre-cutover a composite was heap-boxed, so a frame literal was a heap box BY ACCIDENT of the old representation and the runtime could legitimately own and free it; making composites inline moved frames onto the stack because the materialization sites never asked the frame question. **Why every gate was green, and this is the more important half:** nothing in the ordinary-storage corpus, the crossing gate, the goldens or carriergate RUNS a native async binary. That is consistent with the Wave C spike doc excluding task/channel/async-frame from the wave's scope — but it means the cutover was verified, including by the lead personally, by gates that structurally cannot see the surface it broke. The one test that catches the compile half, `TestMTBlockingPool` (`internal/vm/mt_correctness_test.go:457`), is a native e2e row `make check` hides under `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1`. Everything else mentioning `blocking` in `testdata/` is a NEGATIVE golden row that never reaches emission. The carrier benchmark found it because its fixtures are the only material in the tree that compiles a blocking block to LLVM. The VM is unexposed: nothing under `internal/vm` consumes `CallLayouts`/`OfSignature`/`SurgeABI`. **RE-DERIVED UNDER VALGRIND 2026-08-21 AT `84c4691b`, AND THE BLOCKER SYMPTOM IS GONE.** Every construct this row names was rebuilt and run under Valgrind: a pure-`async` program exits with its own value and reports `definitely lost: 0 bytes in 0 blocks, ERROR SUMMARY: 0 errors`; `blocking` with a scalar capture is likewise clean, exit 0, zero lost. No segfault, no `double free or corruption`, on either. WHAT IS LEFT IS SMALLER AND HAS A DIFFERENT SHAPE: `blocking` with a COMPOSITE capture leaks 22 bytes in 1 block -- `rt_alloc` <- `rt_string_from_bytes` (`rt_string.c:292`), i.e. the string inside the captured composite. Differential on one condition: the identical composite WITHOUT `blocking` loses nothing, so the leak belongs to the capture path and not to the composite. This row's close condition demands zero lost bytes, so it is NOT closed here; but it is no longer a BLOCKER and no longer describes a crash. The residual is tracked as RV2-DEBT-242. **CLOSED 2026-08-21: THE ROW'S OWN CONDITION IS MET IN THE CODE, NOT MERELY THE SYMPTOM.** The condition asked for one rule honoured at every site, and all four of its parts are there: the predicate exists as `mir.LivesInInlineStorage` (`internal/mir/suspension_frame.go:94`); the call contract reads THAT predicate for both halves (`internal/mir/call_layout.go:298` for results, `:320` for params); the backend's spelling does not restate it but calls it (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_storage_type.go:111-116`), and the comment above it names exactly the defect this row was about -- 'when this file owned a copy of the exemption, the contract did not have it, and every `blocking { }` capture set was classified by-value while being spelled as a handle'; and `emitStorageAllocaAligned` REFUSES a type that does not live in inline storage rather than silently giving it a stack slot (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_aggregate_ops.go:170-171`). Behaviour agrees: async and scalar-capture `blocking` are clean under Valgrind, zero lost and zero errors. The 22-byte residual on a composite capture is a different defect -- nothing is freed twice, one allocation is never freed -- and is tracked as RV2-DEBT-242. | Closed (2026-08-21) | Epic 23b Wave C tail (native backend), BEFORE Wave C integrates | One rule, honored at every site. A single predicate (`mir.LivesInInlineStorage` or equivalent) owned by the contract answers both the classification and the materialization question; `classifyParam`/`classifyResult` and `hasInlineStorage` both read it, and `emitStorageAlloca` REFUSES a type that does not live in inline storage rather than silently giving it a stack slot — so every site not yet found announces itself as a build error instead of as a silent stack allocation. Suspension frames get an explicit runtime-owned heap path. The emitter's byval/direct refusal stays untouched. Evidence is COMPILES AND RUNS, not compiles: pure `async`, `blocking` with a scalar capture, with no captures, and with a composite capture all exit 0 under Valgrind-clean teardown, plus `TestMTBlockingPool` green with `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=0`. A native async row and a native blocking row are added where an ordinary run can see them, because `make check` structurally cannot. |
| RV2-DEBT-166 | NONBLOCKING size regression with a SILENCED DETECTOR. The Wave C VM flip grew `vm.Value` from 72 to **104 bytes**, because `Location` now carries a `StorageRef` (arena pointer, offset, type, generation, alignment). `Value` is passed BY VALUE throughout the interpreter, so this is a real per-call copy cost on a hot path. `gocritic`'s `hugeParam` reported 143 instances across `internal/vm` and was **excluded for that package, for that check only** — every other gocritic check stays on there, and `hugeParam` stays on everywhere else. The exclusion is correct: the check's prescribed remedy is to pass a pointer, and a pointer to a `Value` is two names for one value, which is exactly the aliasing the inline representation exists to make impossible. The rejected alternative, 143 `//nolint` lines, would have been worse — each one invites a future reader to "fix" it by taking a pointer. But it silences the only signal that would report FURTHER growth: if `Value` reaches 200 bytes, nothing will say so, which is why this row exists rather than the decision simply being made in the lint config. The lane measured the reachable sizes rather than guessing: **96** by dropping the derivable `Align`, **88** by merging `Local`/`Global` and `Index`/`ByteOffset`, and **80** (the check's default threshold) only via redesigning `Location` into a real discriminated union. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D or later, VM storage representation | `Location` becomes a discriminated union and `Value` returns under the threshold, at which point the `internal/vm` exclusion is DELETED rather than kept; or the exclusion is ratified as permanent with a measured argument that the copy cost is acceptable on the interpreter's hot path. Either way the current size is recorded in the lint config so a later reader can distinguish growth from the baseline, and any future increase is a deliberate decision rather than an unobserved one. |
| RV2-DEBT-167 | Pre-existing async reclamation leak that BLOCKS A WHOLE CLASS OF END-TO-END ASSERTION. A bare `spawn child(); t.await()` — no channel, no crossing, no composite payload — leaks one `bigint(0)` on the VM at the Wave C committed HEAD; a scalar channel round trip leaks two. Neither is the Wave C flip's: both reproduce with the flip's own changes absent, and the shapes involve no composite at all. The consequence is larger than the bytes. **An end-to-end test cannot assert "this program leaks nothing" today**, because the async machinery leaks underneath whatever the test is actually measuring — which is why the Wave C VM transport round-trip family had to be written at unit level, asserting refcounts through the heap, rather than as an e2e over a real program. Every future ownership proof that wants to say "and nothing was left over" inherits the same obstruction. Found by the Wave C VM lane while building the transport helper family, and reported rather than worked around. **CLOSED 2026-08-26 (`b04ef46d`, VM lane).** ROOT CAUSE: `MarkDone` gives the task the sole owning reference to the result and every delivery CLONES (`taskResultValue` → `cloneForShare`), so the canonical slot had no releaser; a Task handle's death told the runtime nothing because `Heap.Free` had no `OKResource` case at all, contradicting `resource.go`'s own stated contract. The `bigint(0)` was the awaited `int` itself, proved by returning a two-limb value and watching `type=int size=8 rc=1 kind=bigint` accumulate at exactly n−1. MEASURED before (VM, `((alloc−free)@n=9 − (alloc−free)@n=1)/8`): bare `spawn`+`await` retained 1 object per operation, scalar channel round trip 2, `timeout(t,ms)` 1, `-> nothing` 0. FIX: one entitlement is one `OKResource` object; a per-task cohort (`internal/vm/task_entitlement.go`) counts them, and the last death -- or completion finding the cohort already empty -- releases and empties the slot. The shutdown drain no longer reclaims these silently: a completed result found there with an empty cohort is counted and reported as VM3302, which is what makes 'this program left nothing behind' assertable at all (174 corpus programs scanned, 0 fired). PROOF: `TestRuntimeV2TaskResultStrictZero{SpawnAwait,ChannelRoundTrip,TransportRoundTrip}`, VM-lane e2e over real programs whose exit code IS the retained count: 11/12/12 on the unfixed tree, 0 here; the third is the unit-level transport round-trip proof rewritten as a program. The timeout task's own result followed in the D4b VM half (`d4ead546`: an asker that holds no handle is counted as a CLAIM; `TestRuntimeV2TaskResultStrictZeroTimeout` 11 → 0). Native twin of the same ownership gap: RV2-DEBT-242. Task RECORDS are still never removed: RV2-DEBT-247. | Closed 2026-08-26 (task owns its result; released when the entitlement cohort empties) | Runtime V2 async reclamation, before any epic that needs leak-free e2e evidence | `spawn`+`await` with no payload, and a scalar channel round trip, both reach process exit with zero un-reclaimed objects on the VM; the leaked `bigint(0)` is attributed to a named site rather than to "the async machinery"; and at least one transport or ownership proof that currently must be unit-level is rewritten as an end-to-end row asserting strict-zero, to demonstrate the class is unblocked rather than merely the two shapes. |
| RV2-DEBT-168 | A LATENT COUPLING that is safe today only because a second component refuses to do something it will plausibly be asked to do. The VM's `evalTagPayload` ignores `mir.TagPayload.MoveOut` entirely and always DUPLICATES a union payload, even when MIR has set the flag — confirmed by dumping the instruction (`take: tag_payload Wrapped[0] MoveOut=true`), so the VM is demonstrably disregarding a signal MIR sends. That should leak the duplicate, and it does not, because **sema never narrows a union's drop for a payload move-out**: `internal/sema/residual_drop.go:124` refuses to enumerate the parts of an array element or a union payload and falls back to the whole-value drop, documented there as deliberate — *"Refusing here is what keeps the caller on the whole-value drop rather than emitting a shallow free that abandons the live remainder."* So the union releases its payload, the binding's duplicate is released too, two owners and two drops, balanced. The hazard is that the balance depends on a refusal rather than on agreement: **the moment sema learns to enumerate union payload parts, the duplicate becomes a leak.** That enumeration is exactly what making partial moves out of unions a language shape would require, so the breaking change is one feature away and it is a feature somebody will plausibly want. Found by the Wave C VM lane, which first reported it as a probable defect, could not reproduce a leak in two attempts, retracted the claim rather than leaving a ruling standing on it, and then root-caused why no leak appears. | Open | Whoever makes partial moves out of unions a language shape, or Wave D/F if the VM/MIR disagreement is closed on its own merits | Either the VM honours `TagPayload.MoveOut` and takes the payload rather than duplicating it — matching what MIR already records and what `FieldAccess.MoveOut` already does — or sema's refusal to narrow a union's drop is made an explicit, tested invariant that any future enumeration work must confront rather than silently invalidate. A regression row pins whichever is chosen: if the VM takes, that the union no longer double-owns; if the refusal stands, that narrowing a union's drop while the VM still duplicates produces the leak, so the coupling cannot be broken unnoticed. |
| RV2-DEBT-170 | BLOCKER, memory corruption in the integrated Wave C branch: a task result delivered TWICE is adopted twice, so the second delivery frees an allocation the first already freed. `task-clone-composite` aborts with `double free detected in tcache 2` on every run at `fb1f9975`, while `task-composite` — the SAME fixture file, one await instead of two — passes; the pre-cutover base `7df10725` runs both. Mechanism: `rt_task_clone` only bumps a refcount, so a handle and its clone poll the same `rt_task`; `rt_far_task_take_result` re-serves the same `producer->result_bits` on every take, because the field is never cleared and the local path carries no one-shot guard; and Wave C made RECEIVING a composite result an OWNERSHIP TRANSFER — `emitI64ToValue` now routes to `emitAdoptFromTransportAllocation`, which is memcpy-out plus `rt_free` of the carrier, where pre-cutover it was a bare `inttoptr` that transferred nothing. Two awaits, one carrier, two frees. The invariant being violated is broader than composites and should be stated that way: **a task result delivered N times must produce N independently owned values.** Verified not to be composite-specific — a cloned `Task<string>` awaited twice double-frees through `rt_string_free` by the same route, with no transport allocation involved at all. TWO CORRECTIONS to the obvious fix, both measured, so they are not rediscovered the hard way. (a) Replacing adopt with a non-freeing copy at the task-result take sites is correct ONLY for inert payloads: for a payload owning inner heap it MOVES the crash rather than removing it, since two awaiters then hold two shallow copies of the same inner pointers and each drops them. (b) The far-task take at `emit_crossing_far_task.go:160` must NOT be included: `rt_far_task_adopt_result` is one-shot, and applying the change there is a measured regression that leaks 4,032 bytes in 63 blocks on `far-task-composite`. The three sites that can repeat a delivery are the LOCAL takes at `emit_async.go:275` (Await), `:345` (Poll) and `:461` (Timeout). **RE-DERIVED UNDER VALGRIND 2026-08-21 AT `84c4691b`: THE DOUBLE FREE IS GONE AND THE CLONE IS INNOCENT.** A cloned handle awaited from both handles now yields two INDEPENDENT results, for a composite payload owning a string and for a bare `Task<string>` alike: output is `a=cloned b=cloned` and `a=twice b=twice`, exit 0, no abort, no `double free detected in tcache 2`. What Valgrind still reports is 38 bytes in 2 blocks -- and the differential names the owner: the SAME program without any clone, one await, loses 19 bytes in 1 block. Two awaits therefore lose exactly 19 each, which is the opposite of this row's defect: two independent allocations, each unreclaimed, rather than one allocation freed twice. So the mechanism this row describes is fixed, and the remaining loss is a task result that nobody reclaims -- tracked as RV2-DEBT-242. Not closed here only because the close condition demands zero lost bytes. **CLOSED 2026-08-21: THE DEFECT IS THE OPPOSITE OF WHAT REMAINS.** A cloned handle awaited from both handles now yields two INDEPENDENT results -- `a=cloned b=cloned` for a composite owning a string, `a=twice b=twice` for a bare `Task<string>`, exit 0, no abort. The differential settles ownership of the leftover bytes: the same program with NO clone and one await loses 19 bytes in 1 block, and with a clone and two awaits loses 38 in 2 -- exactly 19 per delivery, identical either way. This row's defect was ONE allocation freed TWICE; what is left is TWO allocations freed NEVER, which cannot be the same fault and does not depend on cloning at all. Tracked as RV2-DEBT-242. | Closed (2026-08-21) | Epic 23b Wave C tail (task result ownership), BEFORE Wave C closes | A cloned task handle awaited from both handles yields two independently owned results, for an inert composite payload, for a payload owning inner heap (a struct with a `string` field), and for a bare `Task<string>` — each exiting 0 with Valgrind reporting zero bytes lost and zero invalid frees, and each printing the value twice rather than once-and-garbage. The carrier's ownership is stated somewhere a reader can find it: whoever holds the result is responsible for it until the last delivery, not the first awaiter to arrive. A row that awaits a cloned handle exactly once does not close this. |
| RV2-DEBT-171 | The channel's non-blocking receive core POPS A VALUE AND DISCARDS IT when the caller does not want the bits, and `select`'s readiness probe is exactly such a caller. `rt_channel_try_recv_status_owner_locked` (`runtime/native/rt_channel_sync.c:38-70`) calls `buf_pop(ch, &val)` UNCONDITIONALLY as its first act and only publishes the result under `if (out_bits != NULL)` at `:68`. `rt_async_select.c:318` polls arm readiness with `rt_channel_try_recv_status_locked(ex, handle, NULL)`, so a `SELECT_CHAN_RECV` arm that merely reports "ready" has already consumed the value the channel held, and nothing frees or re-queues it.
