It became measurable the moment the anchored send began publishing composite payloads into transport allocations: `far-select-composite` leaks 4,096 bytes in 64 blocks under Valgrind, exactly one 64-byte payload per operation. The leak is the mild reading. The severe one is that a readiness PROBE has a side effect on the thing it probes, which means the value is gone before any arm can bind it — and the reason no test noticed is that the far-select fixture's arms bind nothing, so its checksum is correct while its payload is silently destroyed. That also retires an inference recorded during Wave C: `far-select-composite` was NOT evidence that the anchored send was sound; it passed because its stale payload address was never adopted, a false negative. Found 2026-08-08 by the lead while closing RV2-DEBT-169. | Open | Runtime V2 channel/select core | A readiness probe does not consume. Either the recv core stops popping when it has nowhere to publish the value, or `select` stops asking the question through a mutating entry point — whichever keeps the shard-lock discipline intact, since every `channel_pop_candidate_locked` caller already holds the owner shard lock and the fix must not widen what runs under it. Proven by a `select` over a channel carrying a composite whose arm BINDS the payload: the value arrives intact, is dropped exactly once, and Valgrind reports zero bytes lost; plus a negative row showing that a probe which reports "not ready" has removed nothing. |
| RV2-DEBT-172 | The VM's map element borrow is INDEX-KEYED, not key-keyed, so the VM has the same class of dangling-element bug RV2-DEBT-158 records for the native map — and 158's text asserts the opposite. That row states "The VM is safe BY CONSTRUCTION (map elements resolve by KEY through `MapInfo`, never by address)". It does not. `internal/vm/intrinsic_map.go:246` (`get_mut`, and `:172` for `get_ref`) builds `Location{Kind: LKMapElem, Handle: handle, Index: idx32}` — a POSITIONAL index resolved once at borrow time from `obj.MapIndex[key]`, with the key not retained. `internal/vm/place_container.go:221-224` (`storeMapElem`, and `:184-196` for `loadMapElem`) then resolves `obj.MapEntries[idx]` BY INDEX, bounds-checked only; `MapInfo` is consulted at `:225` solely to obtain the value TYPE for union retagging, never to re-resolve the key. And the VM's own removal is swap-with-last (`intrinsic_map.go:421-431`), identical in shape to native `rt_map.c:241-248`: it repairs `obj.MapIndex[swapKey]` but nothing repairs outstanding `Location`s. So `let p = m.get_mut(&"b"); m.remove(&"b"); *p = 99` writes into the slot `"c"` was swapped into, and `place_container.go:241` first calls `vm.dropValue` on the occupant — dropping `c`'s live value and then overwriting it. Memory-safe in Go's terms; an ownership bug in the language's terms, and silently wrong data either way. The distinction that matters: the VM is immune to ADDRESS invalidation, which is what 158 is about on the native side, and NOT immune to INDEX invalidation, which its own remove performs. 158's cited native reproducer (five inserts between `get_mut` and the write) does pass on the VM, because `append` does not shift indices — removal does, and nobody tried removal. Found 2026-08-08 while adversarially re-reviewing the Wave D plan, which relies on 158's VM-safety claim three times over: to scope 158 as native-only, to declare the container lane unblocked, and to reduce the VM's obligation to "keep key resolution honest" — a mechanism that does not exist. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: option A — sema refuses.** Mutating a container while a borrow of one of its elements is live is a compile error, on both backends, decided at the only stage that still knows the borrow is outstanding. The alternative considered and rejected was making the borrow survive relocation (a stable slot table or key retention on the native side, the key kept in `Location` on the VM side): it costs work on every element access and changes the container representation in the middle of the wave that is already changing it. Option A costs nothing at run time and removes the VM/native asymmetry at compile time, which is what the no-GC ownership model asks for. **CLOSED 2026-08-19 together with RV2-DEBT-158, which is how the ruling asked for it.** This row named the mechanism correctly and the fix is where the two rows meet: the VM's index-keyed element borrow and the native map's interior pointer are both made unreachable by refusing the shape, so neither lane has to keep a stale index or a stale address alive. EVIDENCE: `testdata/golden/sema/invalid/map_element_borrow_across_remove` is refused with SEM3018 and, with the predicate reverted, is accepted and prints the corrupted value on both lanes. | Closed 2026-08-19 | Epic 23b, container lane; ruling taken, implementation pending | The VM and the native map answer the element-borrow question the SAME way, whatever way is chosen, and 158's text is corrected so no future reader plans against a safety claim the code does not make. Whichever answer is taken — sema refuses container mutation across a live element borrow, or the borrow yields something that survives relocation — it is proven by a VM row and a native row that both perform `get_mut`, then a `remove` that relocates the borrowed entry, then a write through the old borrow, and both refuse or both behave identically. A test that only inserts does not close this row. |
| RV2-DEBT-174 | **PARTIALLY CLOSED 2026-08-08.** The compiler half landed in `853aafba`: a coroutine writes its suspension state in place instead of rebuilding the two boxes on every entry to a pending block, so an aborted park now costs nothing. Re-measured afterwards at nine runs per row, **44 of 46 rows report a single value across all nine**, and the twenty-five budgets that had drifted were re-captured from those agreeing runs (the manifest schema takes an exact key set and its parser is itself a digest-frozen harness file, so the sample count backing each number is recorded here and in the commit message rather than as a new manifest field — that part of the close condition is deliberately unmet, not overlooked). **What remains is the whole of the gate-contract half, and exactly two rows:** `channel-unbuffered-scalar` (nine runs: 3×5, 4×4) and `channel-unbuffered-composite` (100×5, 99×3, 101×1). Their spread is ±1 and sign-flipping, which is NOT the ±2 aborted-park signature the compiler half removed — a second contributor, still unidentified, lives on the unbuffered-channel path. Until it is found or the contract gains a tolerance, those two rows cannot hold an exact budget and the canonical benchmark cannot go green, so their budgets are left at their stale values rather than set to a modal number that would make the gate a coin flip. ORIGINAL FINDING: the carrier benchmark's allocation counts are NOT DETERMINISTIC, and the gate asserts exact equality, so eleven rows could not pass at any threshold. Measured by the lead 2026-08-08, three samples per row on a quiet host: `far-immediate-scalar` [281, 283, 281], `far-select-scalar` [681, 679, 679], `blocking-scalar` [342, 344, 342], `blocking-composite` [408, 406, 406], `far-large-result` and `far-task-composite` [665, 667, 665], `far-channel-scalar` [672, 672, 674], `far-jumbo-contention` [1170, 1170, 1172], `channel-unbuffered-scalar` [131, 131, 133]. The spread is almost always exactly 2 and clusters on far/blocking/channel rows; purely local rows (`local-*`, `array-*`, map scalar rows) are perfectly stable. ROOT CAUSE, pinned by return-address attribution over 30 runs (16×281 with `spurious_wakes_absorbed=0`, 14×283 with `=1`, perfect correlation): a compiled coroutine's "pending" block heap-allocates exactly TWO boxes — the resume-payload union arm and the `{pc,payload}` state struct — before `rt_async_yield`, and frees and rebuilds them on every re-entry. When a wake token arrives inside `park_current`'s register-then-commit window the park is ABORTED and the task is requeued READY, so the coroutine re-polls, re-enters the same pending block, and rebuilds the pair: `allocation_count = floor + 2 × spurious_wakes_absorbed`. The counter itself was cleared of suspicion first, with an LD_PRELOAD interposer on malloc that independently counted 577 vs 579 real allocations for the 281 vs 283 runs. TWO CONSEQUENCES BEYOND THE GATE. (a) This is not a micro-optimisation: **128 of `far-immediate-scalar`'s 281 budgeted allocations are these per-park pairs** (64 parks × 2 in the inner coroutine, confirmed by site histogram), so removing them is a large allocation win on every async program, not just a determinism fix. (b) **The frozen budgets were captured from single runs**, so whichever interleaving happened at capture time became the contract: `channel-unbuffered-scalar`'s recorded 130 is a RETRY value while the quiet floor on the reference host is 131 (34 of 40 runs), which is why that row reads as "+1 over" rather than as a budget snapped to a rare schedule. WARNING for whoever fixes it: the obvious "give the coroutine an activation-lifetime slot" CANNOT be an alloca — the pending block ends in `unreachable` immediately after `rt_async_yield`, so the C frame is destroyed at suspension while the state pointer outlives it and the runtime may still call the abandoned-state drop through it. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-09: find the second contributor; do not hide it, and do not give the contract a tolerance.** The two remaining rows keep their stale budgets and the canonical benchmark stays red until the cause on the unbuffered-channel path is pinned the way the first one was — return-address attribution over ~30 runs plus an `LD_PRELOAD` malloc interposer to clear the counter of suspicion. A ±1 tolerance was considered and refused: it would blind the gate on exactly the two rows already known to be wrong. Scheduled as its own work item, not as part of the gate-integrity step. **WHAT IS ACTUALLY RED, MEASURED 2026-08-11 at b9f647c5 — this row described it wrongly and the correction is two separate faults, neither of which is the two unbuffered-channel rows named above.** (1) THE FROZEN TEST AND THE MANIFEST HAVE DIVERGED: `scripts/runtime_v2_carrier_bench_manifest_file_test.py`'s `nonzero_allocations` dict disagrees with `testdata/runtime-v2-carrier-bench.json` on 25 of 30 rows, and only 6 agree. The cause is this row's own partial close: the re-capture landed in `14dd0793` (2026-08-09) and the test's hardcoded expectations were last touched in `cbfe3a22` (2026-08-05), which is its ancestor - so the twenty-five re-captured budgets were never written into the assertion that freezes them. Examples: `blocking-scalar` manifest 213 against test 278, `far-jumbo-contention` 657 against 1042, `map-replace-composite` present in the manifest and absent from the test entirely. This is bookkeeping drift, not measurement noise, and it is repaired by updating the test from the manifest in one commit. (2) A LIVE OVERSHOOT ON A ROW THIS TEXT CALLS STABLE: the benchmark reports `array-grow-composite candidate warmup 0 batch 0: allocation_count=8, want exact structural budget 7`. Manifest and test AGREE on 7 for that row and the 2026-08-09 re-capture did not touch it, so this is not a stale expectation - it is eight allocations where seven are budgeted, on one of the `array-*` rows the text above calls "perfectly stable". Owner read it as a probable regression 2026-08-11. Pre-existing at `f2641713` and not introduced by Wave D step D0: the gate was run in a clean worktree at that commit and the two failures are byte-identical to the ones at `b9f647c5`. Whoever picks this up should date the overshoot first - the array rows have a budget older than the re-capture, so a bisect over the branch will say whether 8 is new or whether 7 was never right. **MEASURED 2026-08-20 AT `9221d45e`, AND `make runtime-v2-carrier-bench` HAD NEVER BEEN RUN IN THIS TREE BECAUSE IT CANNOT BE.** It refuses fail-closed on a dirty worktree, and agent tooling keeps `.claude/*` and two database files permanently modified here, so every local invocation aborts before measuring anything - the gate is not red, it is UNREACHABLE in place. Run in a dedicated clean worktree it executes and fails on substance: `channel-unbuffered-composite candidate warmup 0 batch 0: allocation_count=100, want exact structural budget 130`. Note the DIRECTION: the candidate allocates THIRTY FEWER than the exact budget, which an 'allocations went up' reading would miss entirely, and an exact budget refuses an undershoot precisely because a scenario that stopped doing part of its work looks like an improvement. This is a DIFFERENT failure from the one `runtime-v2-carrier-check` reports (`array-grow-composite allocation_count=8, want 7`): that target runs the bench's unit tests, `internal/carriergate` and the VM bench tests, not the benchmark itself, so the two have never been measuring the same thing. `channel-unbuffered-composite` is the unbuffered channel path with a composite payload, i.e. exactly what step D3 rewrites, so this number is D3's before-figure and must be re-measured after it - in a clean worktree, which is now the only honest way to run this gate at all. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-29 -- A BUDGET HAS TWO PHASES.** `initialization_budget` runs from the cold state to the end of the first working batch; `steady_structural_budget` runs over the batches after an explicitly NAMED warmup or setup, and only those. Averaging the expensive first batch into the rest is forbidden, and so is hiding it silently inside a warmup. Each window snapshots on a named quiet lane set and ends after the drain that window requires. This settles the two rows measured 2026-08-28 that carry no single number -- `channel-unbuffered-composite` and `-scalar` read 4 in warmup batch 0 and 3 in every measured pair: they are not budgets needing a re-pin, they are rows that have not said which of their two budgets each figure belongs to. Written at `docs/RUNTIME_V2.md`'s exact-allocation-budget paragraph. **2026-09-03 (Wave E, E5):** the four bench emitters that had no production caller -- `record_move`, `record_credit_stall`, `transport_acquire`, `transport_release` -- are wired: every physical move (`rt_value_move_init_detached`), every data-lane stall, and every resident acquire/release of `rt_resident_bytes.h` (envelope, padding, record, payload, sidecar). `bytes_moved`, `credit_stalls` and `peak_transport_bytes` are therefore no longer structural zeros; the manifest's numbers for them (`credit_stalls eq 128`, `peak_transport_bytes le 9472`) were written against the byte-credit model the 2026-08-29 ruling withdrew and are NOT re-pinned here -- the measured table is the owner's E5 decision, and the frozen six-metric contract is untouched until it lands. | Open | Epic 23b, async lowering; ruling taken, own work item | Two independent things, both settled. The compiler half: a pending block re-entered for the same state index stops freeing and rebuilding its two boxes — either by overwriting the existing pair in place, or by giving the coroutine one runtime-owned suspend-state allocation for the largest variant — so an aborted park costs zero allocations and the eleven rows become stable across repeated runs. The contract half: every budget is re-captured from at least three agreeing runs rather than one, and the manifest records how many samples backed each number, so a single rare interleaving can never again become a frozen requirement. Evidence is the same rows measured ≥5 times with a single distinct value each, plus `spurious_wakes_absorbed` observed non-zero during those runs to prove the aborted-park path was actually exercised and no longer costs anything. |
| RV2-DEBT-176 | Native `rt_fs_read_dir` builds an array of DirEntry POINTERS where the language expects an array of inline DirEntry VALUES. `DirEntry` is `{ name: string, path: string, file_type: FileType }` (`core/intrinsics.sg:32-36`), which the layout registry lays out inline at 24 bytes, and the intrinsic is declared to return `Erring<DirEntry[], FsError>` (`core/intrinsics.sg:53`). The runtime builds `void** elems`, stores an 8-byte `DirEntry*` per entry (`runtime/native/rt_fs.c:536-556`), copies that pointer buffer into the array's data block (`:565-575`) and hands back a header over it (`:578-584`). The consumer walks by the element type's own stride, so entry 1 is read 24 bytes in — inside the neighbouring pointer words — and the `name`/`path` handles projected out are arbitrary addresses. This is one level deeper than the Metadata payload defect fixed alongside it: an array element rather than a union payload. Partially closed 2026-08-09 — `TestLLVMParity/fs_dir_smoke` now passes — but `TestLLVMParity/walkdir_for_in` remains red, so the element-buffer half is not fully retired. WHY NO GATE CAUGHT IT: the only two rows that read a directory are both under `TestLLVMParity`, which `make check` hides behind its timeout skip. | Open | Runtime V2 native fs intrinsics | A directory listing round-trips on both backends with every entry's `name` and `path` readable, `walkdir_for_in` green, and Valgrind clean over a directory with more than one entry. |
| RV2-DEBT-178 | A captured reference into an async poll activation outlives the activation, and the VM refuses the read instead of running the program. The VM keeps a task's state as a composite — a reference to bytes in some activation's arena — rather than as a counted object. An arena carries a generation and retiring one bumps it, so every reference taken beforehand is refused afterwards (`internal/vm/storage_value.go`, `StorageRef.resolve`). Exactly one path defends against this: `execTermAsyncYield` calls `collectTaskStatePins` before `dropFrameLocals` (`internal/vm/vm_terminator.go:103`), which pins the arena and retains what the extent holds, and retirement is then refused while a pin is outstanding (`internal/vm/task_state_pins.go:256-271`). Something in the HTTP accept worker holds a reference the pin walk does not reach, so its activation retires underneath it and the next read kills the server mid-request: `panic VM1999: storage: stale reference to type#601`. This is the pin walk being incomplete rather than the generation check being wrong — the check is the thing that turned a use-after-free into a diagnosable panic, and it should stay. Note this row and RV2-DEBT-180 both surface through the same HTTP fixture from opposite backends: the VM refuses a stale read while the native side exits early on a clock that outran the wait. | Open | Runtime V2 VM task-state pinning | The pin walk reaches every reference a parked task can still read, so no activation retires while one is outstanding. Proven by the HTTP server fixture completing a request/response cycle on the VM, and by a targeted row that captures a reference into a poll activation across a yield and reads it after — passing rather than panicking. |
| RV2-DEBT-179 | A suspension frame does not record whether it owns what it holds, so which reclamation to use is a convention with two consumers and no check. Two emitter sites hand a frame away and need OPPOSITE treatment. The yield that finds its task already cancelled abandons a frame the pend block has just packed, so the frame is the only owner of the locals inside it and reclamation must WALK them (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_async.go:154` registers the walking release). The cancel terminator holds a payload that was read out at the top of the poll into locals that have since been released, so walking it would free those values a SECOND time (`emit_async.go:725-745` emits the shallow one inline). Nothing in the frame's type, its layout or its MIR distinguishes the two: the native backend does not act on `Field.MoveOut` at all — the identifier appears nowhere under `internal/backend/llvm` — so reading a payload out leaves the frame's bytes exactly as they were. Both function comments state the rule, which is why it is correct today. But a THIRD site that abandons a frame — a new crossing form, a scope-join variant, a blocking body — would pick a release by guessing, and guessing the walking one on a spent frame is a double free rather than a leak. No gate catches the difference: the strongest witness in the tree is one Valgrind row, and a leak is what a wrong choice produces only in the harmless direction. Found 2026-08-09 by the reviewer of the leak-lane patch, which is what made the convention visible. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D (async frame ownership) | The frame itself answers who owns its contents, rather than the caller remembering: either a spent frame is marked when its payload is read out, or the two releases become one that can tell. Proven by a negative row — a frame abandoned through the wrong path is REFUSED at build time or trapped at run time, not silently double-freed — plus the existing Valgrind witnesses staying green. |
| RV2-DEBT-180 | BLOCKER-CLASS: a native `timeout()` cannot protect a network wait, because the runtime advances its clock past the wait. `advance_time_to_next_timer` (`runtime/native/rt_worker_turn.c:250`, `:273`, `:288`) jumps the virtual clock straight to the next timer deadline whenever the executor samples idle, and a task parked on network readiness does not hold that sample back — so any `timeout(task, ms)` wrapping a net wait resolves Cancelled almost immediately, whatever `ms` says. Measured: the stdlib HTTP server built with `accept_timeout_ms = 60000` exits 0 after `elapsed=0.10` seconds — a sixty-second timeout served in a tenth of a second. Each accept worker wraps `net.accept` in `timeout(...)` (`stdlib/http/accept.sg:29`), takes the Cancelled arm (`:44-47`), stops, and once all four workers stop `serve` closes the listener and returns (`stdlib/http/server.sg:453-476`). `strace` confirms bind/listen succeed and each worker gets exactly one `accept(5) = -1 EAGAIN` before `exit_group(0)`. The control is decisive: with `accept_timeout_ms = 0` — the branch that arms no timer at all — the server stays up indefinitely and parks on accept correctly, so the park path is sound and only the clock is wrong. WHY THIS WENT UNSEEN FOR SO LONG: `TestMTCorrectnessHTTPServer` IS the gate, and it has been failing; the client reports `read: connection reset by peer` / `EOF`, which reads like a network flake, so the row was recorded as intermittent rather than as a server that exits before answering. A virtual clock that ignores a class of parked task is the same defect shape as a gate that is green without running: the timer is measuring something other than what the program is waiting for. | Open Ruling Р2 (2026-08-27) makes this row the one that closes it: monotonic wall time is the default everywhere and a virtual clock is reached only by an explicit selection at the run's boundary, so the idle jump this row measures is not a liberty to be bounded but a path to be removed. The VM side inverts a default in the same breath -- `surge run --real-time` opts INTO wall time today -- and the owner accepted the price: every VM golden and async test that currently finishes instantly starts paying real time, so the tagged suites re-time and CI lengthens. | Runtime V2 scheduler clock | A net wait protected by `timeout(task, ms)` survives at least `ms` of wall clock before it is cancelled: the idle sample does not advance past a deadline while any task is parked on external readiness. Proven by the HTTP server accepting and answering a request with a non-zero `accept_timeout_ms`, and by a direct row asserting that a timeout over a socket that never becomes readable fires no earlier than its deadline in real time. `TestMTCorrectnessHTTPServer` passing is a necessary consequence, not the evidence. |
| RV2-DEBT-181 | An intermittent witness race in `TestRuntimeV2FarTaskCallerCancel/cancel_after_publication_before_first_poll/shards_2`: the row fails with `missing far-body witness at shards=2` under full-suite load and passes 5/5 in isolation, so it is a scheduling race rather than a defect in what it measures. The row cancels a far task between publication and its first poll and then asserts the far body left a witness behind; at two shards the cancellation and the body's first turn can be ordered either way, and only one of those orders produces the witness the row demands. Either the row is asserting an ordering the runtime never promised — in which case it should assert what IS promised, that the body either runs completely or not at all — or the cancellation is genuinely racing the publication and the runtime owes the ordering, in which case the row is right and the runtime is wrong. Found 2026-08-09 while integrating the VM-suite triage: the row is one of two the earlier crash fixes had turned green, so its return under load is what makes it a race rather than a regression. **CLOSED 2026-09-03 (Wave E, E6), by the second branch of its own close condition, which had already landed:** `d06b40cb` rewrote the row to assert the guarantee the model makes -- publication promises the task exists and can be cancelled, not a first poll; the far-body witness at 2+ shards was reporting the host (81 red of 100 on 16 cores, 0 of 3 on 32) -- and kept the shards=1 direction as the program's own arrangement; the ordering question is pinned separately by `phase_sweep_publication_and_first_poll`. The ledger row was not updated when the rewrite landed; this entry is the bookkeeping, not new work. | Closed | Runtime V2 far-task lifecycle | The row passes 20 consecutive full-suite runs, or is rewritten to assert the guarantee the runtime actually makes and a separate row pins the ordering question. A row that passes in isolation and fails under load is not closed by running it in isolation. |
| RV2-DEBT-183 | The VM allocates a QUADRATIC number of heap objects in the number of await sites a coroutine has. Measured on the pinned compiler 2026-08-10, one run per point and stable across five repeats at n=2: n=0 -> 10, n=1 -> 17, n=2 -> 26, n=3 -> 37, n=4 -> 50, n=5 -> 65, which is `allocations = n^2 + 6n + 10`. The second difference is exactly 2, so each additional await site makes EVERY suspension cost two more allocations — the per-suspension cost is proportional to the number of state-machine variants rather than to the live locals a suspension actually carries, which is what a suspension should cost. The program measured is the `async_no_poll_alloc` fixture shape: `rt_heap_stats()`, an async block with n `checkpoint().await()` calls, `rt_heap_stats()` again. Nothing leaks and nothing is retained: live_blocks after the await is 8 at n=0, 2 and 4 alike, and the residue is the HeapStats struct plus the awaited `Task<int>` handle. Found while triaging the behavioural runners after they were made to execute (RV2-DEBT-173); it is the reason that fixture kept a stale budget of 15 for seven months without anyone noticing the real number had reached 26. This is a performance defect, not a correctness one, and it is deliberately NOT a reason to leave the gate red: the recorded budget is now 26, the true value, and it will change again when this is fixed — which is what a budget is for. | Open | Runtime V2, async lowering; own work item | The per-suspension allocation cost is independent of how many OTHER await sites the coroutine has, so allocations grow linearly in n rather than quadratically. Evidence is the same six measurements repeated with a first difference that does not grow, plus the `async_no_poll_alloc` budget re-recorded from at least three agreeing runs in the same commit. Related: RV2-DEBT-174 removed the per-ABORTED-park pair of allocations from this same lowering, which is a different quantity — that one was charged per spurious wake, this one is charged per suspension per site. |
| RV2-DEBT-187 | A native panic prints its message and nothing else, where the VM prints the message, the source span and a backtrace. `arrays_negative_index_panics` records five lines — `panic VM2105: array index -1 out of range for length 4`, `at testdata/.../arrays_negative_index_panics.sg:4:13`, `backtrace:`, and two frames — and native emits only the first. Same for `arrays_positive_index_panics` and `abi_arrays_views_panics`. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10: the native backend must carry both, and traceability generally.** The reason is a planned language feature, `surge exec <binary>`, which runs a compiled binary in isolation and captures traces; a panic that names neither its location nor its call chain gives that feature nothing to capture. The open question is therefore not WHETHER but what it costs at run time — what has to be retained, and whether the cost is paid always or only under a flag. **SPLIT 2026-08-10 by measurement, and the halves cost nothing alike.** The SPAN is already available at the emit site: `mir.Instr` carries no span, and the VM does not read one — `setSpanForInstr` (`internal/vm/vm_util.go:224`) recovers a location from the DECLARATION span of the instruction's destination local (`mir.Local.Span`, `internal/mir/types.go:69`), and the LLVM `funcEmitter` holds the same `*mir.Func` and the same `*mir.Instr`. It simply never reads it: `.Span` appears zero times in `internal/backend/llvm`. The emitted panic call already sits in a cold block terminated by `unreachable`, so widening it with a constant span pointer costs nothing on the live path. What is missing is only that `EmitModule` is not handed a `*source.FileSet`, so it cannot render `path:line:col`, and the `rt_panic*` family takes a message and nothing else. The BACKTRACE has no foundation: user functions are emitted as `fn.<ID>`, no `!dbg` metadata is produced, and the IR is compiled without `-g`, so the binary carries DWARF for the C runtime only. One fixture, `abi_arrays_views_panics`, panics from inside `rt_array.c` where no caller context exists at all — only a maintained call chain can reach it — and it additionally prints VM3202 where the VM records VM1003, which is a separate small correction. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10: the static span ships as its own change, ahead of and independent of any decision about the call chain, and the chain's design is discussed separately.** **STATIC HALF DONE 2026-08-10.** All three fixtures now print the `at <path>:<line>:<col>` line, byte-identical to the VM's, and the rendering lives once - the VM's `formatSpan` moved to `source.FormatSpan` and both backends call it. Two things were learned doing it and are worth keeping. `rt_panic` was deliberately NOT widened: giving it a span made native print an `at core/intrinsics.sg:...` line for an explicit `panic("msg")` that the VM never prints, so the two reporters that DO carry a location are the bounds and numeric ones, and a NULL span prints no line at all. And the premise that `abi_arrays_views_panics` could not be closed this way was WRONG - it does not panic from inside `rt_array.c`, it panics from an emitter site and its `at` line matches now like the others. WHAT REMAINS on this row is the backtrace, plus one thing found on the way and deliberately not fixed here because it belongs with the same work: native reports `VM3202` where the VM records `VM1003` for `array view is not resizable`, because `array_panic` in `runtime/native/rt_array.c` routes EVERY array-side panic through `rt_panic_numeric`, whose prefix is hardcoded to the numeric code. That is about twenty call sites with different messages, and the VM does not answer them all with one code either - the capacity one really is a numeric conversion - so closing it is a message-to-code mapping, not a one-line change. **MEASURED 2026-08-11 by a full `make behaviour-check-all` at `5ebb0cec`, and the result narrows this row to ONE thing.** Four fixtures now fail on the native lane and all four fail identically: `arrays_negative_index_panics`, `arrays_positive_index_panics`, `abi_arrays_views_panics` and `vm_int8_overflow_add` each produce the recorded message, the recorded `VM<code>` and the recorded `at <path>:<line>:<col>` line BYTE FOR BYTE, and stop there — the two `backtrace:` lines are the entire remaining difference. The code half of this row is therefore closed by `ec46ba40` for every condition any fixture reaches: `VM1003` for the array view, `VM2105` for both index panics and `VM1101` for the arithmetic overflow all match. Those four fixtures are the whole of what stands between the native lane and green apart from RV2-DEBT-186's cycle, so this row and that one are now the only two holding it. The ~20 remaining `array_panic` call sites still route through the numeric reporter and still need their per-site comparison, but no recorded fixture reaches any of them, which means the corpus cannot see that work and it must not be credited to the corpus going green. **CORRECTION 2026-08-11 to what was written here and in `904f7a28`'s message about `div_by_zero_panics`.** That commit said native prints no `at` line for `return 1 / 0` because the statement writes no local and the emitter's span cursor is therefore empty. That is WRONG, and the wrong reading would have sent someone to fix the cursor. Measured side by side: `let c: int64 = a / b` with a zero divisor prints the SAME `at` line on both backends, byte for byte, because a fixed-width divide is raised by the EMITTER and carries its span. The same program at `int` prints no location on native — because `int` is arbitrary-precision, the divide runs in the bignum C runtime, and every C-runtime reporter passes a NULL span. So the cursor is correct everywhere the emitter raises a panic, and this fixture is simply an instance of the third gap already listed above rather than a fourth one. It also means the gap and the backtrace have ONE fix between them: an unwind that reaches the innermost SURGE frame gives the location a runtime-raised panic cannot supply for itself. **CENSUS DONE 2026-08-11, and it splits the remaining work in two — one half is not about codes at all.** On CODE, every EMITTER-raised panic now agrees with the VM; the remaining code disagreements are in the C runtime, where `array_panic` binds `VM3202` for conditions the VM answers differently: `rt_array_append_raw_bytes` and `rt_byte_array_drop_prefix` still say VM3202 for the view-resize condition the emitter's own copy already fixed to VM1003, so the SAME message printed a different code depending on which of three call paths reached it, and two byte-array bounds failures disagree on MESSAGE as well as code (the VM renders them through `errorBuilder.outOfBounds` as "index N out of bounds for length L" under VM1004). On MESSAGE, the claim that the emitter is finished is FALSE, and this was caught by running programs rather than reading: at least three emitter operand-range guards print a different sentence than the VM under the same VM3202 — `byte range start out of range` vs `byte array append start out of range`, `byte range length out of range` vs `byte array append length out of range`, `byte drop count out of range` vs `byte array drop prefix count out of range`. Two more look the same by inspection and were not measured: `fs seek offset/whence out of range` vs the VM's `seek offset/whence out of range`. **A THIRD GAP, and it is the one no fixture can see:** every C-runtime reporter call passes a NULL span, so a panic raised from inside the runtime prints no `at <file>:<line>:<col>` line at all, while an emitter-raised panic does. Fifteen of the runtime's sites are null-pointer or allocation guards that should NOT be given invented codes. Method note worth keeping: a census taken against a PATCHED tree reports patched line numbers; the first pass here cited every `rt_array.c` coordinate post-fix, off by a uniform -32 before base line 381 and -36 after, and the verification had to re-derive all of them against `5ebb0cec`. State which tree a line number belongs to, or it is not a fact about the base. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-11 on the backtrace: mechanism B — recover the frames at panic time, do not maintain them as the program runs.** The reason given is that native performance is to be preserved as far as possible, and B is the only option that costs nothing while nothing is panicking. `surge exec` is NOT to be implemented now; the trace is wanted on its own merits, as the thing future defects will be debugged with. **A MEASUREMENT CORRECTED THE MECHANISM WITHIN B, and in the same direction.** The earlier note here reasoned about frame pointers and said they survive because the IR is compiled without `-O`. They do not: `clang -c -x ir` emits `fn.3` opening on `sub $0x18,%rsp` with no `push %rbp` at all. What the emitted object DOES carry is `.eh_frame` (`X86_64_UNWIND`), which LLVM produces unasked. So the walk is a CFI unwind, not a frame-pointer chase, and that is strictly better on every axis the ruling cares about: still zero cost in the steady state, no `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` and therefore no register given up, and CFI keeps working under optimisation where a frame-pointer chain would not. **What the trace has to reproduce, measured rather than assumed.** A frame is the SOURCE function name plus the location IN that frame, which for every frame but the innermost is the call site: `0: level3 at deep.sg:2:12 / 1: level2 at deep.sg:6:12 / ... / 4: __surge_start at <no-span>`. The surprise is what the VM does inside a spawned task: it prints `0: boom / 1: inner$poll` and STOPS — no `main`, no `__surge_start`. So the VM's contract is the frames physically on its stack, not the logical `await` chain, and a machine unwind reproduces that shape naturally by stopping where Surge-emitted code ends. That removed what looked like the hardest constraint on B. Three things are still missing on the native side and only one is hard: the function's source name (functions are emitted as `fn.<ID>`; `mir.Func.Name` has the real one and is simply not written out), the caller's call-site location (the span cursor knows it at emit time and nothing records it), and a way to know which frames are live — which is what the unwind answers. **BUILT 2026-08-11, and all eight fixtures match the VM byte for byte.** The emitter drops a LABEL and two words of data in a section of its own at each function entry (`surge_fn_map`: address to function name) and at each CHANGE of source location (`surge_line_map`: address to `file:line:col`), both self-relative so they need no relocation in a PIE — an absolute address produced a `DT_TEXTREL`, which is how that detail was settled. The runtime walks with `_Unwind_Backtrace` over the `.eh_frame` LLVM already emits and answers each address from those two tables by linear scan; sorting would need an allocation on a path that is already ending the process, and a scan that cannot fail is worth more there than a search that can. **Cost, measured rather than argued: zero instructions.** Disassembly of a marked function shows nothing between the `call` and the instruction after it. The tables are proportional to the SOURCE, not to the generated code: a five-function program carries 40 bytes of function map and 64 bytes of line map against a 250 KB `.text`. No frame pointer is needed and none is given up. **Two bounds had to be added and both were found by the output being wrong, not by reasoning.** The function map records only where functions BEGIN, so every address in the C runtime — which links after the emitted object and therefore sits above it — resolved to the last Surge function; three frames of the runtime's own panic path printed under a Surge name until an empty `__surge_trace_text_end` function gave the module's code an upper bound. And `__surge_poll_call` is emitted glue rather than a function the program wrote, so it printed as whatever function preceded it; it now carries a sentinel row that STOPS the walk, which puts the end of a native trace exactly where the VM's ends — at a task's poll entry. **The runtime-raised location gap closed with it, as one report rather than two.** `at` and `backtrace:` are written by one function now, and when the caller has no span of its own the innermost Surge frame supplies one: that is what gives a bignum divide-by-zero the line the emitter never knew, and it is why `div_by_zero_panics` — which was missing its `at` line as well as its frames — matches now. **A fixture had to be added for a reason worth keeping.** All eight fixtures that were red panicked directly in `main` or one call down, so a two-frame implementation that never walked a stack would have passed every one of them. `arrays_index_panic_call_chain` panics four calls deep and `t30_panic_in_task_backtrace` panics inside a spawned task, so the gate can now tell a real walk from a shape that happens to fit. NOT done and deliberately so: Windows, by owner ruling — the walk is `_Unwind_Backtrace`, and the Windows equivalent is a different API for a platform that is not being built now. And inlining will erase frames once optimisation is turned on, since the IR is compiled with no `-O` today; CFI survives optimisation but inlined frames need inline records to come back. **THE C-RUNTIME CODE MAPPING IS LANDED 2026-08-11; the message half and the backtrace are what remain.** `array_panic` keeps its `VM3202` and now says in a comment that the binding is what a failed numeric CONVERSION is, so a condition that is something else names its own code. Two do: the view-resize guard in `rt_array_append_raw_bytes` and in `rt_byte_array_drop_prefix` now raise `VM1003` through `rt_panic_code`, matching the emitter's own copy and the VM's `arrayOwnedFromValue`; and the two byte-array bounds failures route through `rt_panic_bounds(kind=0, ...)`, which is what makes the TEXT the VM's text as well as the code — the VM renders them through `errorBuilder.outOfBounds` as `index N out of bounds for length L` under VM1004, and hand-writing the code alone would have left the old sentence printed under a new number, which is worse than the disagreement it replaced. **NO FIXTURE CAN COVER THIS, and that is a finding rather than an omission.** Every one of these conditions is unreachable from Surge source: the stdlib guards `rt_byte_array_append_range` and `rt_byte_array_drop_prefix` with `is_valid_range` before calling them, and sema refuses a resize of a view outright — `view.clear_keep_capacity()` fails at compile time with `array view is not resizable; clear_keep_capacity requires an owned array` on both backends. So these are defence-in-depth reporters, correct now and invisible to the corpus, and nothing about them may be credited to the lane going green. Verified as not a regression the only way available: the three array panic fixtures that DO run differ from their recorded answers by exactly the backtrace, before and after. **THE MESSAGE HALF IS CLOSED 2026-08-11, and the claim just above it that these conditions are unreachable from Surge source is WRONG.** The stdlib does guard `rt_byte_array_append_range` and `rt_byte_array_drop_prefix` with `is_valid_range`, but the intrinsics are declared `pub` in `core/intrinsics.sg` and a program may call them directly, which is exactly what the corpus now does. Six operand-range guards were reported differently by the two backends under the same VM3202, all six measured by running the same program on both lanes: `byte range start` / `byte range length` / `byte drop count out of range` against the VM's `byte array append start` / `byte array append length` / `byte array drop prefix count out of range`; `fs seek offset` / `fs seek whence out of range` against the VM's `seek offset` / `seek whence out of range` (these two were the pair this row listed as suspected-but-unmeasured, and both were real). The sixth was not suspected at all and only running found it: `emitNetWriteBytes` guards the OFFSET operand of `rt_net_write_bytes` and printed the LENGTH sentence, so native named the wrong argument where the VM says `net write offset out of range`. A SEVENTH came from measurement rather than inspection and is not a reword: `rt_array_append_raw_bytes` had no operand guard on the native side at all, so a length above `INT64_MAX` was carried into `array_grow_cap` and reported as `array capacity out of range` — a capacity nobody asked for — where the VM refuses the argument up front with `array append length out of range`. The guard now sits at the top of `rt_array_append_raw_bytes` in `runtime/native/rt_array.c`, ahead of the null and view checks, because that is where it sits in the VM: it is a fact about the argument, not about the array. The VM was treated as the reference throughout and nothing on the VM side was touched. Codes did not move: every one of these is VM3202 on both sides before and after. Six fixtures under `testdata/golden/vm_intrinsics/` now hold all of it — `byte_array_append_start_panics`, `byte_array_append_length_panics`, `byte_array_drop_prefix_panics`, `array_append_raw_bytes_length_panics`, `fs_seek_offset_panics`, `fs_seek_whence_panics` — and they were confirmed live rather than assumed: reverting one message turned the `/llvm` lane red with the exact text diff, and all twelve cases (six fixtures x two backends) pass with the fix in. STILL not covered by any fixture and still true: the `net write offset` site, because reaching it needs a live TCP connection, and the C-runtime sites this row already lists as defence-in-depth. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend; the code mapping and the message mapping are settled for everything a fixture reaches, the backtrace design is the open owner question | A native panic prints the same message, span and backtrace the VM prints, and the FOUR fixtures that record one — `arrays_negative_index_panics`, `arrays_positive_index_panics`, `abi_arrays_views_panics` and `vm_int8_overflow_add` — pass on `--backend=llvm` unchanged. The run-time cost of retaining a call chain is measured and recorded BEFORE the mechanism is chosen, since that cost is the only part still in question; the owner has already ruled that the backtrace itself is required. Separately and not covered by any fixture: every remaining `array_panic` site in `runtime/native/rt_array.c` names the code the VM names for the same condition, established per site rather than by matching message text. |
| RV2-DEBT-188 | Eight behavioural fixtures interleave differently on the two backends, and NOBODY HAS DECIDED whether that is a defect or a licence. `t04_fairness` records `L1 R1 L2 R2 L3 R3` and native produces `L1 R1 L2 L3 R2 R3`; the values are identical and only the order differs. The same for `async_fairness`, `async_joiners`, `vm_async_j7_unbuffered`, `t08_joiners`, `t10a_timeout_cancel`, `t15_fairness_round_robin` and `t16_no_starvation_chatty`. This is the question `docs/RUNTIME_V2.md` (Refactor Policy) already told the project to answer BEFORE changing scheduling: "document which scheduler properties are language contracts and which are current implementation artifacts. The VM backend and golden tests may implicitly depend on FIFO ordering that the native runtime should not promise forever." Eight fixtures now depend on exactly that, and until the question is answered it cannot be said whether native is wrong or the fixtures over-specify. Owner has flagged this as needing discussion. **MEASURED 2026-08-10, and the row as filed conflates two things.** All eight fixtures match their recorded `.out` byte-for-byte on `--backend=llvm` at `SURGE_THREADS=1` — 20 runs each, zero failures — and diverge run-to-run at the default worker count, which is `max(2, nproc)` (32 on the measuring machine). So this is not a backend disagreement about ordering; it is the corpus running the native lane in a configuration nothing promises ordering for. `internal/vm/test_helpers_test.go` already has `envForParity`, which appends `SURGE_THREADS=1` and is used by every LLVM parity suite; the behavioural corpus is the one harness that does not use it. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10, in two halves.** (1) Backend PARITY is compared single-threaded, because the VM is single-threaded and a multi-worker interleaving is not a backend difference. (2) That is NOT the whole test story: the native binary IS multithreaded, and the new native runtime must be exercised multithreaded as well, because a defect that only appears once work is split across workers is invisible to a single-worker lane. So the resolution is TWO lanes, not a narrowing to one. Also ruled: `docs/CONCURRENCY.md` states the language-level async rules of the CURRENT model and pairs with `docs/RUNTIME.md`, which describes the runtime that exists today; `docs/RUNTIME_V2.md` is a separate target document and says so in its own first line ("target architecture, not an implementation contract"). So CONCURRENCY.md's F1/F2/F3 are today's contract, not V2's, and they may not survive V2 unchanged. Any edit to any of the three is agreed with the owner first rather than made in passing. **CONFIRMED 2026-08-11 by a full `make behaviour-check-all` at `5ebb0cec`: all eight fixtures pass on both backends, and no scheduler-ordering failure appears anywhere in the corpus.** So the parity half of this row is settled and the ordering difference it was filed on is gone as a corpus failure. What the row is still open on is the part the ruling created rather than the part it answered: the MT lane asserts only termination, exit code, no signal and no allocator output, and the stronger per-fixture properties ("these lines in any order", "every spawned task is observed") are undesigned. Until they exist the MT lane can see a hang — it found RV2-DEBT-190 on its first run — and cannot see a wrong interleaving that still terminates. Writing down which ordering properties are contracts is therefore not bookkeeping left over from a closed question; it is what the MT lane needs before it can assert anything about order at all. | Open | Language contract; owner ruling taken 2026-08-10 (two lanes); the MT lane's assertions still to be designed | The scheduler ordering properties that ARE language contracts are written down, in RUNTIME_V2.md or the spec, and the eight fixtures either pass on both backends or record only the properties the contract promises. Fixing native to match the VM without answering the question first is explicitly NOT the close condition — it would freeze an artifact into a promise. |
| RV2-DEBT-193 | `wake_key_all_with_policy` (`runtime/native/rt_task_park.c`) is the THIRD collect-then-wake site of the shape RV2-DEBT-190 closed, and it is not covered by the counter that closed the other two. It pops every waiter matching a key out of the store under the shard lock, releases the lock, and only then wakes each one — deliberately, and for the reason its own comment gives: "waking under a held store lock would self-deadlock the same-shard case (same mutex)". For the length of that gap a collected task is in no waiter store, no ready queue and no `running_count`, so `rt_sched_idle_sample_locked` reads the executor as idle while the batch sits in a local array. That is the same predicate `advance_time_to_next_timer` moves the virtual clock on, so a sample landing there can jump time past a deadline that is already due — which is exactly the failure RV2-DEBT-190 measured at the sleep site. Filed 2026-08-11 while closing that row. **This is a reading, NOT a measurement**, and the distinction matters because the sleep site's own residual was named by reading too and then took a deterministic proof to confirm: no program has been observed to hang through this path, and a 7000-run sweep of the select fixture at 8 and 16 workers found nothing after the sleep-site fix. Two things make it worth a row anyway. The mechanism is identical rather than analogous, down to the same justification for the same lock release. And the machinery it needs already exists — `rt_sched_publishing_begin_locked` / `rt_sched_publishing_end` and the `publishing_count` field were built for the sleep site and are general. The JOIN branch is the awkward part: it collects through `rt_waiter_collect_join_waiters`, which takes the route lock itself, so the claim has to be made inside that helper rather than at the call site. | Open | Runtime V2 native scheduler; collect-then-wake invariant | The claim is made in the same critical section that removes the batch, on both branches of `wake_key_all_with_policy` including the JOIN collector, and released after the wakes — and a sync point at the gap proves it the way `SP_SLEEP_FIRED_BEFORE_WAKE` does, with a negative control that observes the executor reporting itself idle when the claim is inert. A rate sweep is not evidence here: nothing has been seen to hang through this path, so a green sweep would only restate that. |
| RV2-DEBT-194 | `--release` optimises nothing. It selects an output directory and that is all: `cmd/surge/build.go:125-127` turns the flag into `profile = "release"`, which reaches `CompileRequest.Profile` and decides `target/release/` instead of `target/debug/`. No compile flag anywhere depends on it. `compileLLVMIR` (`internal/buildpipeline/build.go:264`) runs `clang -c -x ir out.ll -o out.o` with no `-O` at all, in both profiles, so a release binary is an unoptimised one under a different path. The C runtime is compiled with `-g` in both. Found 2026-08-11 while answering whether a `--release` build still carries the panic backtrace — it does, and the reason it does is partly this. **The owner expected the flag to set optimisation flags**, which is the real cost of the gap: a release build is what a user measures, benchmarks and ships, and every performance number taken through it so far describes `-O0` code. Note what that means for numbers already recorded elsewhere in this ledger: the carrier bench budgets, the crossing costs and the allocation probes were all measured on unoptimised native code, so they are internally comparable but are not statements about what this compiler can produce. | Open | Toolchain; build profiles | `--release` passes an optimisation level to the IR compile and the C runtime build, the two profiles differ by more than a directory, and the difference is stated where a user can find it. Two things to settle in the same change rather than after it: whether `-g` stays on the release C runtime, and that turning optimisation on will erase inlined frames from the panic backtrace (CFI survives; inlined frames need inline records), so RV2-DEBT-187's trace has to be re-measured under `-O` rather than assumed to carry over. |
| RV2-DEBT-195 | An ARBITRARY-PRECISION shift bounds its count differently on the two backends, so the same program gets a different answer, a different panic, or no panic at all. Native bounds the count by C's `INT_MAX` (`runtime/native/rt_bignum_uint_div.c:702`); the VM bounds it by Go's `int`, which is 64-bit here (`internal/vm/eval_ops.go:278`). Every count in `(2^31-1, 2^63-1]` therefore lands in the gap. Measured independently at `3d307109`, before any of the message work, so this is pre-existing and not a consequence of it: `let a: int = 12345; let b: int = 100000000000; a >> b` prints `0` on the VM and panics `VM1101: integer overflow` on native; `let a: int = 1; a << b` with the same count panics `VM3201: numeric size limit exceeded` on the VM and `VM1101: integer overflow` on native. So one shape is a WRONG ANSWER (the VM answers, native refuses) and the other is a DISAGREEMENT ABOUT WHICH REFUSAL (both refuse, under different codes with different meanings — a size limit is not an overflow). `int` and `uint` behave identically; a first census claimed the `uint` path was correct and running it disproved that, which is the usual reason to run rather than read. **This is NOT the same defect as RV2-DEBT-192**, which was about FIXED-WIDTH shifts and is closed: the fixed-width guard refuses a count outside `[0, width)` on both backends and agrees. Here the type has no width, and the disagreement is about how large a count the implementation will entertain before it gives up. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend; arbitrary-precision numerics | The two backends agree for every count, including the whole `(2^31-1, 2^63-1]` band — same answer where there is an answer, same code where there is a refusal. Decide the CONTRACT first rather than making one side match the other by inspection: a count above what any allocation could satisfy is a size limit rather than an overflow, and the VM already says so for `<<` while saying `0` for `>>`, so the question is what the language promises for a shift nobody can perform. Fixtures land with it — no corpus fixture reaches this band today, which is why it survived. |
| RV2-DEBT-196 | A panic message BUILT BY FORMAT STRING must have every one of its expansions pre-registered by hand, and nothing checks that the two lists agree. `emitFarTaskLifecycleErrorBlocks` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_crossing_far_task.go:194-229`) produces seven messages as `fmt.Sprintf("far Task.%s ...", method)`; `EmitModule` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit.go:258-271`) registers the expansions for exactly two methods, `await` and `cancel`, as fourteen literal strings. A third `far Task` method compiles the COMPILER cleanly and then fails at build time for any program that uses it: `emitBytesConst` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_term.go:476-479`) returns `missing string const %q` on a lookup miss, and that error travels up out of `EmitModule`, so the whole compile dies rather than the one call site degrading. Found 2026-08-11 while unifying panic wording, by reading rather than by hitting it — and hit incidentally in the same session, where reverting one message string to test a gate produced a CLI usage dump instead of the expected text diff, because the emitter refused to run at all until its registration was reverted too. The coupling is invisible from either side: nothing in the emitter says "these strings must exist", and nothing in the registration list says which format string it is expanding. Note this is NOT the same as a message parity defect — the two backends would agree perfectly; the compiler simply stops working. | Open | Runtime V2 native backend; emitter string-constant registration | Either the registration is derived from the same source the message is (one list, expanded once, so a new method cannot be half-added), or a test enumerates the format strings and their permitted expansions and fails when a call site can produce a string the module never registered. Prove it the way this repository proves a gate: add a third method, watch the check go red, remove it. A fixture cannot cover this — the failure is a compile error in a program nobody has written yet, which is exactly why it needs a static check rather than a corpus entry. |
| RV2-DEBT-197 | The two backends BOUND A SHIFT COUNT AT DIFFERENT WIDTHS, so a shift by a count between 2^31 and 2^63 gets a value on the VM and a panic on native. The VM's `shiftCountFromBigUint` (`internal/vm/eval_ops.go`) bounds the count by Go's `int`, which is 64-bit here; the native `shift_count_from_biguint` (`runtime/native/rt_bignum_uint_div.c`) bounds it by C's `int`, which is `INT_MAX`. Measured 2026-08-11 on 106e3bbe with `SURGE_STDLIB=$(pwd)`: `let a: int = 12345; let b: int = 100000000000; print((a >> b) to string);` prints `0` and exits 0 on the VM, and exits 1 with `panic VM1101: integer overflow` on native. This is a WRONG ANSWER, not a wording difference - one backend completes the program. Two further shapes were measured alongside it: `0 << 100000000000` has the same split, and `1 << 100000000000` panics on both but under different codes (`VM3201 numeric size limit exceeded` on the VM, which accepts the count and then hits the shared 1,000,000-limb cap, against `VM1101 integer overflow` on native, which never gets that far). `uint` behaves identically to `int`, so this is the count check and not a bigint/biguint asymmetry. A negative count (`1 << -1`) already agrees on both. NO FIXTURE IS RECORDED FOR THIS: the behavioural lane is green and a fixture for a live divergence would land red, so it belongs with the fix. Distinct from RV2-DEBT-192, which was about fixed-width shifts wrapping; every fixed-width shift and negation edge added to `vm_numbers` on 2026-08-11 agrees on both backends. | Open | Runtime V2 bignum shift API; VM/native parity | `bu_shl`/`bu_shr`/`bi_shl`/`bi_shr` take a 64-bit count, or the native count check keeps its narrower bound and still answers zero (or the limb cap) for the counts above it as the VM does; and a fixture in `testdata/golden/vm_numbers` records all three measured shapes on both backends. |
| RV2-DEBT-198 | A COMPOSITE THAT HOLDS A CARRIER is droppable to sema and receives a drop-glue body that reclaims nothing, and neither leg of the drop-glue agreement gate can see it. `type Box = { ch: Channel<int> }` classifies `CarrierDroppable` true - it holds a channel, which requires reclamation - while `typeOwnsHeap` answers false and `emitDropGlueBody`'s struct arm walks the fields into `emitDropHandle`, which has no case for a channel, a task or a map. The emitted body is `entry -> br ret -> ret void`: FLAG_DROPPABLE set, `drop_in_place` non-null, the runtime's "droppable implies a drop function" invariant satisfied, and the value leaked. The gate cannot record the shape either: `leafFamilyOf` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue_agreement_test.go`) answers the empty string for any composite and `isUnreclaimedFamily("")` is false, so such a row can be neither compared by the first leg nor excused by the second - it is unrepresentable rather than merely absent. Distinct from the `Channel<int>` and `far Channel<int>` rows, which ARE recorded excuses on the leaf itself; this is the leaf one level down. Found 2026-08-11 during Wave D step D0 by adversarial review of the gate D0.3 built, and it is PRE-EXISTING - the empty body predates that gate, which is why the gate was written. Load-bearing for Wave D because a carrier descriptor is itself a synthesized struct holding a channel, and the async payload/state types the compiler synthesizes today (`__AsyncPayload$f`, `__AsyncState$f`) already have this shape. **THE CHANNEL HALF CLOSED WITH D3b C1 (2026-08-26).** `typeOwnsHeap` answers YES for a channel and `emitDropHandle` reaches `rt_channel_handle_drop`, widened together, so `@copy type Gate = { ch: Channel<int> }` gets a struct arm that walks into a real release; `fieldDropIsExclusive` admits the channel because the clone glue now retains it per copy. The gate can say so: `leafFamilyOf` answers `channel handle` for the leaf (and `task handle` / `map` / `range` for the other runtime objects, each matched to its helper in `leafFamilyByHelper`, where they had been one unmatchable "opaque runtime resource"), the first leg compares `Gate` by the families of the leaves it holds, and the second leg carries `Gate` as an ordinary row that fails on an empty body. What remains of this row is the FAR half: a `far Channel<T>` stored as a field is still not released by any glue (Wave E), and `leafFarLease` is the one family left in `unreclaimedFamilies`. **THE FAR HALF CLOSED WITH WAVE E STEP E3 (2026-09-03).** `typeOwnsHeap` answers YES for a far channel handle (it holds a lease on the owner shard's registry entry) and `emitDropHandle` reaches `rt_far_channel_handle_drop` -- the same release a far LOCAL's scope-exit drop has always made -- widened together, so `type Holder = { ch: far Channel<int> }` gets a struct arm that walks into a real release; `fieldDropIsExclusive` admits it because a far handle is move-only. The gate says so: `unreclaimedFamilies` is empty (declared, so a family losing its release goes back in with a recorded excuse), `far Channel<int>` and `FarGate` are ordinary rows in both legs, and `leafFamilyByHelper` maps the release to `far lease`. Witness: `TestRuntimeV2FarHandleFieldDropReleasesTheLease` -- two holders, one dropped at the end of the frame that built it, one moved into a callee that drops it at its own scope exit; valgrind strict zero. Rule 13: the walk's far arm removed (`typeOwnsHeap` NO for a far channel) leaves the program at exit 0 and valgrind at `definitely lost: 48 bytes in 2 blocks` -- the two caller-side tokens, whose registry entries went unreleased with them. | Closed 2026-09-03 (channel half 2026-08-26, far half E3) | Epic 23b Wave E (E3) | The owner migration that makes a held carrier reclaimable closes it: once `emitDropHandle` has a release for the carrier families, the composite field walk reaches it and the body stops being empty. The gate then needs `leafFamilyOf` to answer for a composite by the families of the leaves it holds, so the row becomes comparable rather than unrepresentable, and this row retires with a recorded composite-holding-a-carrier row in the second leg. |
| RV2-DEBT-200 | `make` SILENTLY SUCCEEDS ON EVERY UNKNOWN TARGET, so a gate that does not exist reports success rather than absence. `Makefile` ends with a catch-all `%:` whose recipe is `@:`, added to stop `make run foo bar` from trying to build the command arguments as targets. The side effect is total: `make this-target-does-not-exist-at-all` exits 0 with no output, and so does any typo in a gate name. Measured 2026-08-11. This is the mechanism that let RV2-DEBT-199's sibling blocker survive - `runtime-v2-carrier-sanitizer-check` was named mandatory in three documents and had no target, so anyone who ran it saw exit 0 and would reasonably conclude the gate passed. Every gate invocation in this repository, in CI or by hand, is therefore unable to distinguish "green" from "not a target".  **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at HEAD.** The catch-all is now defined only when `run` is the FIRST goal - `ifeq ($(firstword $(MAKECMDGOALS)),run)` - which keeps the argument-swallowing exactly where it was needed and gives every other unknown name back to make. `run` is the only goal in the file that reads `MAKECMDGOALS`, so the narrowing is total rather than a heuristic. EVIDENCE: `make this-target-does-not-exist-at-all` now exits 2 with `No rule to make target 'this-target-does-not-exist-at-all'`, naming what it could not find; `make runtime-v2-heap-chek` - one character from a real gate, which is the shape that actually happens - exits 2 as well; a real gate still resolves; and `make run version` prints `surge 0.1.0` with exit 0, BYTE-IDENTICALLY to the old Makefile, which is the comparison rather than an assertion that it looks right. Pinned by `internal/gatecheck/unknown_make_target_test.go`, shown failing on revert on exactly the two subtests that matter. It sits beside `documented_make_targets_test.go` on purpose: that one asserts a target a document PROMISES exists, this one asserts make can still say NO, and a promise checker is worth little if the thing it checks against answers yes to everything. CAVEAT worth stating: make parses a leading `--flag` after `run` as one of ITS OWN options and prints its help. That is unchanged, predates this row, and is why the pinning test passes word arguments only. **THE FIX BROKE A PROBE BUILT TO CATCH THIS VERY CLASS, and the reason is worth carrying:** `HasExplicitMakeTarget` (`internal/carriergate/compare.go`) dumps make's database by asking for a goal named `:` that deliberately does not exist, and treated a non-zero exit as failure. While the catch-all was unconditional, `:` "succeeded" and the probe read exit 0; giving make back the ability to refuse it - the entire point of this row - made the probe error on the repository's own Makefile. It was reading the exit code of a question it was not asking: make prints the whole database to stdout BEFORE failing on the goal. Fixed by tolerating the exit and validating the DUMP instead. A second false answer surfaced while pinning that: a MISSING makefile also produces a structurally complete database (an empty `# Files` section plus an error on stderr), so the probe would have reported "no such target" for every target of a file it never read - a false NEGATIVE in a gate built to catch false positives. The makefile is now stat'd before make is asked, and `TestExplicitMakeTargetProbeErrorsWhenMakeCannotAnswer` pins it. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b gate integrity | The catch-all is narrowed so it cannot swallow a target-shaped name: restrict it to the argument positions it exists for (the `run` goal's trailing words) rather than matching every goal, or make the fallback fail loudly for any goal that is not a known argument of a known target. Evidence is `make <nonexistent>` exiting non-zero with a named message while `make run foo bar` still works, plus the documented-target scan in `internal/gatecheck` continuing to pass. |
| RV2-DEBT-201 | A PARKED CHANNEL ENTRY SURVIVES AN ABORTED PARK. `park_current`'s first abort branch (`runtime/native/rt_task_park.c`) calls `park_requeue_locked`, which sets `task->park_key = waker_none()` and `park_prepared = 0` and requeues the task - but does NOT retire the entry `channel_park_prepare_locked` (`rt_channel_lane.h`) already appended to the channel owner's store. The task then reaches `mark_done` with nothing to remove, unpins, and the channel can be freed with that entry still live. Found 2026-08-11 by adversarial review of the RV2-DEBT-199 reproduction, and driven by an ordinary-source shape the tree already instruments: `cancel_task`'s own comment describes exactly this window and names `SP_CANCEL_BEFORE_WAKE` against `SP_PARK_BEFORE_WAITING` as its deterministic driver, so an anchored `on` body whose first operation is a channel receive, cancelled in that window, reaches the state. STATED CONSERVATIVELY, because it does NOT complete the fault RV2-DEBT-199 reproduced: after `park_requeue_locked` no task holds that key, and every routing dereference took a caller-supplied key rather than one read back out of a store - so nothing re-routes the stale address. RV2-DEBT-199's fix removes the dereference entirely, so the residual hazard is not a use-after-free but MISDELIVERY if the allocator hands the same address to a later `rt_channel_new`, defended today only by the park-generation (`seq`) validation and the re-arm branch in `rt_channel_lane.h`. **RE-DERIVED 2026-08-19, and the first proposed fix was WRONG.** That proposal guarded the new removal with a key-kind allowlist (`RT_DEBT046_STALE_KEY_REMOVABLE`, exempting only `WAKER_JOIN`) and would have run an UNQUALIFIED sweep for scope/net/blocking/select/remote-reply keys, whose entries `add_waiter` always appends at `seq 0` — the exact RV2-DEBT-046 lost-wake shape the file already documents at `rt_task_park.c:110-123`. Re-reading the two branches gives a fix that cannot have that effect, because it reuses what the function already knows. **The mechanism, verified by reading:** the first abort branch (`rt_task_park.c:272-277`) fires BEFORE `add_waiter`, so the only registration in flight is the one `channel_park_prepare_locked` appended, and that entry carries a NONZERO generation — `rt_channel_lane.h:210-214` and `:225-231` each stamp `task->park_seq++` with a `0 -> 1` fixup. `park_requeue_locked` (`:180-189`) clears `park_key` and `park_prepared` but leaves `park_seq` alone, so the generation is still readable at the abort. The SECOND abort branch already does the right thing (`:294-305`): it captures `abort_seq` — nonzero only for `WAKER_CHAN_SEND`/`WAKER_CHAN_RECV` — and calls `remove_waiter_generation` AFTER releasing the owner lock. **So the fix is to give the first branch the same two things the second one has**: an `abort_seq` computed the same way, and a removal placed after the unlock. A generation-qualified removal is structurally unable to eat a fresh registration, because `remove_waiter_from_store_seq` (`rt_async_waiter.c:173-174`) only matches on a nonzero seq and every unqualified registration sits at zero. `channel_park_prepare_locked` has exactly two callers, `rt_async_channel.c:190` and `:363`, both channel keys, so no other key kind can reach the prepared state this retires. NOT YET IMPLEMENTED: the Rule 13 proof is a deterministic sync-point harness on `SP_CANCEL_BEFORE_WAKE` against `SP_PARK_BEFORE_WAITING`, templated on `internal/vm/runtime_v2_cancelled_join_waiter_syncpoint_test.go`, with a negative-control build that stays red. **HARNESS ATTEMPT 2026-08-19 — the window is reachable, the DRIVER is not yet right, and the attempt corrected two things.** A fixture was built on the freed-waiter template: mint a capacity-1 anchored channel, prefill it so the next send parks, hold the poller between the prepare and the park, cancel from another thread, release, then count entries by key VALUE in the owner's store. **(1) `SP_PARK_BEFORE_WAITING` CANNOT be the rendezvous.** It is reached by EVERY park in the process, so arming it to block stops threads the row has nothing to do with; the first run deadlocked with `rt_sync_point: block timed out (no matching open?)`. A narrower point placed right after `channel_park_prepare_locked` in `rt_async_channel.c` works and passes the static gate, which needs three edits: the enumerator in `rt_sync_point.h`, the name in `rt_sp_name` (`rt_sync_point.c`), and the `WINDOW_FILE` entry in `check_sync_points.sh`. **(2) THE DRIVER CANCELS THE WRONG TASK'S PARK.** With a probe in the first abort branch the run reports `branch A ... kind=11 prepared=1 seq=0` — kind 11 is `WAKER_REMOTE_TASK_REPLY`, the anchored CALLER, not the body's `WAKER_CHAN_SEND`. The store is observed holding exactly one entry at the window and ZERO after the unwind, so on this path nothing is stranded and the row's conclusion is NOT reproduced. The next attempt must drive the BODY's own park into the abort and show which of them the row's claim depends on. **(3) The probe VALIDATED the guard the re-derivation proposes and is itself the counter-example to the first proposal**: a `WAKER_REMOTE_TASK_REPLY` key reaches that branch with `park_prepared = 1` and `park_seq = 0`, so a removal guarded on `park_prepared` alone would have been UNQUALIFIED there — the lost wake. Guarding on a NONZERO generation skips it by construction. The harness was reverted rather than committed: it reaches its window and proves nothing, which is the one thing this ledger refuses to keep. **FIXED 2026-08-20, AND THE DRIVER PROBLEM THAT SANK THE PREVIOUS ATTEMPT IS SOLVED.** The earlier harness reported `kind=11` - WAKER_REMOTE_TASK_REPLY, the anchored CALLER - so it never drove the BODY's own park into the branch and the row's conclusion went unreproduced. The new one catches `kind=4` (WAKER_CHAN_SEND) and shows the stranding directly: with the retirement reverted the probe prints `entries=1` after the unwind. THE FIX gives the first abort branch the two things the second one always had: the generation is read on the parking task's own thread BEFORE `park_requeue_locked` can hand the task to another worker, and the removal runs AFTER the owner lock is released. It is guarded on a NONZERO generation, which is what keeps it from becoming the RV2-DEBT-046 lost wake: `remove_waiter_from_store_seq` treats seq 0 as match-any, and the join, scope and remote-reply registrations that also reach this branch all sit at seq 0. A generation-qualified removal also cannot eat a fresh registration, because a requeued task can only re-register at a LATER generation. VERIFIED BY THE LEAD: three tests green across shards-1/2/8 including the negative control, the retirement shown reverted and the entry shown surviving, and both `make runtime-v2-lifecycle-check` (with the three new tests wired into it) and `make runtime-v2-syncpoint-check` green afterwards. | Fixed 2026-08-20 | Epic 23b Wave D, channel owner (step D3) | The abort branch retires the entry it registered, or the design states explicitly that a stale channel entry is tolerated and names what makes misdelivery impossible. Evidence is a deterministic sync-point row driving the cancel/park window with a negative control that reproduces the surviving entry, plus a probe showing an address reused by a later channel does not deliver into the stale registration. This is where the deferred "retire entries at close versus at free" decision gets taken. |

Seven panics, seven failing subtests, one process each. **A TAGLESS REPO-WIDE GUARD NOW HOLDS IT**: any channel receive handed a literal null sink fails `TestRuntimeV2ChannelRecvSinkIsNeverNull`. It is deliberately untagged, because the harness that broke sits behind `//go:build runtime_v2_pending` - `make check` never compiled it, which is how `853aafba` passed the hook while reddening a mandatory gate. It scans `.go` files as text, since the C harnesses live inside Go string constants, and it SKIPS build outputs: on its first run outside a fresh worktree it reported eight stale copies of `rt_async_select.c` under `build/showcases/`, frozen at what the runtime said months ago. Section 12 names the transport gate mandatory; it exits 2 through `runtime-v2-transport-contract-check`, and no row in this ledger mentions it. `TestRuntimeV2RemoteSelectAbandonEdges` fails seven of its eight subtests - `cancel-vs-commit-ships-winner`, `cancel-before-dispatch-refuses-body`, `double-cancel-is-idempotent`, `refusal-after-shipped-unpins-prefix`, `initial-owner-mismatch-drops-payloads`, `initial-enqueue-refusal-drops-payload-once`, `recv-winner-returns-losing-payload`; only `initial-null-anchor-array-drops-payload` passes. Every failure is the same runtime panic: `async: channel try-recv with nowhere to put the value`. THE PANIC NAMES THE DEFECT PRECISELY. It is raised by `rt_channel_try_recv_status_owner_locked` (`runtime/native/rt_channel_sync.c`) when `out_bits == NULL`, and the comment above it states the contract being broken: a try-recv is irreversible - it takes a buffered entry or acks a parked sender - so "a caller with no sink was not asking a question, it was destroying the answer", and a caller that does not want the value must still take it and release it through `rt_channel_release_payload`. So some remote-select abandon path is asking whether a value is there by consuming it into nowhere. MEASURED 2026-08-11 at three commits, failure-for-failure identical at all three: `f2641713` (before Epic 23b Wave D step D0), `4f2a1191` (after D0, before the D0.7 routing fix) and the D0.7 fix itself. It is therefore fully pre-existing and neither D0 nor RV2-DEBT-199 introduced it. A SEVEN-KIND program (whole binding, self-compound `u += u`, struct field, tuple element, store through a `&mut` parameter, a plain `int` that owns nothing, array concat) goes from `88 allocs, 60 frees, 1,176 + 32 bytes lost` to `88 allocs, 88 frees, zero errors and zero leaks`, with the answer 156 unchanged both ways - which is why this was invisible.
