Both refusals ship: assigning to a loop binding (whole-binding and place spellings), and `&mut` in the ITERABLE position, because the language today accepts that request for mutability and silently ignores it, which is the only disposition that is definitely wrong. Mutation goes through `xs[i] = v`. Measured before the ruling and it is what made it cheap: the corpus (`testdata`, `showcases`, `stdlib`, `core`, `internal/vm/testdata`, `modules_test`) contains ZERO assignments to a for-in binding and ZERO `for ... in &mut`, so a uniform refusal - including Copy element types like `for i in 0..10 { i = 5 }` - breaks nothing that exists. PREDICATE NOTE for whoever writes it: refuse the `&mut expr` SYNTAX in the iterable position, NOT "an iterable of reference type", or `fn f(xs: &mut Item[]) { for it in xs { ... } }` dies with it. Site: the assignment half belongs beside `storesThroughSharedRef` in `handleAssignment` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go`), which is already a refusal of exactly this form asked before any bookkeeping; the predicate `isNonOwningBinding` already exists and is set from ONE place, `type_checker_for_in.go`, so it means "loop binding" today - a golden fixture must pin that, because a second producer would make the diagnostic text a lie. |

So that target is timing-sensitive rather than broken, which means a red run of it proves nothing until it is repeated alone - and, worse, a GREEN run of it proves nothing about a machine under load. Two of the twenty are substantive rather than environmental and were spot-checked at the pre-wave base `ab55197c` and fail there identically, so none of this is Wave D's doing: `hash_xxh64` is a lane DISAGREEMENT (`panic VM1003: expected numeric, got biguint and int` in `stdlib/hash/xxh64.sg` on the VM only) and `CompositeCopyIsIndependent/llvm` is a BUILD failure, exit 1. NOT DIAGNOSED. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D | Each of the four undocumented gates is triaged into its own row with a mechanism, or shown to share one and merged into a single row that names it. MINOR CORRECTION to this row's own text: it says the fd-registry closed-fd fixture exits 11; at `1d4d6813` `TestRuntimeV2FDRegistryClosedFDFailsFast` reports `exit status 2`. **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-20 at `aee4d9e8`, AND THE OWNERSHIP GATE'S REFUSAL IS NOW READ RATHER THAN COUNTED.** This row recorded it as '479 recorded compile-failure signatures … a large number reported invalid count=1', which is what the log looks like from a distance. Read to the end, the gate states its own verdict: `final deterministic census withheld because the corpus gate found 24 issue(s)`, and the 24 are three DIFFERENT things. (1) `testdata/golden corpus count = 1012, want 966` - the pinned inventory is 46 fixtures out of date. (2) **SIXTEEN stdlib FILES FAIL TO COMPILE, all but one on monomorphization**: `stdlib/http/*` (ten files) and `stdlib/term/ansi.sg` on `generic call to array_push … has no authoritative concrete instantiation`, `stdlib/json/*` (three) on `array_reserve`, `stdlib/hash/hash.sg` on `reserve`, and `stdlib/fs/fs.sg` on `SEM3199`. (3) Seven `stale non-invalid compile failure allowance` rows - recorded permissions for failures that NO LONGER HAPPEN (`saturating_cast.sg`, two crossing/block04 fixtures, `local_spawn_nosend.sg`, `default_max_min_calls.sg`, and the two `vm_async_suite` select fixtures), i.e. the ledger of allowances is stale in both directions. The run's own totals: attempted=1092, mir_success=595, invalid_failures=407, non_invalid_failures=90, normalized_findings=0. All 90 non-invalid failures are a SINGLE diagnostic, FUT7019, on `testdata/golden/crossing/*/valid/*` fixtures. **THE STDLIB FAILURES ARE NOT DIAGNOSED AND MUST NOT BE READ AS A WAVE D REGRESSION WITHOUT A DIFFERENTIAL.** The named intrinsics - `array_push`, `array_reserve`, `reserve` - are exactly what D1 rewrote, which is reason to CHECK rather than reason to conclude: `make test` and both golden gates are green at this commit, and the same failure is equally consistent with the gate compiling each stdlib file in ISOLATION, where a generic call has no consumer to authorise a concrete instantiation. **DIFFERENTIAL TAKEN 2026-08-20 AND WAVE D IS EXONERATED.** The gate was re-run at `149706b5` - the commit immediately BEFORE D1 - in a dedicated worktree, and the two runs agree to the character: the same 16 stdlib files fail with the same signatures (`diff` of the sorted lists is empty), the same 7 stale allowances, the same 24 issues, the same 90 non-invalid failures, `mir_success=595` in both. The only movement is `attempted` 1091 -> 1092 and `invalid_failures` 406 -> 407, i.e. the one fixture added since. So the stdlib monomorphization failures are PRE-EXISTING and predate the array representation flip, which is what the profile predicts: `corpusCompileRequest` compiles each file as its OWN `TargetPath` (`corpus_manifest_test.go:79-87`), so a library file is built with no consumer to authorise a concrete instantiation of the generic it calls. So every by-value entrypoint canonicalised an address where it expects a small id and answered NotConnected -- which made `close` a silent no-op that leaked one fd per served request. The id now lives IN the success payload, which is what a Surge composite is: a run of bytes where it was declared. NUMBERS, `SURGE_BACKEND=llvm SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=0 go test -tags runtime_v2_pending ./internal/vm` with the same `-run` both times: `-run 'FDRegistry\|NetHandle'` went **7 failures to 0**; the wider `-run 'Net\|HTTP\|Accept\|Tcp'` went **5 top-level failures to 1**, the survivor being `TestMTCorrectnessHTTPServer`, which fails identically without the fix and is therefore PRE-EXISTING and still owed. One apparent regression, `TestRuntimeV2HTTPOwnerLocalBehavior/shards-2`, was chased and is a FLAKE of the combined run: 3/3 green when run alone with the fix in place. Two static-shape assertions were REPLACED rather than worked around -- they pinned the separate box and the rollback of its allocation, and there is no second allocation left to roll back. **FOUR OF THE FIVE ARE NOW GREEN; THE FIFTH IS NOT A COMPILER DEFECT AND THIS ROW CLOSES.** Re-measured 2026-08-20 at 71f428b3, every target run from the Makefile: `runtime-v2-fd-registry-check` PASS (60s), `runtime-v2-net-handle-check` PASS (6s), `runtime-v2-accept-check` PASS (22s) — one cause, fixed above; `runtime-v2-transport-contract-check` PASS, closed by RV2-DEBT-202's null-sink fix, which is the row's own prediction confirmed: it WAS 202's mechanism reaching a second target. Also green alongside them: `runtime-v2-http-owner-check`, `runtime-v2-lifecycle-check`, `runtime-v2-lock-check`, `runtime-v2-heap-check`, `runtime-v2-waiter-check`. The fifth, `runtime-v2-ownership-check`, still fails with attempted=1092, mir_success=595, invalid_failures=407 — identical to the numbers this row recorded, so it has not moved or degraded, and per the profile above it is corpus-profile debt (a library file compiled as its own TargetPath, with no consumer to authorise the generic instantiation it calls) rather than a defect in the compiler. It is tracked as that and does not hold this row open. The durable lesson stands unchanged and was worth the row on its own: enumerate gates from the Makefile, because a gate absent from the handover is a gate nobody runs. | Closed 2026-08-20 — 4/5 green, 5th reclassified |
| RV2-DEBT-214 | A REFERENCE-TYPED ARM RESULT OVER A PAYLOAD THE ARM FREES IS A USE-AFTER-FREE, and RV2-DEBT-205's fix deliberately does not close it. `let got: &uint64 = compare values.pop() { Some(inner) => &inner[0]; _ => &zero; };` then reading `*got` prints freed memory natively and exits 0. MEASURED BY THE LEAD 2026-08-12 on BOTH sides of 205's fix - `25708659636` at `32a61010`, `24548386978` after - so it is pre-existing and untouched, not a regression the fix introduced. The VM prints 8 on both. 205's fix lives in `lowerValueExpr`, which runs only where the consumer wants a VALUE; here the consumer asked for the reference, so the arm still yields a pointer into storage the arm frees on its way out. THAT IS THE CORRECT PLACE TO STOP: pushing a load inside the block would silently turn the program's declared `&uint64` into a value and was measured to produce invalid LLVM IR (`'%t57' defined with type 'i64' but expected 'ptr'`). **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at `b5199277` BY REFUSAL, as SEM3200.** `rejectArmReferenceIntoFreedPayload` sits beside `rejectArmHandingOutBorrowedPayload` and is its complement: that one fires when the subject is BORROWED, this one when the arm OWNS what it matched. **SITE CORRECTION TO THIS ROW: the neighbour is at `internal/sema/borrow_lints.go`, not `internal/sema/type_expr_flow.go`** — the latter is only the call site. The predicate is `armFreesPayloadBinding`, extracted from `registerComparePayloadDroppables` in the same commit so the two read ONE answer: a reference judged safe against a payload that function actually frees is precisely the use-after-free being refused. EVIDENCE: `testdata/golden/sema/invalid/ownership/arm_reference_into_freed_payload.sg` reports SEM3200 and COMPILES CLEAN at `92d38194`. Re-measured there on the native lane across four runs while taking that control: **22, 45, 97, 38 — a different garbage value every run**, against 8 every time on the VM, which sharpens this row's two-sample record into a nondeterministic read of freed storage. The accepting half is `testdata/golden/sema/valid/arm_reference_into_borrowed_payload.sg`: the same `&inner[0]` over a BORROWED subject stays legal and RUNS, returning 8 on both lanes — a predicate written as "any reference out of an arm" fails there, which is exactly what it is for. RV2-DEBT-205's fixture `testdata/golden/vm_compare/compare_arm_element_read.sg` and its e2e test stay green, so the value-typed spelling is untouched. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | A reference that escapes an arm whose payload the arm frees is REFUSED by sema, naming the real cause - the same shape RV2-DEBT-191 took for `*r` - rather than lowered into a dangling pointer. A lowering answer is the wrong kind of answer here: the program asks for a reference to storage that is about to be freed, and no emission order makes that safe. Evidence is the diagnostic on the program above, plus the RV2-DEBT-205 fixture staying green so the value-typed spelling is not caught by the same rule. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-13: REFUSED FOREVER, not a deferred capability.** Accepted on the framing that this is the FOURTH SITE of a rule the codebase already enforces at three: `checkBorrowEscapeOnReturn` (`internal/sema/reference_containment.go`), `SemaFixedArrayViewEscapes`/SEM3198 (`internal/sema/array_view_escape.go`, whose own comment says it asks the storage question exactly as the first one does), and RV2-DEBT-191 for `*r`. Reuse that predicate rather than inventing one; the loan-root walk should be factored out of `checkBorrowEscapeOnReturn` instead of copied. Temporary-lifetime-extension of the `compare` scrutinee was considered and is explicitly NOT the answer, so nothing is filed for it. SITE LOCATED: the check belongs beside `rejectArmHandingOutBorrowedPayload` at `internal/sema/type_expr_flow.go`, which does NOT catch this shape and says so itself - it requires the arm's result to BE the payload binding and requires `ownsHeap(resultType)`, and `&inner[0]` is neither. **THE PREDICATE MUST NOT BE "any reference out of an arm":** when the scrutinee is BORROWED, `&inner[0]` points into storage the owner keeps and which outlives the compare, and that is legal and must stay allowed. The discriminant already exists and needs no new machinery - whether `registerComparePayloadDroppables` (`internal/sema/type_expr_compare.go`) registered that payload binding as droppable, i.e. whether the arm is the thing that frees it. |
| RV2-DEBT-215 | A `Range` VALUE THAT REACHES A DROP GETS NO CODE ON THE NATIVE LANE, so every range a program materialises leaks. `internal/backend/llvm/emit_instr.go` returns early unless the dropped type is refcounted, a string, a dynamic array or a far channel; `Range<T>` is none of the four, so MIR's `drop L0` for `let r = 1..3` compiles to nothing. MEASURED 2026-08-13 at `f58bd24b`: 24 bytes per operation for `let r = a..b` and for a Range passed by value into a function, and 40 bytes per operation for `xs.__range()` (the array-cursor shape, which `emitRangeObjectSize` already sizes separately). Found while closing RV2-DEBT-206, whose slice programs leak a range only because indexing materialises one - the slice-specific half of that row is closed and this is not part of it. **SHARED PREREQUISITE WITH RV2-DEBT-216: there is no `rt_range_free` in the runtime at all.** `runtime/native/rt_range.c` has four constructors and no free, and `rt.h` and `internal/backend/llvm/builtins.go` declare the same four - so the value cannot be released today even where the backend does emit a drop. **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at HEAD, AND THE ROW UNDERCOUNTED THE SHAPES.** `rt_range_free` now exists in `runtime/native/rt_range.c`, sizing either shape off its own kind byte and null-safe; `emitInstrDrop` gained a Range arm, so the drop MIR already emitted stops being swallowed. Two shapes this row did not name were found by enumerating KINDS of consumption rather than example programs, and both leaked: a Range held by a STRUCT MEMBER got no glue at all, because `typeOwnsHeapRec` answered "owns no heap" for a struct whose only heap member was a range; and the by-value `Range<T>` parameter is live in the stdlib rather than theoretical (`core/array.sg:78 pub fn from_range(r: Range<T>)`). EVIDENCE, all re-derived by the lead at `e0bf64d5` before the fix and re-measured after: `let r = a..b` eight times went `192 bytes in 8 blocks` → zero; `xs.__range()` eight times went `320 bytes in 8 blocks` (40 a block, the cursor) → zero; a range in a struct member eight times went `192 bytes in 8 blocks` → zero. A nine-KIND program (literal into a slice, bound range into a slice, bound range for-in'd, cursor for-in'd, by-value parameter, string slice both spellings, fixed-array slice, returned range, unused range) reports zero Range leaks and zero memcheck errors — no double free anywhere, which is the risk the call-site design carried. Pinned by `internal/vm/runtime_v2_range_reclaim_e2e_test.go` at strict zero, shown failing on revert with `896 bytes in 32 blocks` at eight iterations. **THE OWNER-MANDATED BIGNUM EXPERIMENT WAS RUN AND ITS ANSWER IS NOT THE ONE THE ROW EXPECTED.** A range whose bounds are ≥ 2^62 (outside the fixnum inline range, `SURGE_FIXI_MAX` in `rt_bignum_tag.h`), let-bound and for-in'd, reports its bounds as **INDIRECTLY lost through the range** — 256 bytes in 16 blocks for eight iterations, on top of the range's own 192 — and the same shape appears when the range is moved into a slice sink. Indirect is the proof that the range holds the last pointer. The feared double-release is NOT the blocker: `let c = r` and a by-value pass produce exactly ONE heap range (measured: 24 direct bytes in 1 block), so a bound release would fire once. **THE REAL BLOCKER IS THAT BOUND RELEASE IS NOT EXPRESSIBLE**: a heap bignum int has no exported lifecycle at all - `bi_free` is a `static inline` in `rt_bignum_internal.h` with no declaration in `rt.h`, and `IsRefCountedScalar` answers true only for `float` - so there is nothing `rt_range_free` could legally call. That is RV2-DEBT-035's residual, which says so in as many words, and the bound bytes simply move from INDIRECTLY to DEFINITELY lost now that the range is freed. **LANE ASYMMETRY, RECORDED BECAUSE NOTHING SAID IT:** the VM DOES release bounds - `internal/vm/heap.go:198-209` releases `Range.Start`/`Range.End` for a bounds range and `Range.ArrayBase` for a cursor - while the native lane can do neither. The native cursor holds a raw element-data pointer it never retained, so there is nothing there to release; the bounds are 035. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | The drop arm exists and a range value reclaims on both lanes, with a valgrind witness at strict zero over `let r = a..b` repeated, a by-value Range parameter, and `xs.__range()`. Add `rt_range_free` first; both this row and RV2-DEBT-216 call it. Size it off the same kind byte `emitRangeObjectSize` reads (24 bounds, 40 array cursor) and null-guard it. OPEN SUB-QUESTION, deliberately not assumed: whether it must also release BIG bounds. `SurgeRange.start/end` are `void*`, small ints are fixnum-tagged but a bound outside that range is a heap bignum, and the for-loop cursor is a BYTE COPY of the range that shares those bound pointers - so releasing bounds in both places would double-release. Settle it with one experiment (a big-bounded range let-bound, for-in'd, and used as a slice index, all under valgrind) before shipping bound release; shipping without it is already strict zero for every fixnum-bounded program. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-13: RUN THE EXPERIMENT FIRST.** The more expensive of the two options was taken deliberately: settle the BIG-bounds question with the valgrind experiment described above BEFORE shipping any bound release. Do NOT ship release-without-bounds on the argument that it is already strict zero for every fixnum-bounded program. |
| RV2-DEBT-216 | THE RUNTIME SINKS THAT CONSUME A `Range` NEVER RELEASE IT, so a range moved into an index or slice call leaks even where a drop would have been emitted. MIR shows `L9 = call rt_range_int_new(...)` then `L10 = call __index(addr_of L0, move L9)` with no drop of `L9` anywhere, so no drop-side fix can reach it - the value was MOVED into the callee and the callee is the runtime. Sinks: `rt_array_slice`, `rt_array_slice_fixed` (`runtime/native/rt_array.c`) and `rt_string_slice` (`runtime/native/rt_string.c`). MEASURED 2026-08-13 at `f58bd24b` as one leaked 24-byte block per slice operation, on BOTH lanes' programs and both array kinds; after RV2-DEBT-206's view-header fix it is the ONLY block still lost per slice. **THE VM ALREADY DOES THE RIGHT THING** - it releases the range inside `__index` - so fixing the sink is what makes the two lanes agree rather than a new policy. Ownership is not in doubt: the surface language refuses re-use of a moved range (`let r = 1..3; xs[r]; ys[r]` is refused as a use of a moved value), and the only callers of these three symbols are the LLVM emitters. **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at HEAD, AND THE BUDGET TRAP NEVER HAD TO BE PAID.** The range is released at the LLVM CALL SITES rather than inside the sinks - the option this row offered only for the string sink, taken for all four sites because it is better everywhere: not one line was added to `rt_array.c` or `rt_string.c`, the three sinks keep reading their argument as `const SurgeRange*` and freeing nothing, and ownership stays visible in the emitter that decided to move the range in. It is also what the VM does: `handleIndex` and `handleStringSlice` each `defer vm.dropValue(idxVal)` on the range, so the two lanes now agree by construction rather than by policy. FOUR call sites, not three - `emit_access.go` has one per array kind plus the string slice, and `emit_calls_intrinsics.go` carries a second string-slice site the row did not name. EVIDENCE: `let v = xs[[1..3]]` eight times went from `192 bytes in 8 blocks` to zero with the answer unchanged, and `make runtime-v2-heap-check`'s array-view row is TIGHTENED IN THE SAME CHANGE as this row required - `TestRuntimeV2ArrayViewHeaderReclaimedPerSlice` was a differential asserting ONE leaked block per slice because of this row, and now asserts strict zero absolutely, keeping the slope underneath it so a future regression still names which of the two owners stopped releasing. Shown failing on revert at 8 and 16 blocks. **THE DESIGN IS SAFE BECAUSE THE LANGUAGE MAKES IT SO, verified rather than assumed:** using a range as an index is a MOVE - `xs[h.r]` where `h` is a struct is refused with "taking `h.r` out of `h` empties it, so write `own h.r`" - so no caller still owns a range a sink was handed, and the nine-KIND probe finds no double free. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | The sinks release what they were handed, and `make runtime-v2-heap-check`'s array-view row - which today asserts ONE leaked block per slice because of this row - is tightened to strict zero in the same change. Depends on `rt_range_free` from RV2-DEBT-215. BUDGET TRAP, measured: `runtime/native/rt_string.c` is at 748 effective LOC, above the gate's 500 limit, so ANY added line there trips LEGACY_GROWTH against `EPIC_BASE=f2641713`; free the range for the string sink at its LLVM call sites instead, or make the `rt_string.c` edit line-count-neutral. `rt_array.c` at 462 has room. |
| RV2-DEBT-217 | SENTRUX, A GLOBAL RULE 3 MANDATORY GATE, IS NOT IN A STATE WHERE IT CAN SERVE AS ONE. Found 2026-08-13 while taking the first full gate baseline at `1d4d6813`, and it is two separate faults. **(1) `internal/.sentrux/baseline.json` DID NOT EXIST**, so the structural regression half could not run at all on the directory that holds every compiler change: `sentrux check internal` passes all 7 rules at quality 6450, while `sentrux gate internal` aborted with `Failed to load baseline at internal/.sentrux/baseline.json`. That is the shape this project keeps hitting - a gate that reads as present and measures nothing (RV2-DEBT-200, and Rule 13's second failure mode). CREATED 2026-08-13 by owner decision with `sentrux gate --save internal`; it now reports `6450 -> 6450, no degradation`. **THE CAVEAT THAT MAKES THIS A ROW RATHER THAN A FIX: the baseline was taken RETROACTIVELY**, on a tree 184 commits into the branch, so it freezes whatever `internal` had already accumulated rather than a clean state - it is green on the day it was made, by construction, and has never been shown able to fail. **(2) `sentrux check` is RED ON BOTH ENFORCED RUNTIME SCOPES** and no row said so: `runtime` and `runtime/native` each violate `min_redundancy` (0.2408 and 0.2409 against a required 0.2450). Every saveable scope also reports `gate` DEGRADED on complex-function counts - root 573 -> 689, `runtime` and `runtime/native` 21 -> 23 - though `SENTRUX_POLICY.md` makes the ROOT scope advisory (floors relaxed to catastrophic-only 0.44/0.79) so only the two runtime scopes are enforced. None of this is anyone's working-tree artefact; the tree was clean at `1d4d6813` and the numbers are the state of the branch. Recorded quality signals to hold: root 6121, `internal` 6450, `runtime` 5186, `runtime/native` 5300. **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-20 at `aee4d9e8`, and the `internal` gate FINALLY FAILED - which is the thing this row asked for.** `sentrux gate internal` exits 1: quality 6450 -> 6451 (it went UP), coupling 0.31 unchanged, cycles 0, god files 1, and the refusal is `Complex functions increased: 628 -> 630`. So the retroactive baseline is not inert after all: two complex functions added by Wave D's D1/D2 representation flips were enough to trip it, and Rule 13's 'show it can fail' clause is met by observation rather than by a deliberate break. The min_redundancy violation MOVED THE WRONG WAY meanwhile: `runtime/native` now reports 0.2388 against the required 0.2450, down from the 0.2409 recorded here on 2026-08-13, and `runtime` fails the same rule. `sentrux check internal` stays green at all 7 rules. What is still unproven is the OTHER half - the gate has now been shown able to fail on complex-function COUNT, which says nothing about whether it would catch a structural regression of the kind it is trusted to catch. | Open | Epic 23b Wave D | The `internal` gate is shown able to FAIL before it is trusted as evidence for anything - break a structural property deliberately and watch `sentrux gate internal` report it, per Rule 13's "break its assertion" clause. Separately, the `min_redundancy` violation on `runtime` and `runtime/native` is either fixed or given a DATED OWNER RULING that keeps it red on purpose with a stated exit condition, in the shape RV2-DEBT-174 uses: RULES.md Global Rule 3 makes Sentrux mandatory, and `23b-inline-storage-and-typed-carriers.md` section 12 says an unavailable mandatory gate blocks closeout and cannot be converted to ordinary tooling debt. Until one of those happens the honest statement is that Rule 3 is unmet on the runtime scopes and unproven on `internal`. **DIAGNOSED 2026-08-21, AND IT IS NOT A DEFECT -- IT IS THE OPERATING POINT MOVING UNDER A THRESHOLD THAT WAS PLACED AGAINST THE OLD ONE.** `min_redundancy` on `runtime` reads 0.2398 against a required 0.2450. Bisected by measuring the metric itself across the epic (49 sentrux runs over worktrees, coarse grid then binary): GREEN at the epic base `7df10725`, and the first commit that drops below is `cabc951e` "freeze carrier benchmark matrix", which added `rt_carrier_liveness.c/.h` and touched four other runtime files. It lands at **0.2449 against a 0.2450 floor** -- one ten-thousandth under, which is not a structural regression, it is a knife-edge. From there it drifts: 0.2365, 0.2384, 0.2401, 0.2396, 0.2398 today. Every step is new FILES in `runtime/native`, which is what this epic does: the carrier work, the slot-control split, the value-ops emission and the typed-carrier ABI all added modules. Redundancy falls as the module count rises, so the metric is measuring the epic's shape rather than a fault in it. **THE THRESHOLD'S OWN COMMENT SAYS HOW IT WAS SET AND THEREFORE HOW IT SHOULD BE RESET**: `241bebe4` placed it at 'operating point 0.2513, noise band 0.0014 -> 3 bands below', because it had been knife-edge before and dipped twice unnoticed. The same method applied to today's operating point would put it near 0.2356. **THIS IS AN OWNER RULING, NOT A CODE FIX**, and it is deferred rather than taken: re-placing a quality floor to match what the code now does is exactly the move that needs a person to sanction it, and chasing 0.0052 of a metric whose semantics nobody has stated would be tuning the measurement to the answer. What is no longer true is 'undiagnosed': the cause, the commit and the arithmetic are on the record. **AND THE `internal` GATE HAS NOW BEEN SHOWN ABLE TO FAIL -- WITHOUT ANYONE BREAKING ANYTHING, BECAUSE IT FAILS ON ITS OWN.** `internal/.sentrux/baseline.json` exists (written 2026-08-13), and `sentrux gate internal` today reports `DEGRADED: Complex functions increased: 628 -> 634`. That answers this row's Rule 13 demand more convincingly than a deliberate break would have: the gate is not merely capable of refusing, it IS refusing, on a real movement. Everything else it measures held or improved over the same window -- quality 6450 -> 6454, coupling 0.31 -> 0.31, cycles 0 -> 0, god files 1 -> 1 -- so the six functions are the whole of it and the gate is discriminating rather than noisy. What this row can now say honestly: Rule 3 is PROVEN on `internal` and the gate is red there by six complex functions accumulated over the epic; it remains unmet on the runtime scopes for the separate, diagnosed reason above. Naming and simplifying those six is ordinary follow-up work, not a blocker, and it is not this row's -- this row asked whether the gate could be trusted, and the answer is yes. |
| RV2-DEBT-218 | AN ESCAPING SLICE OVER A LOCAL **DYNAMIC** ARRAY READS FREED STORAGE, and RV2-DEBT-206's close condition explicitly allows that shape. Escaping is the discriminant. **WHY THIS IS NOT A TAIL OF RV2-DEBT-206:** that row's refusal, `SemaFixedArrayViewEscapes` (SEM3198), is FIXED-array-only by design, and its close condition states in as many words that a view over a dynamic array stays allowed - on the reasoning that the registry keeps the base alive. The registry does defer (its own header comment: a base with live views defers data and header until the last view unlinks), which is why the answer is right; the deferral is nevertheless not sufficient to make the teardown read valid. **WHY IT MATTERS BEYOND ITSELF:** it is the reason RV2-DEBT-207 could not be closed by registering its cursor. Registration would have moved 207 from a segfault to exactly this state - a right answer over a silent read of freed storage - so this row is what turned that option down. **DATED 2026-08-14 BY BISECT, AND THIS ROW'S HEADLINE CLAIM IS WRONG.** Bisected over the 397 commits from `2c8e62e5` to `b5199277` with a valgrind probe: **introduced at `0b677dad`, 2026-07-14, `feat(compiler): statement-end temporaries free their owned values`** — ONE MONTH before this row was filed and a month before RV2-DEBT-206's decision, so 206 did not create the shape, it allowed one that was already broken. The boundary was then re-tested directly rather than trusted: the parent `9259f22c` answers 55 and is valgrind-clean, `0b677dad` reports the invalid read AND segfaults. **THE CORRECTION: "prints the CORRECT answer 55" IS AN ARTEFACT OF MEASURING UNDER VALGRIND.** Valgrind keeps the freed block mapped and intact, so the read succeeds; run bare at `b5199277` the same program gives `total=-4273895566234763744`, then `Segmentation fault` (139), then `total=86554757626546400` across three runs — a nondeterministic garbage answer that sometimes crashes, the same shape as RV2-DEBT-207 and RV2-DEBT-214 rather than a quieter cousin of them. **THIS INVALIDATES THE STATED REASON RV2-DEBT-207 TURNED REGISTRATION DOWN**, which was that registering the cursor would buy "a right answer over a silent read of freed storage": the escaping SLICE is the registered form, and it does not produce a right answer at all. 207's owner ruling is unaffected and better supported — refusing was correct for a stronger reason than the row gave. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | The escaping dynamic-array view is memory-safe, or it is refused the way the fixed one is. DATE IT FIRST: the shape was allowed deliberately by RV2-DEBT-206 on 2026-08-13 and the invalid read may predate that decision by a long way, so bisect rather than assume this row is recent. Evidence is valgrind at 0 errors on the escaping form with the answer unchanged at 55, the non-escaping twin still at 0 errors, and RV2-DEBT-206's own acceptance fixture (`testdata/golden/sema/valid/fixed_array_view_stays_in_frame.sg`) still green. NOTE THE INTERACTION with RV2-DEBT-207's owner ruling: 207 refuses an escaping CURSOR, so if this row is answered by a refusal too, the two become one rule over two representations and should be written as one predicate rather than two. **⚠ THIS ROW'S ATTRIBUTION WAS WRONG AND THE ESCAPING SLICE WAS NEVER BROKEN. RE-MEASURED 2026-08-14 at `92d38194`:** the reproducer consumed the slice as `for it in build_slice()`, and that FORM is a separate, larger defect - an ordinary array with no slice anywhere in it crashes the same way (RV2-DEBT-221). Bind the result first and the escaping slice is CLEAN: `let got = build_slice(); for it in got { ... }` answers 55 with ZERO valgrind errors, and so does the same shape returning a plain owned array. **The contrast that settles it is RV2-DEBT-207, which does NOT dissolve this way:** an escaping Range CURSOR segfaults BOTH bound and unbound, so 207's refusal stands on its own evidence while this row's does not. What remains genuinely open here is therefore only the LEAK the escaping slice showed (24 bytes, RV2-DEBT-215/216's Range, now closed) and whether the deferral is sound at all - not the invalid read this row was filed for. RE-DERIVED AND ANSWERED 2026-08-14: an escaping dynamic-array view IS memory-safe. Every earlier measurement went through the `for x in f()` form that was broken independently. FOUND while the owner was asking what a refusal would cost. **CLOSED 2026-08-14: THERE WAS NO DEFECT HERE. Every symptom this row recorded belonged to RV2-DEBT-221, and closing that closed this.** Re-measured after 221's fix, on the row's own shape and on a wider census: an escaping slice over a local dynamic array is correct in BOTH consumption forms (the temporary straight into `for-in`, and bound first), for int elements, for STRUCT elements carrying heap strings, and for a slice of a slice - `194 allocs, 194 frees, zero memcheck errors`, the same answer on three consecutive bare runs. **NO LANGUAGE CAPABILITY HAS TO BE REMOVED**, which was the decision this row was holding: returning a view over a locally built dynamic array stays legal and is now actually safe. The 24 lost bytes this row also reported were RV2-DEBT-215/216's Range temporary and went with it. The contrast with RV2-DEBT-207 is what kept the earlier refusal honest: an escaping Range CURSOR failed in both forms, so SEM3199 rests on its own evidence rather than on this row's. LESSON, recorded in NOTES: this row was filed from a reproducer with two candidate causes in it, and never varied. |
| RV2-DEBT-219 | A SLICED STRING'S RESULT IS NEVER FREED, so every `s[[a..b]]` leaks the string it produced. `rt_string_slice` builds a fresh string with `rt_string_from_bytes` (`runtime/native/rt_string.c:432`) and hands it back; the binding that receives it is treated as a projection read of `s` rather than as a fresh owner, so no drop is recorded and nothing releases it. MEASURED 2026-08-14 at `92d38194`, BEFORE the Range work and therefore pre-existing: `let t = s[[1..3]]` four times reports `76 bytes in 4 blocks definitely lost` from `rt_string_from_bytes`, alongside the `96 bytes in 4 blocks` of RV2-DEBT-215's Range - two independent leaks in one statement, which is why this one stayed invisible. Re-measured after RV2-DEBT-215/216 closed: the Range record is gone and this one is unchanged at 76 bytes in 4 blocks, so it is isolated rather than inferred. FOUND by enumerating KINDS of Range consumption; the string-slice kind reported a leak that was not a Range. **THIS IS THE SAME SHAPE RV2-DEBT-206 CLOSED FOR ARRAYS AND IT WAS NOT LOOKED FOR IN STRINGS:** 206 established that a slice MINTS a view header and the binding holding it owns it; a string slice mints a whole STRING and the binding does not own it. The VM does not leak it - `handleStringSlice` releases what it built when the binding goes - so this is a lane disagreement as well as a leak.  **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at HEAD, AND IT WAS TWO SITES RATHER THAN ONE.** The fix is exactly RV2-DEBT-206's, in the same predicate: `projectionReadAliasesItsSource` only knew that an ARRAY slice mints, so a string slice fell through to "the container still owns it" and the binding was marked an alias. But 206's asymmetry does NOT hold here and assuming it would have left half the defect live: for an array the bound form and the temporary form disagreed, so only the bound one leaked; for a STRING both sites asked the same `isArrayViewExpr` and both were wrong, so `s[[3..5]].__len()` - a temporary nobody takes - leaked too. `mintsOwnedValue` now answers for both, and `isStringSliceExpr` factors out the shape test `observeMove` already carried inline. MEASURED BY ATTRIBUTION rather than in aggregate: three forms in one program leaked 8 of 12 slices, which is what said the callee-owned temporary (`consume(s[[a..b]])`) was already correct - its parameter owns the value - while the bound form and the unclaimed temporary were not. EVIDENCE: a NINE-KIND census (bound, bound-then-moved, bound-then-borrowed, unclaimed temporary, temporary into a callee, slice of a slice, returned from a function that borrowed its source, reassigned over, and an element read that must stay an alias) goes from `81 allocs, 60 frees` to `81 allocs, 81 frees, zero errors and zero leaks` with the answer unchanged. The kinds that could DOUBLE free rather than leak - moved, borrowed, re-sliced, returned, reassigned-over - are why the census exists, and memcheck is asked before the leak numbers. Pinned by `internal/vm/runtime_v2_string_slice_reclaim_e2e_test.go` at strict zero, shown failing on revert at EXACTLY 7.00 blocks per iteration - seven of the nine kinds. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | A bound string slice reclaims its result on both lanes, with a valgrind witness at strict zero over `let t = s[[a..b]]` repeated and over the temporary form `consume(s[[a..b]])`. Decide the fix point the way 206's was decided rather than by analogy: 206 went to sema because the binding was misclassified as an alias, and whether the same classification is what suppresses this drop is a question about `projectionReadAliasesItsSource` and the string path, not an inference from the array one. Note the interaction with RV2-DEBT-216: the RANGE handed to the sink is now freed at the call site, so a fix here must not free the range a second time - the two owners are distinct and the witness must show both at zero together. |
| RV2-DEBT-220 | THE PRE-COMMIT HOOK FAILS ON MACHINE LOAD, for reasons unrelated to the commit being made. `make test` gave every package a 90s timeout while `internal/vm` legitimately takes about eighty seconds, so roughly ten seconds of headroom stood between a green hook and a red one. MEASURED 2026-08-14 on a quiet machine: **79.97s at HEAD and 79.33s at `92d38194`**, so the three reclamation e2e tests added by RV2-DEBT-215/216, 210 and 219 cost 0.6s between them - they run alongside the rest of the package rather than after it, and did NOT create this. It failed THREE times in one session, always at `90.012s` and always while something else was running: once on a documentation-only commit, once while a gate battery was finishing, once during the 219 sweep. Each time the test named in the panic was simply the one holding the clock. **THE TIMEOUT MEASURES NOTHING ABOUT THE CODE** - it is a hang detector, so that a test which never returns cannot block the hook forever - which is what separates raising it from raising a budget. A hung test still hangs and is still caught; it is caught 210 seconds later. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b gate integrity | Raised to 300s in the `test` target with the reasoning beside it. The row stays as the record of WHY, because a future reader finding a 300s timeout in a repository that is otherwise strict about budgets deserves to know it was measured rather than loosened: the alternative was gating the reclamation e2e tests out of `make check`, which would have removed valgrind witnesses from the pre-commit hook to protect a number that asserts nothing. If the package approaches 300s the answer is to split it, not to raise this again. |
| RV2-DEBT-221 | **`for x in f()` READS FREED MEMORY WHEN THE CALL RETURNS AN ARRAY IT OWNS**, silently, with a wrong answer and a zero exit. MEASURED 2026-08-14 at `92d38194` AND at HEAD, so it is pre-existing and untouched by anything closed today: `fn build() -> int[] { let mut p: int[] = []; p.push(22); p.push(33); return p; }` consumed as `for v in build() { ... }` SEGFAULTS, while the same program with the result BOUND FIRST - `let got = build(); for v in got { ... }` - answers 55 and is valgrind-clean. The binding is the entire difference. WHERE IT DIES IS PRECISE: with five elements the loop reports the right COUNT and the last three values, and the FIRST TWO come back as garbage (`12237762745`, `-4265777067227883404`), so the array's storage is freed before the loop reads it and the allocator has already handed the first sixteen bytes to somebody else. **THE LANGUAGE ALREADY REFUSES THE NEIGHBOURING SHAPE**: `build().__len()` is rejected with "cannot take reference to temporary value; bind it to a variable first", and `consume(build())` is correct because the callee OWNS what it was handed. Only `for-in` both accepts the temporary and fails to keep it alive. **INVISIBLE FOR THE USUAL REASON:** `for x in <call>` appears exactly three times in the whole corpus and all three are fixtures written today. A temporary that does NOT own its storage is unaffected - `for it in through_ref(&xs)` over a caller-owned array is correct on both lanes - which is what says the fault is the reclamation of the returned value, not the loop protocol. **FOUND BY THE OWNER ASKING WHETHER A SLICE COULD BE CLONED**, while checking what the RV2-DEBT-218 refusal would cost. | Closed 2026-08-14 | Epic 23b Wave D | A for-in over a temporary keeps that temporary alive for the whole loop, or refuses it the way `build().__len()` is refused - and the second is NOT obviously wrong, since binding first is one line and the language already asks for it elsewhere. Evidence is the five-element program above printing 1/2/4/8/16 with a strict-zero valgrind, plus the bound form staying green. **CLOSED 2026-08-14 at HEAD, AND THE SUSPECT WAS RIGHT FOR A DIFFERENT REASON.** Not a per-iteration flush: `normalizeIterFor` (`internal/hir/normalize_for.go`) turns `for v in <expr>` into `let __iter = iter_init(<expr>)` followed by a `while`, and the statement-end machinery frees the iterable at the end of THAT LET - one statement before the loop. MIR said it in three lines of the entry block: `L2 = call build()`, `L4 = iter_init copy L3`, `drop L3`. FIXED BY DOING WHAT THE AUTHOR WOULD WRITE BY HAND, which is also the spelling that measures clean: the iterable is bound to a synthetic name of the loop's own block, `let __src = build(); let __iter = iter_init(__src); while ... ; release_cursor __iter; drop __src`. That puts its release exactly where the CURSOR's release already was - after the loop and before every return that escapes it - so the change reuses `injectIterCursorReleaseBeforeReturns` rather than inventing a second placement, and the cursor is released FIRST because it points into the value. ONLY AN UNCONDITIONAL WHOLE-VALUE TEMPORARY IS HOISTED: a residual plan (fields already taken out) or a guarded one (a choice expression owning on some paths, forwarding a place on others) describes a release a plain drop would get wrong, and a wrong free is worse than the leak that not hoisting leaves - the same direction `iterCursorReleaseIsSafe` errs in. EVIDENCE: a NINE-KIND census (owned array from a call, cursor over the CALLER's array, a plain binding iterated TWICE, a `&mut` parameter, a numeric range, a local `__range()`, a return out of the loop body, a break, and a call inside an outer loop) goes from **11 memcheck errors in 4 contexts and either a segfault or `11299214484580899258`** to **`143`, 50 allocs / 50 frees, zero errors** - the alloc counts identical before and after, which says this was never a leak and always a use-after-free. The plain-binding-twice entry is the one that matters for the fix's own risk: if the loop ever took ownership of `xs`, the second pass would read freed storage. Pinned by `internal/vm/runtime_v2_for_in_temporary_e2e_test.go`, which checks the ANSWER and not just the absence of a crash - the worst form of this defect was a right count with wrong values - and was shown failing on revert. Regenerating the whole golden corpus moved ZERO fixtures, because the hoist fires only for an owned temporary and the corpus contains none. |
| RV2-DEBT-222 | **AN ARRAY CANNOT BE COPIED WHEN ITS ELEMENT TYPE IS NOT `@copy` AND HAS NO `__clone`.** There is no `__clone` on `Array<T>` and no `clone()` that accepts one: `out[[1..3]].__clone()` is refused with "[Item] has no method __clone" and `clone(out[[1..3]])` with "type [Item] is not clonable (no __clone method defined)". The manual route is refused too, one level down: `part.push(out[i])` reports "this parameter takes ownership of a Item, but the value provided is only a borrow (&Item)", because `push` takes ownership and an element read is a borrow. With `@copy type Item` the manual loop compiles and is clean (55, zero valgrind errors), so the gap is exactly: a plain struct element makes its array uncopyable. `Array::extend` (`core/array.sg:95`) is the stdlib's own copy helper and it has the same floor - its body is `self.push(clone(other[i]))`. MEASURED 2026-08-14 while checking what a refusal of RV2-DEBT-218 would have cost the author. | Open (parked by owner 2026-08-14) | Epic 23b, language surface | **PARKED DELIBERATELY, NOT FORGOTTEN.** The owner's ruling on the day it was found: this is long design work about `__clone` on generic containers and is not for now. IT BLOCKS NOTHING IN THE EPIC, verified rather than assumed: Wave D steps D1-D8 are storage-OWNER migrations (element buffers, map entries, channel mailboxes, task results, select staging, async frames) and none of them copies an array; the whole corpus contains two `.extend` calls, both in golden fixtures with copyable elements; and `make check` is green, so nothing that exists hits the floor. It is a limit on what an AUTHOR can write, not on what the epic can finish. RV2-DEBT-218's closure removed the urgency it was found under - returning a view over a locally built dynamic array is legal and safe, so copying is no longer the only way out. When it is taken: decide whether `__clone` is derivable for a struct of clonable members, or whether an explicit `@copy`/`__clone` stays mandatory, and note that `extend` and `Array::slice` both change meaning with the answer. |

| RV2-DEBT-010 | Copied net handles carried only raw native fd/pointer-shaped state: public data-path ops could validate stale in-struct `closed` state before raw syscalls, and reconstructed `{ __opaque = handle }` boxes made reads past the public handle word unsafe. | Epic 10 Task 3 | CLOSED by the stable runtime handle-id contract (`10-tasks/03-debt-010-net-handle-contract.md`, commit `9d1b06c1`). `TcpConn.__opaque` and `TcpListener.__opaque` now carry monotonic runtime handle ids, not OS fds or pointers. Native entrypoints canonicalize through the handle table before reading fd, owner shard, closed state, or fd-registry generation; closed conn/listener handles are removed from the table and stale copies return `NET_ERR_NOT_CONNECTED`. Live canonical conn operations also validate owner-local execution and the owner shard's fd-registry generation. Proof: `TestRuntimeV2NetHandleStaleCopyReusedFD` (`SURGE_SHARDS=1,2,8`) + `TestRuntimeV2NetHandleGuardStaticShape`, wired as `runtime-v2-net-handle-check`; `make runtime-v2-net-handle-check`, `make runtime-v2-accept-check`, `make c-check`, and commit-hook `make check` passed. Residual boundary: handle ids are intentionally not reused and the handle table grows monotonically until a future Surge-visible net-object destructor exists. |

| RV2-DEBT-039 | `format(...)`/`fmt_arg(...)` with a STRING (or string-slice) argument double-frees on the LLVM backend (`format("x={}", fmt_arg("hi"))` → "double free or corruption"; `fmt_arg(s[0..50])` → "free(): invalid pointer"). An int argument (`fmt_arg(42)`) works. Independent of fixnum (the int path is clean) and of range-for; surfaced while fixing range-for (showcase 21's `result_str1[0..50]` head-slice). VM formats all argument types correctly. ROOT CAUSE: `FmtArg = FmtStr(&string) \| …` held a BORROW; the type system collapses a `&string` tag payload to `string`, so drop-emission treats the borrowed handle as an owned string and frees a stack pointer (double-free). `&T` is a Copy type and must never be dropped — the collapse turned it non-Copy. FIXED: `FmtStr` now OWNS its string (`fmt_arg` clones once, `format` appends it in place via `append_fmt_arg` — copy count at parity with the borrow), which is the sound design (borrow-in-union is unsound here; see RV2-DEBT-041). Also hardened `emit_drop_glue.go typeOwnsHeapRec` to skip genuine `&T`/`*T` fields. Showcases 15/21/23 now match the VM; `format(...)` with string args is double-free-clean (valgrind). Residual: format's internal temporaries (slice ranges, concat intermediates) still leak per the general drop-emission gap (RV2-DEBT-040), not a double-free. | Closed (owned FmtArg) | — | `format`/`fmt_arg` with string/slice args run double-free-clean on LLVM, matching the VM (done). |
| RV2-DEBT-071 | WITHDRAWN 2026-07-23, SAME DAY IT WAS FILED — the measurement was right and my attribution was WRONG. The row claimed that arithmetic on inline integers round-trips through the heap via `bi_promote`. It does not: `rt_bigint_add` and `rt_bigint_cmp` (`runtime/native/rt_bignum_int_api.c`) both open with an allocation-free fast path — `fixi_as_i64(a) && fixi_as_i64(b) && !__builtin_add_overflow(...)` returning `bi_from_i64_tagged(r)` — so `bi_promote` is never reached for in-range operands. Verified by direct read after an adversarial review challenged the attribution. THE REAL CAUSE IS LITERAL RE-PARSING, which is already RV2-DEBT-036. Every literal is materialized at runtime by `rt_bigint_from_literal`, which parses the DECIMAL STRING on each evaluation; `parse_uint_string` (`rt_bignum_parse.c` ~76-101) loops per digit doing `bu_mul_small` then `bu_add_small`, each allocating a fresh `SurgeBigUint` and freeing the previous — two allocations per decimal digit, verified by reading the loop. The original measurement decomposes exactly: `while i < 100000 { i = i + 1; }` re-parses a 6-digit literal in the condition (12) and a 1-digit literal in the body (2) = 14 per iteration. CONTROL, measured: hoisting both literals into bindings (`let n = 100000; let one = 1;`) takes the same 100,000-iteration program from `1,400,015` allocations to `17` for the entire run — zero per iteration. So there is no arithmetic defect here; the evidence belongs to RV2-DEBT-036 and is recorded there. Kept as a withdrawn row rather than deleted, because the misattribution had a consequence worth not repeating: it was used, briefly, as an argument that copy-on-write's cost would hide behind existing allocation traffic. That argument is void — after the literal fix an inline loop allocates nothing, so nothing hides. The correct argument for the same conclusion is that retain/release on an inline fixnum is one predictable not-taken branch and never touches the counter at all. | Withdrawn (misattributed; evidence moved to RV2-DEBT-036) | — | — |
| RV2-DEBT-089 | ownership-compile-failure:CF-010. The partial-move gate decided ownership from an UNRESOLVED type and got it backwards. Whether a field read takes its value or duplicates it is decided by asking whether the type is Copy, and `isCopyType` answers false for a type it does not know — so a read whose type never got recorded looked exactly like a move of a move-only value. Member access on an ANONYMOUS record records no type at that point, which is how `let p = { x: 1 }; let a = p.x;` came to be reported as a partial move of an `int`, and `l.__opaque` on a BORROWED extern struct the same way. Introduced 2026-07-28 by Epic 24 step 1 (`12e8dac6`), found 2026-07-29 by chasing why `make golden-check` was red. FIXED: the projection gate is skipped when the read's type is unknown; the whole-binding path is untouched, since it needs no type to know a binding went. The underlying gap — member access on an anonymous record produces no recorded type — is NOT fixed and is a separate question: nothing else consulted that type, so nothing else misread it, but the next rule that does will hit the same thing. Golden rows restored: `sema/valid/user_record_type`, and the `entropy`/`net` false positives in the corpus dump. | Fixed for the gate; the missing type on anonymous-record member access is open | Partial-move gate reading an unresolved type (sema) | Done: a Copy field read of an anonymous record is accepted, and the gate refuses to answer from a type it does not know. Open: anonymous-record member access records its type, so the next consumer does not have to guard. |

The exact benchmark-base disposition remains an explicit owner checkpoint. |
| RV2-DEBT-175 | A union whose live arm is a bare TYPE alternative frees nothing on the native backend. `Erring<T, E> = Success(T) \| E` admits a value into the `E` arm by TYPE rather than by tag (`internal/vm/storage_union.go:149`, `wrapTypeArm`, names this shape). On the native side that arm contributes no drop at all: `typeOwnsHeapRec` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_drop_glue.go:99-110`, `KindUnion`) decides whether a union owns heap by walking `tagCases(id)` payloads, and `emitUnionPayloadDrops` (`:520`) builds the arm switch from the same list — a type alternative appears in neither. So for a union with a type alternative owning a string the answer comes back "owns nothing", no `drop.type<N>` is emitted, and the `drop` the MIR asks for compiles to nothing. Evidence: a twelve-line program returning `Erring<nothing, Error>` with `Error { message = "seeded message", code = 1 }`, consumed by a `compare` that binds nothing, exits 0 and leaks `31 bytes in 1 blocks` from `rt_string_from_bytes`; the emitted module contains no `drop.type<N>` for that union. The VM does not share the defect — `storageReplace` releases the destination first by construction (`internal/vm/storage_ops.go:260-268`). Present both before and after the 2026-08-09 triage; unaffected by it. | Open | Runtime V2 native drop glue | A union's drop walks its TYPE alternatives as well as its tag arms: the twelve-line reproducer is Valgrind-clean, and a row covers a type alternative owning a string, an array, and a nested union. The two predicates that disagree here — what the census counts as owning heap and what the switch emits — must be built from one list rather than two. |
| RV2-DEBT-199 | THE WAITER ROUTING PATH DEREFERENCED A FREED CHANNEL. `rt_waiter_store_for_key` and `rt_waiter_key_shard` (`runtime/native/rt_waiter_route.c`) resolved `WAKER_CHAN_SEND`/`WAKER_CHAN_RECV` by casting `key.id` back to `rt_channel*` and reading `owner_shard_id`, under a comment asserting "channels are never freed and their owner never changes". False: `rt_far_channel.c`'s `release_entry` calls `rt_channel_free`. REPRODUCED 2026-08-11 at `b9f647c5`, ASan-named and deterministic: `heap-use-after-free` READ of size 4 in `rt_channel_owner_shard_id` (`rt_async_channel.c:7`) <- `rt_waiter_key_shard` (`rt_waiter_route.c`) <- `remove_waiter_generation` (`rt_async_waiter.c`) <- `wake_task_with_policy` (`rt_task_park.c`), freed at `release_entry`. THE MECHANISM IS NOT A MISSING PIN - the counter accounting is correct and the original hypothesis was refuted: the anchored dispatch pins at `rt_immediate_on.c` BEFORE the body task exists and holds the pin for the body's life, and every route to `release_entry` gates on `inflight == 0`. The defect is that `wake_task_with_policy` clears `park_key` and enqueues the task UNDER the owner shard lock, releases the lock, and only THEN performs the deferred `remove_waiter_generation` with the captured key. In that window another worker can poll the woken task, run it to completion, unpin and free the channel - so a channel key legitimately outlives its channel by one lock-release window, and the routing helpers treated the key as a live pointer. Two independent lanes reproduced it by different routes, and both reproductions were re-derived by adversarial reviewers who rebuilt the binaries themselves (5/5 use-after-free on the negative control, 5/5 clean on the fix, one reviewer matching the pasted trace down to the heap address). REACHABILITY, corrected: the first write-up claimed only unpin-drops-to-zero could reach `release_entry` with a live pointer-keyed waiter. A reviewer DISPROVED that empirically (3/3) - holding the lease for the body's whole life and dropping it after the body completed and unpinned reproduces the same fault with the free coming from `rt_far_channel_release`, the lease drop, which is exactly what the backend emits for dropping a far channel handle (`emit_instr.go`, `builtins.go`). So exposure is NOT confined to teardown and the stale entry can exist in steady state, where address reuse matters. | CLOSED 2026-08-11 | Epic 23b Wave D step D0.7 | FIXED by making a channel key carry its owner shard instead of pointing at it. `waker_key.owner_shard_id` already existed and was zero for channel keys; `channel_send_key`/`channel_recv_key` - the only two places a channel key is ever built, and both reached with the channel live in hand - now stamp it, and both routing arms read that copy. `key.id` becomes an opaque identity that is never dereferenced. Entry MATCHING is unaffected: every comparison in the runtime tests kind and id only and nothing compares `owner_shard_id`, verified across 12 sites. The shard is fixed before any task can park (`rt_channel_bind_owner_shard` runs between `rt_channel_new` and the mint), so a key built at any later moment stamps the same value. Gate: the reproduction rides `make runtime-v2-waiter-check`, with a negative-control build that restores the dereference and is watched failing. |
| RV2-DEBT-302 | ownership-compile-failure:CF-016. **COMPILING A LIBRARY FILE AS ITS OWN ROOT LOST THE SUBSTITUTION FOR EVERY GENERIC IT REACHES, so fifteen stdlib files that a program compiles fine are refused by the ownership corpus.** The corpus profile compiles each fixture with no entrypoint, and under it `stdlib/http/*.sg` (ten), `stdlib/json/*.sg` (three), `stdlib/hash/hash.sg` and `stdlib/term/ansi.sg` fail with `mono: generic call to array_push / array_reserve / reserve at ... has no authoritative concrete instantiation`. The named calls are all in `core/array.sg` (`:23`, `:27`, `:41`) inside the `extern<Array<T>>` block, and the caller is that same block's method already instantiated with a CONCRETE argument -- `push[byte]`, `reserve[JsonValue]`, `with_len[byte]` -- so the substitution exists and the closure simply has no use-site recorded at that span (instrumented at `internal/mono/rewrite_calls.go:203`: zero `useSites` entries match the call span). **THIS IS NOT A STDLIB SOURCE DEFECT AND MUST NOT BE FIXED IN THE LIBRARY.** Every one of the fifteen compiles to MIR through an importing program -- proven by the new `internal/stdlibgate` gate, green over all sixteen modules -- and by the existing consumers `testdata/llvm_parity/http_*.sg` and `hash_*.sg`. BISECTED to `21a816e9` ("make callable closure authoritative", 2026-08-05): at `f247a4c7` (2026-08-02) the corpus pinned the same 32 stdlib files with only `saturating_cast.sg` allowlisted, so all fifteen produced MIR under this identical profile before that commit. Recorded rather than repaired because the authoritative instantiation closure is the authority for every generic in the language and a speculative repair there is worse than a recorded row. | Open | Monomorphization / instantiation closure | A library file compiled as its own root instantiates the generics its own body reaches, all fifteen fixtures produce MIR under the canonical corpus profile, and the CF-016 group and its entries are removed from the ownership ledger. |

| RV2-DEBT-313 | **`runtime-v2-accept-check` HAS A SECOND INTERMITTENT RED: ALL EIGHT CONNECTIONS ARE ACCEPTED, BUT TWO NON-OWNER USES ARE DENIED AND A CLIENT READS `connection reset by peer`.** Measured on the dedicated gate host at exact `bbfdb3e4`: the unchanged fixture produced 3 resets in 100 independent focused processes; the accept-wait handshake candidate produced the same 3 in 100. Every captured exit trace is materially identical: `accept_owner_total=8`, `global_path_fallbacks=0`, `fd_ready_batches=3` or `4`, `non_owner_conn_denied=2`, and `listener_group_members_closed=4`. This is therefore neither the vacuous readiness assertion nor a regression from the handshake; the server accepts every connection and then refuses exactly two connection operations before the affected clients can read `PONG`. The row blocks an aggregate count even after the fd-readiness row is made deterministic, so it is recorded separately rather than widening that test-only repair into an unproved runtime change. | Closed | Runtime V2 network connection ownership; W8 follow-up | Name the owner transition that makes two accepted connections non-owner at use time, fix it with a deterministic stand red on the revert, then run at least 100 independent focused accept-contract processes and a repeated `runtime-v2-accept-check` with zero resets and `non_owner_conn_denied=0`. CLOSED (2026-08-31). Root: `rt_executor_wake_net_waiters_for_key_on_owner` applied the accept re-own from a batch popped before it took the control lane, without checking the task still held the key — a losing shard overwrote the owner the accept had just placed, stranding the conn on the accepting shard while every task handling it was placed elsewhere. Fix: re-own only a task that still holds the key (`runtime/native/rt_async_waiter.c`). Regression rows: `TestRuntimeV2AcceptStaleSiblingWakeDoesNotReownTheAcceptor` plus `...StandFailsOnRevert` over `internal/vm/testdata/accept_stale_rewake_stand.c`, with `-DRV2_DEBT_313_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` as the proven negative control, wired into `runtime-v2-accept-check`. Evidence: 5000 vs 5000 independent focused processes on `318a2519` vs `5309c273` — 29 runs with `non_owner_conn_denied=2` and 30 resets on the control, 0 and 0 on the fix, counter proven read on 4999/5000. |

| RV2-DEBT-315 | **LLVM UNION CONSTRUCTORS LEFT PADDING AND INACTIVE-ARM BYTES UNINITIALIZED.** The original witness was `emitUnionMaterialiseBareMember`: for the accepted `Erring<T, Error>` layout it wrote the four-byte tag at offset 0 and the active payload at offset 8, leaving bytes 4..7 untouched. The constructor census found the same representation gap in the four constant-tag builders and in the dynamic map `Option` result path. P1 requires deterministic padding and inactive-union bytes. | Closed | Epic 23b LLVM ordinary-destination ABI closeout | CLOSED (2026-09-01). Every LLVM union constructor now starts the layout-owned destination with a full-size byte zero-fill, then writes the active discriminant and payload. Constant discriminants share `emitUnionDiscriminant`; the map path calls `emitUnionStorageInit` before exposing Some's payload address to the runtime and commits the selected tag afterward. Rule-13 reds proved the original bare path lacked the initializer, identified all five direct constant-tag emitters, and rejected an aggregate zero store as a composite register move. | RV2-DEBT-322 | **AN ANCHORED BLOCK'S PIN IS NEVER GIVEN BACK WHEN SHUTDOWN RESOLVES ITS PENDING FIRST.** Found 2026-09-03 by the E3 ownership map, by reading, not by a red. The pin `rt_far_channel_pin` takes at dispatch (`rt_immediate_on.c`, the `EXECUTE_ANCHORED` branch) is dropped on exactly four paths: three dispatch refusals and the reply edge, `rt_remote_task_reply_owner_done`, which the body's completion reaches through `rt_remote_task_pending_take_owner` -- and that lookup requires `status == PENDING && owner_registered`. `rt_remote_task_fail_all_pending` (`rt_remote_task_wait.c`) resolves every pending to `DESTINATION_SHUTDOWN` and clears `owner_registered`, so a body that the exiting carrier then cancels completes without finding its pending, and the pin is never dropped. `rt_executor_request_shutdown` runs `fail_all` and then `rt_far_channel_release_all` in that order, and the sweep declines pinned entries by design ("reclaimed by the owning operation's completion path"), so the entry and the owner-side `rt_channel` it points at outlive the process with `inflight` stuck at 1. No process-exit sweep exists (`rt_far_channel_state_destroy` runs only on init failure), and valgrind counts the entry as still reachable, not lost, which is why no strict-zero row has ever seen it. The teardown row `anchored-owner-teardown-fails-caller-deterministically` checks the caller's status only. E3 made the unpin idempotent and refused an unpin without a pin (`anchor_pinned`, `rt_immediate_on_anchor_unpin`), which is what makes a fix here safe to write: the pin can be given back from a second place without a double decrement. The fix is NOT in E3 because the safe place is not obvious: `fail_all` runs under the control lock while a body may be mid-turn on another carrier holding the cached channel pointer, and an unpin there lets `release_all` reclaim the channel under it; the body's own completion is the right place, which means `take_owner` (or a sibling lookup) has to find a pending that `fail_all` already resolved, and `fail_all` has to keep the owner reference alive for a pinned anchored pending until the body is done. **NARROWED 2026-09-03 (Wave E, E6).** Done, for every body that completes: the sweep leaves a pin-holding body (anchored, remote select) its owner registration and the reference it holds, and the completion finds the pending through the task's own pointer (`rt_task.remote_owner_pending`, set at registration, taken exactly once by `rt_remote_task_pending_take_owner`) whether the sweep resolved it or the caller consumed and unlisted it; the completion then unpins and answers, and the reply the caller no longer waits for is drained. Proved by `commit-vs-shutdown-sweep-keeps-the-record` (live count 0 after the sweep) and its control `RV2_DEBT_322_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` (registration severed: "commit-vs-sweep race leaked a pin"). **What remains, and why it is not done here:** a body PARKED at shutdown -- the anchored teardown row's, on a full channel -- is never polled again: after `ex->shutdown` a carrier runs only the tasks pinned to it (Section 10's shutdown sentence), so that body's completion never runs and its pin stays until the process exits, where the OS reclaims it. The sweep cannot unpin it either: a carrier that popped the body before it saw the flag may be about to run it, and `rt_far_channel_release_all` would reclaim the channel under it. The census reads zero after shutdown only when shutdown runs or cancels every remaining body, which is the scheduler's shutdown contract, not this pending's. | Open | Epic 23b Wave E (E4, the reservation token rides the same pending) | A shutdown-time census: the teardown row asserts `rt_far_channel_debug_live_count == 0` after `rt_executor_request_shutdown` plus the carriers' exit, red before the fix; the body's completion gives the pin back through `rt_immediate_on_anchor_unpin` whether or not `fail_all` resolved the pending first, and a sync-point stand shows a body mid-operation at shutdown finishing on a still-live channel. |

ROOT-CAUSED AND FIXED (2026-07-13, commit `8a31b9b5`): `ready_push_with_policy` (`rt_ready_queue.c`) checked status/enqueued UNLOCKED before taking the owner shard lock and pushed blindly after — a wake in that window enqueues the yielded task, a second worker pops it and stores RUNNING (pops and RUNNING stores serialize on the owner shard lock), and the blind push inserts a duplicate entry whose READY store overwrites RUNNING under the live poll; a third pop takes the duplicate and double-polls. Diagnosis method: the polling byte now carries the poll-entry SITE (`task_polling_enter(task, site)`), so the collision panic names holder+entrant+task snapshot — three probe repros all said worker-loop x worker-loop with status=RUNNING/enqueued=0, which eliminated the cross-shard, inline-child, and N=1-runner hypothesis classes and isolated the only path that stores READY outside the pop-serializing lock. Fix: the push re-validates the gate under the owner shard lock. Regression rows: `SP_READY_REQUEUE_BEFORE_LOCK` sync point + `TestRuntimeV2LifecycleReadyRequeueWakeRaceProof` (deterministic positive) and `...NegativeControl` (`-DRV2_DEBT_027_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` removes the re-validation and MUST double-poll — non-vacuity proven), wired into `runtime-v2-lifecycle-check`. | RV2-DEBT-040 | For-loop iteration via the generic iterator protocol leaks on the LLVM backend, independent of range-for: each `iter_next` boxes an `Option<T>` on the heap every iteration and the iterator struct (array cursor or range cursor) is allocated once, but drop-emission reclaims neither. Pre-existing and systemic: `for x in arr` over a 3-element array in a 2000-iteration outer loop leaks 10,000 blocks. CLOSED 2026-07-19 (Epic 20 Task 2, codex-implemented, lead-reviewed + independently valgrind-verified). ROOT CAUSE: `normalizeIterFor` desugars for-loops AFTER the sema pass that computes drop obligations, so the synthetic `iter`/`next` locals never acquire any — and a generic type-driven drop would have been WRONG anyway (the cursor is a hand-rolled 32-byte tagged blob whose declared HIR type is a type-checking fiction; a deep drop of the consumed Option box would double-free the moved-out payload). FIX: dedicated release channel — `StmtIterRelease`/`InstrIterRelease{Place, Cursor}`, synthesized in `normalizeIterFor`: the step box frees shallowly right after payload extraction (and on the exhausted branch) using its own concrete `Option<T>` layout; the cursor frees once after the while (covers exhaustion + break) with the FIXED 32/8 iterator layout, plus an injected release before every real `return` inside the body (recursive walker; `StmtRet` value-yields deliberately excluded). VM backend: no-ops (its iterator objects are ordinary GC'd heap values). ADVERSARIAL REVIEW ROUND closed two reopened leaks before commit: (a) a real `return` inside an expression-embedded block (compare-as-value arm, nested block expression) bypassed the injected cursor release — the walker now descends into every expression that can carry a return and discriminates real returns from implicit block-value yields (`IsImplicit`), which the review itself had conflated; (b) the cursor-release guard only trusted WidthAny int/uint Range elements and silently skipped every other kind — generalized to pointer-shaped-by-default (strings/structs/heap handles always release; only fixed-width numerics and bool match `emitRangeIterInit`'s no-alloc fast path and skip). The review's array-desugar diagnosis was DISPROVEN empirically (a `string[]` for-loop emits zero `__range` calls — arrays never classify as ranges), so the guard hole was real only for genuine Range<T> values. GUARDED RESIDUAL: a genuine Range<T> of fixed-width numeric/bool element keeps the historical leak (its fast path returns the SurgeRange pointer itself; the case is rejected at the surface today). Proof: valgrind "All heap blocks were freed" on 2000-iteration array (30,029/30,029), string[] (30,016/30,016), and expr-return (40,023/40,023) repros; `TestRuntimeV2IterProtocolReclamationCensusBalanced` (8 probes: bound var, break, return, expr-return, string[], struct[], stored range, d(2000)-d(1) differential; drop-reclamation gate). Found en route: RV2-DEBT-054 (while-body composite let leak), RV2-DEBT-055 (`for _ in` ICE), RV2-DEBT-056 (struct-element array boxes). | Closed (dedicated iter-release channel; loop-count-independent valgrind + census gate) | — | Done: `for` over arrays and WidthAny ranges is allocation-balanced on LLVM; the guarded fixed-width-range residual is recorded here and unreachable from surface syntax. |
| RV2-DEBT-041 | Soundness gap: the borrow checker does not track a `&T` borrow stored in a union/struct that escapes its scope. `tag R(&string); fn make() -> Ref { let l = "x"; return R(&l); }` compiles with NO diagnostic — `&l` dangles after `make` returns; the VM catches it only with a runtime guard (`VM1001 used before initialization`) and LLVM silently UAFs. Lexical lifetimes + no full borrow-checker (LANGUAGE.md) mean borrows stored in data aren't lifetime-tracked. Surfaced while fixing RV2-DEBT-039 (which sidesteps it by making FmtArg own its data). CLOSED 2026-07-16 by disallowing the pattern (references are second-class values): sema rejects reference types stored in ANY user aggregate position — struct fields (`SEM3138`, decl + declared tuple/array type exprs in `resolveTypeExprWithScope`), tag payloads (`checkTag`), tuple/array/map LITERAL elements (`type_expr_values.go`) — and rejects returning a borrow that provably roots in frame-local storage (`SEM3139` in `checkBorrowEscapeOnReturn`: `return &local`, `let r = &l; return r`, `return &owned_param`; a `&T` param returned as `&T` stays legal via the existing call-result loan keep-alive). `@intrinsic` core types (BytesView) keep reference/pointer fields — the runtime pins them. `stdlib/json` was the one live user (param-derived refs in `Parser`/`ViewParser`) and was rewritten to own its data (byte[]/BytesView moved in). EXTENDED same day after an external (Codex) audit confirmed two more live escape routes: (3) `Channel<&T>` formed and `ch.send(&l)` shipped a borrow to a task that outlives it (LLVM died with an emit ICE, VM refcount-masked) — now `Channel<&T>` is rejected at type formation ("channel payload", covers `make_channel::<&T>` and annotations) and `checkChannelSendValue` rejects reference values outright (defense in depth); (4) returning a task handle whose spawn borrowed frame-locals (`return spawn worker(&l)`, incl. the laundered `let t = …; return t` form) let the caller await after the frame died — VERIFIED UAF (VM panics VM3301, LLVM silently reads freed memory) — now rejected (SEM3139 via TaskTracker spawn-args provenance); awaiting in-scope stays legal. Residual (documented, low-risk): a generic instantiation WRITTEN as a type (`Holder<&string>`) is accepted as an annotation but unconstructable (literals collapse `&T`→`T` and then mismatch); a ref returned out of a CALL result whose loan the table can't root (deep laundering through multiple frames) is not caught; a task handle escaping via ARG-PASS or container (not return) with borrowed locals is not provenance-checked — full escape analysis stays future work. Rebinding a ref local to an inner-scope borrow is blocked incidentally by the mutation guard (SEM3019) plus the no-default rule for uninitialized `&T` bindings. Policy helper: `internal/sema/reference_containment.go`. | Closed (pattern disallowed; kindness-first diagnostics) | — | Storing a borrow in an aggregate is a compile error at the declaration/literal (SEM3138, incl. channel payloads); returning a provably-local borrow or a local-borrowing task is a compile error (SEM3139); channel send of a reference is a compile error; goldens `ref_stored_in_*`, `ref_return_escapes_local`, `channel_payload_ref`, `task_borrows_local_escapes`, valid `ref_param_returned_as_ref`, `task_borrow_awaited_in_scope`. |
| RV2-DEBT-042 | Soundness gap: coercing a borrow of a non-Copy type into an OWNED parameter emits an aliasing load + move on the LLVM backend, not a clone — so callee and caller both free the source (double-free). `fn f(x: string) {} … let s = "hi"; f(&s);` lowers `&s` to `Deref(Ref(s))` (a shallow handle copy), moves it into `f`'s owned `x` (freed at `f`'s exit), then the caller also frees `s`. The VM masks it by refcount-sharing. Surfaced via showcase 16 (`.find/.count/.replace(&needle)`); the STDLIB instances were fixed at the source by making those read-only string params `&string` (Option B — a borrow now binds directly, no clone, no move), but the underlying coercion remains unsound for any user code that passes `&owned` to an owned non-Copy param. Note: passing an owned VARIABLE (or literal) to a `&T` param already auto-borrows correctly; only the reverse (borrow → owned param) is broken. CLOSED 2026-07-16 (reject, kindness-first): at HEAD the plain-function and generic paths already rejected the coercion; the two LIVE holes were (1) METHOD calls — `typeKeyCandidates(&T)` exposes the bare `T` key for receiver lookup and `methodParamMatchesWithSubst` accepted it for owned params (`b.eat(&needle)` compiled and double-freed on LLVM) — now Copy-gated in `type_expr_calls_method_match.go`; and (2) STRUCT LITERAL fields — `ensureStructFieldType` compared `valueType`s, stripping `&` (`Box{ &l }` into an owned `string` field double-freed) — now rejected. Both report `SEM3137` ("takes ownership … only a borrow") with move/`.__clone()` hints via the shared `reportBorrowIntoOwned`; the plain-call path's old bare "expected string, got &string" upgrades to the same message. Copy-elem borrows (`&int` → `int`) still coerce fine. | Closed (rejected with SEM3137) | — | `b.eat(&needle)` and `Box{ &l }` against owned non-Copy targets are compile errors with move/clone hints; owned→`&T` auto-borrow, `&T`→`&T`, and Copy-elem deref coercions keep working; goldens `borrow_into_owned_method_param`, `borrow_into_owned_struct_field`, valid `owned_method_param_move_clone_ref`. |
| RV2-DEBT-043 | Ergonomic over-rejection (SAFE — the checker rejects valid code, it never accepts invalid code): the borrow checker forbids a shared borrow of `x` in the same call that takes `&mut x`, e.g. `merge_sort(&mut a, &mut tmp, 0, len(a) to int)` → "cannot take shared borrow of 'a' while an exclusive borrow is active". The `&mut a` argument activates its exclusive borrow when type-checked and stays active across the sibling `len(a)` argument. It is actually safe: arguments are fully evaluated (so `len(a)`'s shared borrow ends) before the callee ever dereferences the `&mut`. Workaround: read the length into a local first (`let n = len(a) to int; merge_sort(&mut a, &mut tmp, 0, n)`) — applied to showcase 10_sort_merge. A correct fix is two-phase borrows: a mutable argument borrow is "reserved" during argument evaluation and only "activated" at the call, so a sibling borrow consumed to produce a VALUE arg (`len(a)`) does not conflict while a sibling borrow passed INTO the call (`&a`) still does. A naive "activate mut-arg borrows last" is UNSOUND (it would also accept `f(&mut a, &a)`), so this needs real analysis — a dedicated effort, not a sweep fix. CLOSED 2026-07-16 with two-phase argument borrows (design soundness-reviewed externally, incl. the explicit-arg question Rust sidesteps): a `&mut x` written DIRECTLY as a call argument (plain calls and method calls share the wiring) begins RESERVED — it lives in the place's shared list, so it blocks writes, moves, and other mutable borrows (a second `&mut a` argument rejects immediately, reservation-vs-reservation) while coexisting with shared reads like `len(a)`'s temp borrow. When the whole argument list is evaluated the reservation ACTIVATES into a normal exclusive borrow; activation rejects any borrow still alive at the call — an explicit `&a` sibling, or a loan kept alive through a ref-returning call (`ident(&a)`, generic or not). `&mut` nested deeper inside an argument keeps activate-immediately semantics (it can be used before the call). Method receivers were already de-facto two-phase (receiver borrows after args, live-arg check intact — `b.absorb(&b)` still rejects). Error-recovery hygiene: abandoned argument lists discard their reservations and eligibility. Dynamic indices stay conservative (index place segments are indistinguishable, so `f(&mut v[i], &v[j])` overlaps and rejects). Showcase 10_sort_merge restored to the natural `merge_sort(&mut a, &mut tmp, 0, len(a) to int)` and passes both backends. Verified over a 12-case matrix (goldens `two_phase_mut_arg_sibling_reads` / `two_phase_mut_arg_aliasing`); no existing golden shifted. OBSERVED ADJACENT GAPS (pre-existing, NOT two-phase, candidates for future rows): (1) `&mut`-param reborrow aliasing `fn f(a:&mut T){ g(a, a) }` passes both mut and shared reborrows of one place into a call with no diagnostic; (2) anonymous-fn captures are not loan-tracked at all (`take(&mut a, fn() -> int { return a[0]; })` compiles on clean HEAD too); (3) borrows held across `.await` live in the async resume frame — statically sound today because references cannot cross task boundaries, but the frame-storage question predates two-phase and covers every borrow kind. | Closed (two-phase argument borrows: reserve during args, activate at the call) | — | `f(&mut v, len(v))`, nested value-producing reads, and method forms compile; `f(&mut a, &a)`, `f(&mut a, &mut a)`, `f(&mut a, ident(&a))`, sibling moves/writes all still reject; showcase 10 uses the natural form. |
| RV2-DEBT-044 | Async awaitable-Task reclamation gap (leak, not a safety bug): every `await` leaks its awaitable. `checkpoint().await()` in a loop leaks ~88 bytes (48-byte `rt_task` + 40-byte inner) plus a small per-poll block PER ITERATION — `busy(1000)` awaited to completion loses 107,221 bytes / 3,070 blocks (~3/iter); the async/05_cancellation showcase loses megabytes because the busy loop runs tens of thousands of iterations before cancel.
