One further observation, recorded as UNVERIFIED because the measurement that produced it is the confounded one: the caller emits its own `drop` for the moved binding at the enclosing scope's end (that drop is present in the MIR both before and after the Epic 24 step-0 fix, so it is not new), and it is not established what that drop actually reclaims. Re-measure before relying on it. CONSEQUENCE for Epic 24: step 0 cannot state the generated protocol as a checkable invariant until this is resolved — the invariant would assert an ownership fact that no site in the compiler actually implements, and a MIR verifier written on top of it would be decorative. Also blocks the wider fix an adversarial review asked for (an explicit `FieldReadMoveOut` vs `FieldReadCopy` mode on `FieldAccess`, since `RValueField` today carries no transfer mode and its object operand is always `OperandCopy`). NOT a live correctness hazard at HEAD: no leak, no invalid access, on either backend, in the shapes measured. ANSWERED 2026-07-28, same session: the reclaiming site is the CALLER's own scope-exit drop of the captured binding, proven by valgrind's free'd-at stack (the second free is `drop.type1502` in the crossing poll; the FIRST is the same glue called from the caller's poll). That drop exists because sema never marks a crossing capture as moved — `observeMove` is called for `blocking` captures and for far-task await, but NOT for `on`/`spawn on` captures — so the caller keeps the binding live and drops it at scope end. That is why nothing leaks and why a body-side drop double-frees. It is safe today only because the affine must-await lifecycle keeps the handle's await inside the capture's scope, which orders the caller's drop after the body's last read. The remaining question is no longer 'what frees it' but whether the caller SHOULD be the owner at all, which is RV2-DEBT-081. CLOSED 2026-07-28 by Epic 24 step 0b, which moved the ownership rather than the drop: the capture is now a real move (RV2-DEBT-081), so the caller stops dropping it and the crossing body starts. Both halves had to change together — either alone is a double free or a leak, which is exactly why the first attempt failed. Proven by valgrind across eight shapes: unconsumed capture, consumed capture, mixed-branch (one exit hands the value on, the other does not), body-local live at `ret`, loop and non-loop, the immediate `on` form, and a CANCELLED far task whose body never runs. Zero definitely-lost, zero invalid reads/writes/frees in all eight. | Closed (ownership moved to the crossing body; step 0b) | Crossing capture ownership (sema + mir lowering) | The reclaiming site for an owned capture is IDENTIFIED and named in one place; the crossing capture protocol is stated as an invariant a test asserts; a body that neither consumes nor hands on an owned capture is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends, with a consuming body as the negative control. |
| RV2-DEBT-081 | Moving an owned value into an `on` / `spawn on` crossing is NOT A MOVE for the caller, so the ownership rule the crossing surface advertises is not enforced. `checkOnCaptures` classifies a capture as `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` and the MIR unpacking treats it as transferred, but no site calls `observeMove` on the capture expression (`internal/sema/on_crossing_capture.go`; contrast `typeExprBlocking` at `internal/sema/type_expr_flow.go`, which does). Measured 2026-07-28, native backend, all accepted with NO diagnostic: (a) reading the binding after the crossing; (b) MUTATING it — `j.id = 99` after `spawn on distributed { ret j.id * 100 + 6; }` prints caller 99 / body 406, so the two sides disagree about the same value; (c) MOVING IT AGAIN — `consume(own j)` after the capture compiles, i.e. one owned value is moved twice. Consequence beyond the missing diagnostic: replacing a captured heap field in the caller (`j.note = build_note("z-")`) ABANDONS 219 bytes, because the shipped state still references the old string and nothing frees it. No use-after-free was produced in the shapes measured — the affine must-await rule keeps the await inside the capture's scope, which orders the caller's drop after the body's last read, and attempts to defer the await past that scope are rejected by the must-await-or-return check. So the current safety rests on a lifecycle rule rather than on ownership. This is the same defect class Epic 24 exists to fix (an ownership rule that sema does not track), found while resolving RV2-DEBT-079, and it decides that row: if the capture becomes a real move, the caller stops dropping it and the BODY must, which is exactly the change that double-frees today. CLOSED 2026-07-28, same session it was opened, by Epic 24 step 0b. `checkOnCaptures` now calls `observeMove` for a `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` capture, and `registerCrossingBodyOwnership` gives the body the drop obligation the caller just gave up. Reading the binding after the capture, moving it a second time, and capturing the same owned value into TWO crossings are all rejected as use-after-move; a Copy capture stays usable and an owned capture nobody touches afterwards stays accepted (`internal/sema/crossing_capture_move_test.go`). Far-handle captures are deliberately untouched: the anchored form USES the destination handle inside the body it was captured for, so consuming it would reject every anchored channel operation. RESIDUAL, and it is NOT crossing-specific: a field WRITE after a move is still unchecked — `j.id = 99` after the capture is accepted, exactly as `consume(own j); j.note = "z";` is accepted with no crossing in sight, because the moved-set is symbol-keyed and the write goes through a projection. Epic 24's own steps 3 and 5 close that, and `TestCrossingCaptureFieldWriteIsNotYetCaught` is the tripwire that fails when they do. | Closed (residual field-write gap owned by Epic 24 steps 3/5) | Crossing capture move tracking (sema) | A capture classified `CrossingCaptureMoveOwned` marks the binding moved; a later read, mutation or second move reports use-after-move; whichever side then owns the value is stated once and proven by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends, with a consuming body and a mutating caller as controls. |
| RV2-DEBT-083 | A partial move whose result ESCAPES corrupts memory — it is not merely the alias RV2-DEBT-077 records. Two shapes measured 2026-07-28 on the native backend while landing Epic 24 step 1, both printing the RIGHT answer and exiting 0 while valgrind reports invalid reads and invalid frees: (a) a by-value receiver handing out a heap field, `fn get_bar(self: Foo) -> string { return self.bar; }` — seven distinct invalid-read contexts plus an invalid free over 8 calls; (b) a value built from the fields of one that dies immediately, `return Req { method = head.method, target = head.target };` — invalid reads plus TWO invalid frees. The string leaves with the escaping value while the container's drop takes it as well. The alias case RV2-DEBT-077 records is protected by `markAliasedBinding` (a projection-read binding never drops, leaking rather than double-freeing); neither shape above goes through that, because the value escapes by return or into a literal rather than staying in a binding. The borrowed receiver `self: &Foo` is a TYPE ERROR (`expected string, got &string`), so the damage is confined to by-value receivers and struct-literal field reads. MITIGATED, not fixed, by Epic 24 step 1: the gate rejects both shapes, so they are unreachable from source; the 8 stdlib sites that used them were rewritten to borrow at the use site or to copy explicitly (`stdlib/http/interim_copy.sg`, a file step 8 deletes). The underlying defect is the same one the epic exists to fix. CLOSED 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 8: both shapes compile with `own` written and are covered by the valgrind census; `stdlib/http/interim_copy.sg` is deleted and its six callers take their fields directly. | Closed (the escaping partial move is a real transfer) | Partial moves that escape (sema) | `own o.field` performs a real transfer, the container is invalidated for that place only, and each shape above is proven leak-free and double-free-free by allocation census plus valgrind — with the interim copies in `stdlib/http` deleted and the direct field reads restored. |
| RV2-DEBT-084 | A field read whose BASE is a TEMPORARY segfaults. `let e = mk().inner;` where `Inner` owns heap crashes on the native backend; under valgrind it is invalid reads plus invalid frees. The temporary is materialized, the binding takes a projection of it, and the temporary's statement-end drop frees what the binding now holds. Measured 2026-07-28 at Epic 24 step 1, PRE-EXISTING (identical with the step-1 gate reverted). NOT gated by step 1, and that is a gap rather than a decision: `resolvePlace` does not resolve a call as a place, so the rejection never sees it. This is the shape the epic's contract row 11 and trap 3 both require to STAY ACCEPTED, on the reasoning that the base is a temporary nobody else can be using — which is correct as an ownership argument and is exactly why it should WORK. As it stands, 'stays accepted' means 'accepts a use-after-free'. Epic 23's owning-temp marking (`consumeTempCandidate`) is called on this path and does not cover it. Pinned as a sentinel by `TestPartialMoveOutOfTemporaryIsNotGated`. MITIGATED 2026-07-29, not fixed: the shape is now REFUSED (`SemaPartialMoveUnsupported`) rather than compiled into a crash, so the segfault is unreachable from source. Blast radius measured before gating — ONE site in the whole corpus (`s.bytes().len`, showcase 13), and that one is a false positive of the probe rather than a real move, so the corpus is byte-identical with the gate in. Deliberately NOT fixed here: the MIR shows the fix is step 6's mechanism applied to a temporary rather than to a named binding — `drop L2` must release the temporary's RESIDUAL instead of all of it, exactly as a partially-moved binding must. Fixing it earlier means building a special case that steps 6 and 7 then replace. It does NOT block steps 6/7 (it consumes them); it DOES block step 8, whose contract row 11 requires `f().inner` to stay accepted — which today would mean accepting a use-after-free. STILL OPEN after Epic 24 step 8, and deliberately: the rest of step 8 landed for NAMED bindings and the temporary did not. The reason is sharper than the row's original guess. Steps 6+7 give a residual drop to a value with a NAME, computed from that symbol's moved-set; a temporary has no symbol, so `TempDrops` would have to carry a plan the way `DropLocal` now does and sema would have to compute a residual from an expression's TYPE plus the projections taken out of it. That is a separable piece of work, not a tail of steps 6/7. The refusal moved to its own code (`SEM3179`) so it is not confused with the two rules that are permanent, and it is SAFE: a compile error naming the way around it, not a miscompile. Contract row 11 is therefore NOT satisfied by step 8. CLOSED 2026-07-29, later the same day, once the residual walk stopped being about symbols. `residualProbe` separates the question (what does the moved-set say about this path) from the walk, so a binding answers from its interned moved-set and a temporary answers from the paths taken out of it; `TempDrops` carries a plan the way `DropLocal` does and MIR's temp frames carry it to the flush. The plan is COMPLETE for a nameless value because a temporary cannot outlive its statement or be mentioned again, so it is computed when the temp frame closes. MIR now shows `drop L2.label; drop_shallow L2` where it showed `drop L2`. Gated by the `from_temporary` row of `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (valgrind, native, strict zero) plus a sema trio: bare, `own`-written, and a nested path all accepted, with an element off a temporary still refused. Contract row 11 now means accepted AND correct. SEM3179 survives, narrowed to a projection off a value whose release is not ours to narrow — a control-flow expression's result — where refusing is the conservative answer. | Closed (a temporary releases its remainder, like any other value) | Partial move out of a temporary (sema temp ownership + mir temp drops) | `let e = f().inner;` compiles, runs, and reports strict-zero definitely-lost with no invalid access on both backends; contract row 11 is restated as 'accepted AND correct' and pinned by valgrind rather than by compiling. |
| RV2-DEBT-085 | Eleven `TestLLVMParity` http rows fail to BUILD, not to run: `member "next" of module "stdlib/http" is not public or not a function`. `next` is declared `pub fn next(self: &mut ByteStream) -> Option<byte[]>` inside `extern<ByteStream> { ... }` (`stdlib/http/parser.sg`), so a `pub` method in an extern block is apparently not reachable as a module member the way the parity programs reference it. Noticed 2026-07-28 while looking for an acceptance test for the Epic 24 step-1 stdlib rewrites, and PRE-EXISTING (identical with every Epic 24 change reverted). Consequence for other work: the whole http parity suite is dark, so no end-to-end test currently covers `stdlib/http` — which is exactly where RV2-DEBT-083's memory corruption lived undetected. Any change to `stdlib/http` is presently unverifiable beyond compiling and hand-written valgrind probes. Affected rows: http_parse_request, http_parse_request_strict, http_response_bytes, http_response_formats, http_context_helpers, http_helpers, http_cookie_helpers, http_query_helpers, http_chunked_request, http_server, http_connect. Out of Epic 24's scope; recorded because it is load-bearing for anyone trusting the http suite. MOSTLY CLOSED 2026-07-29, and it was one contradictory line. `publicModuleValueExports` deliberately falls back to receiver-bound exports when a module has no free function of that name — that fallback is what makes `http.next(&mut body)` work, a method called with an explicit receiver — and `moduleFunctionResult` then filtered receiver-bound exports out again, discarding exactly the candidates the helper had just selected. The filter was unreachable whenever free functions existed, so it only ever fired on the fallback it was defeating. Removing it restored the build for every affected row. The rows themselves then hit the Epic 24 step-1 gate on `req.body` / `req.path` — real partial moves in the test programs — rewritten to advance the body reader in place (`&mut req.body` off a mutable rebinding) or to copy the field until step 8 makes `own req.path` a real move. RESULT: 10 of the 11 rows build and pass. The eleventh, `http_connect`, fails at BASELINE with `cannot send 'listener' of @nosend type 'TcpListener' to spawn` — a separate language-rule question, unrelated to module exports or to partial moves, and left alone. So `stdlib/http` has end-to-end coverage again, which matters beyond this row: it is the coverage whose absence let RV2-DEBT-083's memory corruption live in the request parser unnoticed. | Closed for the export defect; `http_connect` remains on an unrelated @nosend rule | Module exports for extern-block methods (project/module resolution) | Done for the export bug. The residual is `http_connect`'s @nosend violation, which needs its own decision: either the test program is wrong or the rule is too strict. |
| RV2-DEBT-086 | `@drop o.inner` — an explicit drop of ONE place — is settled as legal by Epic 24 (settled decision 4) and sema is ready for it, but NEITHER BACKEND CAN PERFORM IT, so it stays rejected until Epic 24 step 7. Measured 2026-07-29 by implementing it at step 5 and then backing it out: on the native backend a projected `InstrDrop` is a SILENT NO-OP (`placeBaseType` refuses a projection and `emitInstrDrop` swallows the error), so `@drop o.inner` releases nothing and the field is reclaimed later by the binding's ordinary scope-exit drop — valgrind clean, but the statement did not do what it says. On the VM it is worse: `execInstrDrop` IGNORES the projection and drops the whole local, so `@drop o.inner; return o.label;` panics `VM3301: use-after-free: local \"o\" used after drop` on the very next line. One statement, doing nothing on one backend and corrupting on the other. Sema now refuses it with a diagnostic naming the real reason, replacing `drop target must be a binding` — which was true of nothing and sent the reader after an addressability problem that did not exist. BLOCKED ON, not merely waiting for, step 7: the projected drop has to arrive together with step 6's path-carrying drop obligations, or the explicit drop and the binding's scope-exit drop both release the same field. CLOSED 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 8. `@drop o.inner` is an explicit move into nothing: it records the place as moved, so a later read of the field or of the container is a use-after-move and the sibling stays readable, and the container's exit drop reclaims the residual. Gated by the `explicit_drop` row of `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (valgrind, strict zero) plus a sema pair: the sibling read is accepted, the dropped place is not. | Closed (a projected drop releases that place and only that place) | Explicit projected drop (sema surface + both backends) | `@drop o.inner` releases that field and only that field, the container's residual drops exactly once, and both are proven by allocation census plus valgrind on both backends — with a sibling read after the drop as the must-still-work control. |
| RV2-DEBT-087 | A compare-arm payload binding never acquires a drop obligation, so a heap payload bound and used locally is abandoned. Repro is six lines: `tag Bytes(byte[]); type Payload = Empty() \\| Bytes(byte[]);` then `compare body { Bytes(b) => b.__len() to int; ... }` over an owned scrutinee loses one block per evaluation (96 bytes in 4 blocks over 4 iterations, valgrind, native). Measured 2026-07-29 while landing Epic 24 step 8, and PRE-EXISTING — byte-identical on the tree with every step-8 change reverted, so it is not a partial-move defect. It surfaced only because step 8 removed an accidental cover: the arm used to be followed by a whole drop of the container the scrutinee came from, and that drop walked into the union and freed the payload. Removing that drop was correct (it was a double free against the container's own residual drop — see the step-8 record), and it left the payload with no owner at all. This is RV2-DEBT-058's shape re-measured on a fresh path and adjacent to RV2-DEBT-052/075/078, all in the compare-arm release model, which Epic 24 lists as out of scope. Not a correctness hazard beyond the leak: no invalid read or free on either backend. The Epic 24 census row works around it by handing the payload to a sink that takes ownership, which keeps that row measuring the double free it exists for. CLOSED 2026-07-30. Two changes, and the second only became visible by measuring. (a) An arm's pattern bindings now get the scope-exit obligation every other binding has: a drop scope opens before the pattern is typed so the bindings land in it, `registerComparePayloadDroppables` registers them, and what the arm still holds is read off that scope before it closes — a payload the arm moved onward is skipped by liveDroppables, which is why the ubiquitous `Success(x) => x` shape hid this for so long. They ride the channel the one-sided arm obligations already use, so an arm can owe both. (b) `wrapArmDrops` emitted those drops BEFORE the arm's value, which was fine for the obligation it was written for (a binding a sibling arm moved, which the result never touches) and fatal for this one: an arm like `Bytes(b) => b.__len()` reads the very binding it owes, so the payload was freed and then read — an invalid read of a null slot, measured under valgrind. The drops now ride the `ret` as DropsAfterValue, which is the contract `return` already carries for exactly this reason. PROOF: the six-line repro goes from 96 bytes in 4 blocks to `in use at exit: 0 bytes in 0 blocks`, 0 memcheck errors; the Epic 24 census row that had been dodging this by handing its payload to a sink now binds and reads it directly, so it covers the leak and the double free together. Golden moved by exactly one line pair — an arm's `drop L4` after the arm's value instead of before, the only place in the corpus where an arm carried a drop — stable across two regenerations. What this unblocks: RV2-DEBT-052's recorded residuals (mixed payload arms and the whole-value binding arm errred toward leaking because 'without binding-level obligations there is no safe owner to hand the box to'), and `tagPayloadReleaseShape`'s shallow-envelope premise, which assumed the binding would drop and was until now simply false. RV2-DEBT-058 is the same row found earlier from the other side. | Closed (an arm's payload binding drops after the arm's value) | Compare-arm payload bindings (sema drop obligations) | An arm-bound heap payload used locally reclaims at arm end; the Epic 24 census row can drop its `sink_bytes` workaround and bind the payload plainly; RV2-DEBT-058's residual arm shapes are revisited with it. |
| RV2-DEBT-088 | `ret` carries a drop list that three maintenance sites never learned about, all of them the `return` counterpart's neighbours. `cloneStmt` (internal/mono/clone.go) did not copy `RetData.DropsAfterValue`, so every instantiation of a generic function shared ONE list — and `ApplyStmt` writes type substitutions into it, so the last instantiation would decide which drop glue all of them use; `ApplyStmt` (internal/mono/subst_apply.go) never substituted the drop TYPES at all, which is what picks the glue; and `remapStmt` (internal/driver/hir_stdlib.go) did not remap the drop SYMBOLS, so an imported `ret` would drop whatever now occupies those slots. `StmtReturn` does all three at each site; `ret` acquired its drop list later, at Epic 24 step 0b, and these were missed. Found 2026-07-29 by the Epic 24 step-9 audit, PRE-EXISTING, and LATENT rather than live: reaching any of them from source needs a non-empty drop list on a `ret` inside a generic or imported function, and the two shapes that produce one resist combining — a value-producing block expression records no drops at all (its locals leak by design, the recorded safe-direction bound in `appendBlockExprEndDrops`; measured at 392 bytes over 8 iterations and identical on the pre-epic tree), and a crossing body records them but could not be given a type-parameter-typed local. FIXED in the same pass, all three sites now mirroring their `return` neighbour. Gate: `internal/mono/ownership_metadata_test.go` asserts clone and substitution directly for every payload that carries ownership information, with `return` as the row that must always pass (it is what says the test measures a real difference) and negative controls that fire on each half of the `ret` gap separately. The stdlib remap half is fixed by inspection against the line above it — a `ret` does exist in `stdlib/bytes` (`peek_line_lf`) but its locals are Copy, so its list is empty and no measurement can distinguish the fix today. | Closed (all three sites mirror `return`) | Exit drop lists for `ret` (mono clone/subst, stdlib HIR import) | Done: a generic function's `ret` drop list is per-instantiation and type-substituted, an imported `ret`'s drop symbols are remapped, and a unit gate fails if a field is added to any ownership payload and these functions are not updated. |
| RV2-DEBT-090 | An index expression whose `__index` takes `self` BY VALUE reported itself as a use-after-move. The receiver is consumed while the expression is being typed, and the projection read check ran afterwards — so it found the move this expression had just made and rejected `b[0]` outright, on a program with no second read anywhere. `let b = Box { value = 1 }; return b[[1..2]];` went from compiling on main to `SEM3130 cannot use b[?]: b was moved`. Introduced 2026-07-29 by Epic 24 step 3 (`504fcb1c`, which added the place-aware check), found 2026-07-29 chasing golden. FIXED by asking the question BEFORE the expression is typed, which is also the order evaluation has: the place is read at that point, and whatever the operation then does with it happens after. Negative control run: a genuine use-after-move on an index base (`consume(b); b[0]`) still reports. Second half of the same row: `checkPlaceUseAfterMove` routed a WHOLE-binding move through the place-aware wording whenever the READ was a projection, so `b[0]` after `b` went entirely explained a partial move that had not happened; it now keys on the move alone, which is what step 3's own note said it should do. Golden rows restored: `hir/indexing_ranges`, `sema/valid/range_literals`, `sema/invalid/index_range_missing_overload`. | Closed | Place-aware use-after-move ordering (sema) | Done: an index whose operator consumes its receiver compiles, a real use-after-move on the same shape still reports, and a whole-binding move is worded as one. |
| RV2-DEBT-091 | Taking an element out of a TUPLE was refused alongside array elements, which left ordinary code with no spelling at all. Variant A's reasoning for refusing elements is that the survivors of one leaving cannot be listed — true of an array, whose index is chosen at runtime over a container whose length is not part of the question, and NOT true of a tuple, whose arity is fixed and which is only ever indexed by a literal. A tuple is a struct with positional names. Because `resolvePlace` mapped both to one segment kind, Epic 24 step 1 (`12e8dac6`) refused both, and step 8 kept the refusal while lifting it for struct fields — so `let s: string = t.1;` was rejected as a move AND had no accepted form, making a tuple holding anything move-only unreadable. Measured against main: `let inner: (int, int) = nested.0;` and `return t.1;` both compiled there. FIXED: a tuple element is its own place segment carrying its position (`PlaceSegmentTupleIndex`), interned distinctly, enumerable in the residual walk beside struct fields, and projected in MIR as the positional field the rest of the lowering already uses. Two plumbing gaps closed with it: the residual plan silently dropped the tuple projection and emitted a drop of the WHOLE container, and the tuple read never carried the move-out mode so a taken element was counted as a second holder. Also found here: `own T` was accepted for `T` only when the ids matched, so a structurally identical but separately interned tuple made the marker look like a type error (the RV2-DEBT-057 class); it now asks assignability. Gate: two rows in `TestRuntimeV2PartialMoveReclaimsOnlyWhatItStillHolds` (a tuple element with its sibling surviving, and a path mixing field and element), valgrind strict zero; contract rows for accept, sibling survival, mixed path, and the use-after-move control, with the ARRAY refusal kept as the row that says the two are not the same. | Closed | Tuple element as an enumerable place (sema/hir/mir) | Done: a tuple element can be taken with `own`, its sibling survives, its residual is reclaimed exactly once on both backends, and an array element stays refused with the reason that actually applies to it. |
| RV2-DEBT-093 | SOUNDNESS, shipped and live for one day: a `compare` arm whose RESULT is one of its own payload bindings both handed the payload onward AND freed it. `compare v { Payload(s) => s; Empty() => ""; }` — the ordinary spelling — aborts with `free(): double free detected` on the native backend when the result is a fresh allocation, and reads freed memory (3 memcheck errors) when the caller looks at the value first. Introduced by `a8351f82`, whose parent `2df134e1` is clean, bisected on a 12-line reproducer. That commit gave an arm's payload bindings the scope-exit obligation they never had, correctly, and reasoned that `liveDroppables` would skip a binding the arm moved onward — but NOTHING marked that move. `observeMove` is called on a block's TAIL expression and on a `return` operand; an arm's result is the same consuming position and no walk reached it, so the binding stayed live in the arm's drop scope and earned a drop it had already given away. An arm that uses its payload LOCALLY is unaffected, which is why the commit's own measurement looked right. FIX: observe the move on the arm's result, skipped when the compare's value is DISCARDED — there the result goes nowhere, nobody downstream owns it, and the binding's obligation is exactly what reclaims it; an independent review confirmed that guard and found no other missing move on the compare path (guards reject arm-binding moves already, `finally` arms and nested compares go through the same arm-result path). WHY IT REACHED main WITH A GREEN GATE: two existing e2e rows (`TestRuntimeV2CompareScrutineeReleaseCensusBalanced`, `TestRuntimeV2DropUnionCastReclamation`) DID catch it and were red on the tree, but `make test` sets `SURGE_SKIP_TIMEOUT_TESTS=1` and every native e2e test skips on it — see RV2-DEBT-094. GATE: `TestCompareArmReturningItsPayloadOwesNoDrop` (`internal/sema`) asserts the returning arm owes no obligation while a locally-using control arm owes exactly one; it is in a package `make check` actually runs, costs milliseconds, and its negative control is reverting the `observeMove`, which fails it with 2 obligations instead of 1. Measured: reproducer goes from abort to `in use at exit: 0 bytes in 0 blocks` with 0 memcheck errors, and both e2e rows go green. FIRST FIX WAS WRONG AND A REVIEW CAUGHT IT: observing the move at the ARM decides a question only the ARM'S CONTEXT can answer. `let out = compare v { Payload(s) => s; ... }` consumes the result, but `peek(compare v { ... })` with a `&string` parameter only BORROWS it — there nobody downstream owns the payload, and taking the obligation away leaked one string per evaluation (measured: 16 blocks over 16 iterations, and clean with the move reverted). The `!compareDiscarded` guard covered the discarded case and missed the borrowed one, which is the same mistake one step along. CORRECTED SHAPE: the arm KEEPS the obligation by default — the leak-over-double-free direction, while the answer is unknown — and the consuming context takes it back, via `releaseArmResultObligations` called from `observeMove`, which is exactly the set of positions that consume a value. Nested compares recurse; an arm whose result is a BLOCK needs nothing, because a block's tail already observes its own move while the arm is typed. Verified across all five contexts at strict zero: consumed by `let`, borrowed by a call, discarded, used locally inside the arm, and a nested compare. The gate grew a third arm for the borrowed shape, and its negative control now reports 3 obligations instead of 2. | Closed | Compare arm result move | Done: an arm that returns its payload binding neither leaks it nor frees it twice, pinned by a sema gate that runs in `make check`. |
| RV2-DEBT-095 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a TERNARY moves a value into its result without anyone observing the move, so a binding handed to `cond ? a : b` is both consumed and dropped. Measured 2026-07-30 on a 16-line reproducer: `let picked: string = i < 2 ? a : b;` gives 8 memcheck errors in 4 contexts — `Invalid read of size 8` and `Invalid free() / delete / realloc()`, i.e. use-after-free AND double free. Confirmed pre-existing: identical on `2df134e1`, before the compare-arm work of RV2-DEBT-093. Same mechanism as that row and found by an independent review of its fix: `typeExprTernary` (`internal/sema/type_expr_values.go`) types both branches and calls `consumeTempCandidate`, but never `observeMove`, so the `let` bindings it consumed keep the scope-exit obligation they gave away. The review names the select arm result (`internal/sema/type_expr_select.go`) as structurally identical; that one is NOT yet measured and should be before it is claimed. WHY THE FIX IS NOT ONE LINE: only ONE branch runs, so observing the move on both would mark both bindings moved and LEAK whichever branch was not taken. `compare` already solves exactly this with per-arm moved-set snapshots plus `oneSidedObligations`, which drops a binding live on this path and moved on another; a ternary has none of that machinery. So the correct fix is to give the ternary (and select) the same per-branch treatment, not to add the missing call. A naive one-line fix would trade a double free for a leak — an improvement under this codebase's leak-over-wrong-free rule, and still not the answer. CLOSED 2026-07-30, and the scope grew twice while measuring. FIRST: the same hole exists in `compare` for a binding declared OUTSIDE it — `compare c { true => a; _ => ""; }` where `a` is an enclosing `let` was 16 memcheck errors, which RV2-DEBT-093 did not cover because that row was about an arm's OWN payload binding. SECOND: a nested ternary written the way people write one, `x ? (y ? a : b) : c`, went unrecognised because the branch is a GROUP expression and the payload accessor does not see through parentheses. FIX: a ternary and a compare are the SAME shape for this — several branches, exactly one runs, and whichever it is hands its result outward — so both go through one `consumeBranchResults`, called from `observeMove` like the RV2-DEBT-093 retraction, because only the consuming context knows whether the value is consumed at all. Each branch is walked through `observeMove` (so a nested choice settles itself first and this one reads the result), the union of the branch moved-sets becomes the state after the expression, and each branch is given `oneSidedObligations` against what the OTHER branches moved — the existing compare-arm channel, landing on `ArmDropsExpr`, which `wrapArmDrops` already applies to any expression. That is what stops the fix from trading the double free for a leak of the untaken branch. Groups are peeled by `unwrapGroups`. Verified at strict zero across: ternary with two bindings, one binding and a literal, fresh call operands, borrowed result, nested in the true branch, nested in the false branch, nested on both sides, a ternary inside a compare arm, a compare inside a ternary branch, a compare arm returning an outer binding, two arms returning different outer bindings, and the RV2-DEBT-093 payload shapes consumed and borrowed. GATE: `TestTernaryBranchesMoveAndCompensate` (`internal/sema`) asserts BOTH halves — each of the two consumed branches owes exactly one compensating drop — so it fails on a fix that moves nothing (0 obligations, the pre-fix state, double free) and equally on one that moves both and compensates neither (0 obligations, leak). Its negative control reports 0 across 0. Goldens unchanged. A REVIEW OF THE FIX FOUND A THIRD SHAPE, which measurement confirmed and which is now fixed too: a branch can move something while it is TYPED — `cond ? pass(a) : b` hands `a` to a by-value parameter — and the branches were typed without isolating their moved-sets, so the second inherited the first's moves and nothing noticed `a` was still live on the path where `pass` never ran. `typeExprTernary` now snapshots and restores around each branch and records the same one-sided compensation, which is what `compare` has always done across its arms. Measured on that shape: 17 memcheck errors in 3 contexts at the parent commit (a double free AND a leak) -> 0. NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-096: a compare or ternary whose result is only BORROWED leaks what the taken branch produced — measured identical before this work where it overlaps, so it is older and has a different cause. | Closed | Branch result move | A value moved into a ternary or select result is dropped exactly once on every path, pinned by a memcheck-clean reproducer and a sema-level obligation assertion. |
| RV2-DEBT-096 | A `compare` whose result is only BORROWED leaks a binding an arm returned from OUTSIDE the compare. Measured 2026-07-30: `peek(compare i < 8 { true => a; _ => ""; })` with `peek(x: &string)` and `a` an enclosing `let` loses 136 bytes in 8 blocks over 16 iterations — exactly the 8 iterations that take the arm returning `a`. Pre-existing and NOT caused by the branch-move work of RV2-DEBT-095: byte-identical with that change stashed, so the cause is older and separate. Note the shapes AROUND it are clean, which is what makes it narrow: the same compare CONSUMED is clean, the same borrow of an arm's OWN payload binding is clean, and a borrowed ternary is clean. So something on the borrow path consumes the binding without giving the value an owner, rather than the arm-result treatment being wrong — the RV2-DEBT-095 machinery deliberately does nothing here, because a borrowed result moves nothing. Likely neighbours to read first: `consumeTempCandidate` and the implicit-borrow path (`internal/sema/implicit_borrow.go`), which calls `observeMove` itself. ALSO SEEN on a TERNARY, which widens the row beyond compare: `peek(cond ? pass(a) : b)` leaks 8 blocks over 16 iterations — exactly the 8 that take the `pass(a)` branch, i.e. the borrowed TEMPORARY the taken branch produced, not the binding. RV2-DEBT-095's branch isolation halved this one (16 blocks -> 8) by reclaiming the untaken side, so what remains is purely the borrowed result. That both spellings leak only the value the TAKEN branch produced, and only when the result is borrowed, is the shared symptom to explain. ROOT-CAUSED AND PARTLY FIXED 2026-07-30, and the symptom description above was too narrow: it is not about BORROWING, it is about the choice expression's value having no owner at all. Discarding it leaks the same way. MECHANISM, and it was written down as a deliberate bound: `noteTempCandidate` (`internal/sema/temp_drops.go`) flags evaluations that PRODUCE an owned value so a statement-end drop reclaims them, and control-flow expressions were excluded wholesale — "their value can forward a PLACE from any arm... fresh values built in arms leak when the outer value is discarded — the safe direction, recorded as a bound". Both halves of that are true, which is why the rule cannot simply be reversed: a branch that forwards a place must NOT be released (use-after-free through its owner), and a branch that built its value must be. So the question is asked PER BRANCH: a choice expression owns its value only when EVERY branch minted one. FIX: `branchMintsItsValue` + `choiceOwnsItsValue`, consulted from `noteTempCandidate` for `ExprTernary`/`ExprCompare`, asked BEFORE the branch candidacies are consumed because consuming is what erases the evidence. SOUNDNESS POINT FROM REVIEW, which changed the predicate: being a pending temp candidate is necessary and NOT sufficient — a CAST is flagged yet reads its source without consuming, and an identity cast lowers to the same pointer, so `c ? (a to float) : (b to float)` would look like two fresh values while both alias a live binding. The kind must say the value was MINTED, so the test admits call / aggregate literal / string literal / non-assigning binary and excludes cast, unary and index, each of which has a forwarding spelling. Measured: the all-branches-mint shapes go from 16 leaked blocks per 16 evaluations to strict zero, borrowed and discarded alike; the forwarding controls and the consumed regressions stay at zero. A SECOND REVIEW ROUND found the rule too narrow in one direction: a branch that is itself a choice ALREADY PROVEN to mint on every branch has the same standing as a call, and rejecting it by kind left `a ? (b ? make() : make()) : (c ? make() : make())` unflagged — measured 16 leaked blocks, now 0. Inner choices are settled before the outer asks, so the answer is simply looked up. GATE: `TestChoiceOwnsItsValueOnlyWhenEveryBranchMints` (`internal/sema`) pins the rule from every side — never flagging reports 0 and leaks, always flagging reports 2 more than it should and double-frees, dropping the nested case reports 1 short, and only the per-branch rule with nesting reports the expected 2. It carries a nested FORWARDING shape too, so "recurse into nested choices" cannot be read as "always recurse". MIXED, DISCARDED CASE FIXED 2026-07-30, and it needed no new mechanism: a discarded choice has no consumer to hand the candidacy to, so each branch simply KEEPS its own and frees what it built at its own region's end — where it was materialized, so the drop is dominated. The forwarding branch was never a candidate and keeps forwarding. `cond ? a : build()` as a statement goes to strict zero. STRUCTURAL FINDING that bounds this: the same trick does NOT work for a discarded `compare`, and trying it REGRESSED an already-clean shape. A compare is normalized into a block whose arms deliver by `ret`, and `flushTempDropsForRet` deliberately KEEPS the value an exit delivers rather than freeing it — so an arm's own temp survives the arm. Ternary and compare therefore need different treatments here, which was not visible before measuring. BORROWED MIXED TERNARY FIXED 2026-07-30, and NOT by the continuation pushing the review recommended — a cheaper design turned up while scoping that one. A BOOLEAN GUARD: `owns = false` before the choice, raised by the branches that BUILT the value, and the release emitted under it. Strictly better than duplicating the consumer into the branches, which would also reorder side effects (`f(g(), cond ? .. : ..)` moves `g()` inside a branch), and better than the null-sentinel design, which the VM blocks by refusing to drop an uninitialized slot. The guard needs nothing new: the choice's result is assigned on EVERY path already — only who owns it varies, which is exactly what the guard records — and it rides the same `TempDrops` entry, so a context that turns out to CONSUME the value removes both at once and cannot leave a guarded drop behind. NESTED CASE, and it took two rounds: a nested choice does not count as minting for its parent — claiming it raises the parent's guard on the paths where the inner FORWARDED, a double free — and it must not earn a release of its own either, because that fires at the end of the enclosing BRANCH, before the join copies the result, which is a read of freed storage. The answer is that the guard PROPAGATES INWARD: the parent owns the value, and the inner's own minting branches raise the parent's guard, since they are the only ones that know something was built. A review found the second half; my own matrix missed it because its conditions VARIED, so the faulty path was not always reached — the review's used constants. Constant-condition variants are now part of the shape matrix. A THIRD round found the rule was still binary where the question is TERNARY: a branch either mints on every path, mints SOMETIMES (it is a guarded choice), or forwards. A yes/no test answers "no" for the middle case, so a choice with such a branch on BOTH sides owned nothing at all while both inner values had already been taken as branch values — every building path leaked (`peek(p ? (q ? build() : a) : (r ? build() : a))`, 8 blocks over 8 iterations). Sometimes -minting now makes the enclosing choice own AND guard without raising the guard itself. TWO SELF-INFLICTED DEFECTS, both the SAME trap and both caught by measurement: `NoLocalID` is -1 while a zero value is 0, a REAL local — first as an uninitialised struct field (a bool stored into a string slot), then as a `tempDropEntry` literal elsewhere that did not name the new field (an `if` on a pointer, which broke three native rows that `make check` and the valgrind matrix both passed). Fixed the second time by removing the trap rather than the instance: a separate `guarded bool`, so the zero value means no guard. Measured: 10 shapes at strict zero — both orientations of borrowed mixed, nested mixed inside mixed, and seven controls. GATE: the ownership test now pins THREE outcomes (unconditional / guarded / none) with two negative controls — never guarding leaks, and claiming a guarded nested choice double-frees. MIXED `compare` CLOSED TOO, and the `ret` delivery is what shaped the fix: a compare is normalized into a block whose arms deliver by `ret`, so there is no branch body left for the lowering to annotate by position the way a ternary's is. The arm therefore SAYS SO in the tree — `ExprRaiseReleaseGuard` wraps a minting arm's result, evaluates to its operand and raises the guard — which reuses the ternary's mechanism unchanged. Both mixed compare shapes, borrowed and discarded, go to strict zero, as do the cross-construct nestings (a ternary in a compare arm, a compare in a ternary branch). SEPARATE defect uncovered here and filed as RV2-DEBT-098: an arm returning its OWN PAYLOAD binding frees it while the result is still borrowed — byte-identical before this work, and hidden all session by a borrow probe that never dereferenced. The review's recommended answer is path-sensitive lowering — push the consumer's continuation into each arm, so a fresh arm builds, uses and drops in place while a forwarding arm just uses — which fixes mixed without cloning and respects the arm-frame invariant. A blanket "choice results are owned" rule was considered and rejected: it needs a clone rather than a retain for a forwarded `string`, changes `let x = c ? a : b` from moving to cloning, and cannot be implemented at all for a move-only resource. | Closed | Borrowed compare result | Done: a choice expression releases what its branches BUILT, on exactly the paths that built it, for both ternary and compare and in every context. |
| RV2-DEBT-097 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: an IDENTITY cast produces an ALIAS while being classified as a freshly produced value, so releasing its result frees storage a live binding still holds. Measured 2026-07-30: `let x: float = ...; let y: float = ...; cond ? (x to float) : (y to float);` as a discarded statement, then reading `x` and `y`, reports `Invalid read of size 4` — byte-identical with the RV2-DEBT-096 work stashed, so it predates it. MECHANISM: `noteTempCandidate` (`internal/sema/temp_drops.go`) flags every `ast.ExprCast` as producing an owned value, but `lowerCastExpr` reads its source with `consume=false`, and a cast whose source and target types agree lowers to the same pointer. So the flag says "fresh value nobody owns" about something a binding owns. Found while root-causing RV2-DEBT-096, by an independent review that offered it as a counterexample to a predicate based on the temp-candidate flag alone; the counterexample as first written does not compile (borrowing a temporary is rejected earlier), and the DISCARDED spelling above does, and reproduces. RV2-DEBT-096's fix deliberately excludes `ExprCast` from the kinds it trusts, so it neither depends on nor worsens this — but the flag itself is still wrong for a cast in every OTHER consumer of it. DISCUSSED AND DECIDED 2026-07-30, so this is ready to work. MEASUREMENT FIRST, which settled the fork: the CONSUMED form is broken too (`let b = a to float`, then reading `a`), and the sema flag does not govern that — consuming removes it. So the defect is in the LOWERING, not the classification. `lowerCastExpr` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_ops.go`) reads its source with `consume=false`, an ALIAS, and then calls `markOwningTemp` UNCONDITIONALLY — while the comment two lines above it says the marking is for a cast that CHANGES REPRESENTATION. The code does not match its own stated intent, and the missing condition is the bug. Measured across contexts: identity cast discarded, consumed by a `let`, and as a call argument all report `Invalid read`; a WIDENING cast (`int` to `float`) and no cast at all are clean. DECISION (owner): fix at the lowering — mark the temp as owning only when the cast actually changes representation. Explicitly REJECTED the alternative of making the intrinsic honour the call contract with a retain/clone, even though `to` is designed as a MAGIC METHOD (`a to T` lowers to `a.__to(target)`; intrinsic for built-ins, `extern<Foo> { fn __to(...) }` for user types) and that alternative would have kept one semantics for both. The owner's reasoning is a general principle: it is FINE for primitives and the operations on them to be cheaper at runtime than user types, and a user `let b = a to Bar` will be cheap enough with good optimization. THE ASYMMETRY MUST BE DOCUMENTED — that is part of the fix, not an afterthought. Note the fix is NOT "never mark a cast temp as owning": for a user `__to` the marking is correct, since that is a real call returning an owned value. The predicate is whether the lowering actually produced a fresh value. Identity-ness is decidable in sema: `to` on a type PARAMETER is rejected today (`SEM3015`), so it cannot become identity only after monomorphization. DELIBERATELY OUT OF SCOPE, both by the owner's decision: (1) an "unnecessary conversion" diagnostic — it looked obvious until the user-`__to` case came up, since `extern<Foo> { fn __to(self: Foo, target: Foo) -> Foo }` should arguably be banned yet might carry real normalization logic, and the owner will return to that question themselves; (2) constant overflow on `to` (`max_value::<int32>() to int8` crashes) — accepted behaviour and a future ENHANCEMENT, explicitly not debt. ALSO SEEN and to be resolved with the fix: the MIR for a discarded identity cast frees the same temp TWICE (`drop L3` twice), which may be a consequence of the alias or an independent defect of the temp channel. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `575f075d`, with the doubled statement-temporary release in `12d67483`). MEASUREMENT REFINED the decided mechanism: `markOwningTemp` is NOT what released the alias. Every temp holding a reference-counted scalar is registered for a release at `newTemp`, the single point where temps are born, so the cast temp owed a reference the moment it existed while `lowerCastExpr` filled it with a bare alias — which is why the decided predicate (own it only when the cast CHANGES REPRESENTATION) was right about the rule and off by one mechanism. The fix applies the same rule one level up: a cast whose source and target resolve to the same type mints no temp and emits no cast at all, and reads its operand with the CALLER's intent, so `a to float` lowers exactly like `a`. Sema agrees, which the close condition demanded: an identity cast is no longer a producer, and `unwrapTempCandidate` sees THROUGH it so consumption reaches the operand that really produced the value. That second half closed a shape nobody had measured — `let x = 1.5 to float` left the literal's candidacy unconsumed and the statement's release freed the block the binding had just taken; broken identically at the parent commit, so it was older than this work. Rows measured 0 memcheck errors after, red before: discarded, `let`-bound, argument, literal-bound, literal-discarded, literal-through-a-ternary, and a 16-iteration loop; controls (widening `int to float`, no cast at all) unchanged. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2IdentityCastKeepsItsSourceOwned` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks) and `TestIdentityCastSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter, which REFUSES a released slot instead of nulling it — the native backend cannot see a release too many). The doubled `drop` was a second registration, not an alias effect: `lowerOwnedTempExpr` registered its temp explicitly on top of the automatic one, so `2.5;` as a discarded statement panicked on the interpreter. Documented asymmetry: `docs/LANGUAGE.md` §6.6. NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-099: a borrowed `&float` is released by the operations that read it. | Closed | Cast produces an alias | A discarded or borrowed identity cast is memcheck-clean, and the cast's temp-candidate classification agrees with what its lowering actually emits. |
| RV2-DEBT-098 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a `compare` arm that returns its OWN PAYLOAD binding frees it while the caller is still BORROWING the result. Measured 2026-07-30: `peek(compare mk(i) { Payload(s) => s; Empty() => ""; })` with `peek(x: &string)` that actually READS its argument reports `Invalid read of size 8`, 16 errors over 16 iterations — byte-identical before and after the RV2-DEBT-096 work, so it is older and independent. MECHANISM: RV2-DEBT-093 gave an arm's payload bindings a scope-exit obligation, and `releaseArmResultObligations` retracts it when the compare's value is CONSUMED. A BORROWED result is not consumed, so the obligation stands and rides the arm's `ret` as `DropsAfterValue` — the payload is freed right after the arm delivers it, and the borrower then reads it. The retraction is correct for what it was written for; what is missing is that a borrow keeps the value alive past the arm. HOW IT HID: my own probe for borrowed shapes was `fn peek(x: &string) -> int { return 1; }`, which never dereferences, so every measurement of this shape read clean for a whole session. A borrow probe MUST read the borrowed value — that is the methodological fix, and the matrix now uses `len(x)`. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `abd33b34`). The row's mechanism held exactly. The fix is TWO halves that must land together, and the row's own history says why: an earlier attempt dropped the obligation alone and leaked one string per evaluation, which is what the retraction-on-consumption design was working around. (1) An arm whose result IS one of its own payload bindings no longer owes that binding a release — it answered with it. (2) That arm now counts as MINTING the compare's value, so the compare owns the result and releases it when the statement ends, which is the owner the leak was missing. Answered from the arm's OBLIGATIONS rather than its pattern, which keeps a borrowed subject out of it: a payload bound from `compare *slot` earns no obligation and so is never handed out. Measured 0 memcheck errors and 0 definitely-lost, red before: borrowed (16 invalid reads → 0), consumed, discarded, returned across a function boundary, and an arm that BUILDS from its payload (`Payload(s) => s + "!"`), where the payload stays the arm's and the built value is the compare's. MIR proof: the Payload arm's `drop` is gone and the release now sits after the borrower's call. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2ComparePayloadOutlivesItsArm` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks), `TestComparePayloadSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter), and the sema assertion `TestCompareArmReturningItsPayloadOwesNoDrop`, which was UPDATED: it pinned the old borrowed-arm obligation, and now pins both halves — one arm obligation (the payload that never leaves its arm) and exactly one statement-end release (the borrowed compare's own value). NOT fixed here and filed as RV2-DEBT-100: a compare over a BORROWED subject takes its payload by move. | Closed | Borrowed compare payload | A compare arm's payload binding outlives a borrow of the compare's result, pinned by a probe that dereferences. |
| RV2-DEBT-099 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: reading a BORROWED reference-counted scalar releases the value the borrow points at, so the owner's block is freed while the borrow is still live. Measured 2026-07-31 at `71fab2dd` and byte-identical after the identity-cast work, so it is older and has a different cause: `fn peek(v: &float) -> int { if v > 0.0 { return 1; } return 0; }` called on a live `float` binding reports 22 memcheck errors in 17 contexts, and `fn peek(v: &float) -> float { return v to float; }` reports 53 in 36. The shape that is CLEAN is the one that binds first (`let c = *v;`), which is the tell: MIR mints a deref temp for the pointee (`L2 = (* copy L0)`), every temp of a reference-counted scalar is registered for a release when it is born, and the deref filled it with an ALIAS — the same contract violation as the identity cast, in the other operation that forwards storage it does not own. `retainExtractedValue` is the existing repair (a field read, an element read and a borrowed union payload all take it); a deref through a REFERENCE wants it too, and a deref of an `own` pointer deliberately does not, since there the single reference transfers. Found while measuring RV2-DEBT-097 — the first borrow probe written for that row read every shape as broken, including the controls, because the probe itself was the defect. Costs more than it looks: it makes `&float` unusable in any function that READS it, which is most of them. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `a5a9e795`). The row's diagnosis held and was HALF the contract; the other half only became visible once the first was fixed. (1) The deref temp now takes a reference through a REFERENCE operand (`retainExtractedValue`, `derefsABorrow`), and a deref of an owning pointer deliberately still transfers. (2) That alone traded the use-after-free for a LEAK in one shape, `let c = *v;`, which had looked clean only because two defects cancelled: sema marks a binding initialized by a projection read as an ALIAS that never drops, which is right for a value living inside its container and wrong for a reference-counted scalar — a copy at the surface, a counted block underneath. The alias marking now excludes that type (`projectionReadAliasesItsSource`, applied at both the `let` and the reassignment site), so the binding is a real owner. Half (2) closed a leak with NO borrow in it at all: `let f = b.value` on a `float` field leaked 24 bytes per evaluation (192 bytes over 8 iterations), measured before any of this work. Rows now 0 memcheck errors and 0 definitely-lost, red before: compare, arithmetic, `to string`, a forwarded borrow, a deref into a by-value parameter, a bound deref (used and returned), a field read in a loop, and 8 repeated borrows. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2BorrowedScalarSurvivesItsReaders` (native, valgrind, strict-zero leaks — the leak column is asserted BECAUSE a use-after-free fix can trade into it) and `TestBorrowedScalarSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter). The rows use `float` deliberately: an `int` outside the inline range leaks on its own under RV2-DEBT-035, which would drown the measurement. | Closed | Borrowed scalar released by its reader | A borrowed `float` can be compared, cast and printed inside a callee with the caller's binding still live, memcheck-clean, and the deref temp's ownership agrees with what the reference contract says it holds. |
| RV2-DEBT-100 | SOUNDNESS, pre-existing: a `compare` over a BORROWED subject treats the subject as its own, so the arm frees a payload the caller still owns. Measured 2026-07-31 at `71fab2dd` and byte-identical after the RV2-DEBT-097/098/099 work, so it predates all of it: `fn look(slot: &Slot) -> int { return compare *slot { Payload(s) => peek(&s); Empty() => 0; }; }` called on a live `Slot` binding reports 32 errors in 2 contexts — an invalid read AND an invalid free. MECHANISM, from the MIR: `compare *slot` derefs the borrow into a temp (`L3 = (* copy L0)`) and MOVES it into the compare's subject (`L2 = move L3`), so the subject slot holds the caller's union box while claiming to own it; the arm then takes the payload by move (`L5 = move L6`) and releases it at the arm's end (`drop L5`), freeing storage the caller's binding still points at. Same family as RV2-DEBT-099 — a deref temp filled with an alias — but for a COMPOSITE, where the repair is not a retain: a borrowed subject's payloads must be read as borrows, and the arm must own nothing it did not build. Found while gating RV2-DEBT-098; deliberately left OUT of that fix, whose predicate is obligation-based and therefore correctly does nothing here. FIXED 2026-07-31 (commit `ef3f30eb`). The row's MIR trace named the subject move; the defect that actually reached user code was the PAYLOAD half of the same question, and it is far from exotic: `format("hello {}", fmt_arg(name))` reported an invalid read AND an invalid free on every call with a string argument, measured identically at `71fab2dd` (RV2-DEBT-039 closed the earlier double-free on this same user-facing path by a different mechanism; this is a second, independent one). MECHANISM: sema registered every arm payload binding as a droppable, so a `string` payload read out of a BORROWED union owed a release, and the union belongs to whoever the borrow points at. The comment on `registerComparePayloadDroppables` claimed a borrowed extraction retains — true only of reference-counted scalars, which is exactly what `TagPayloadData.SubjectBorrowed` exists for. FIX: the obligation is skipped when the subject is only read, with three exceptions that still own what they took — a refcounted-scalar payload (retains), a `@copy` union (CLONED into the compare even through a deref), and a subject that is not a union at all (MOVED into its binding; a review caught this one, one leaked string per evaluation, and it is the single place the sema classification deliberately parts from HIR's `compareScrutineeOwnership`, which asks about the envelope box rather than the bindings). Rows measured 0 invalid ops and 0 definitely-lost, red before: a borrowed payload read, a borrowed payload appended to a string, a `@copy` union through a deref, a borrowed `float` payload, a plain non-union subject, an owned subject. Gates: `TestRuntimeV2BorrowedComparePayloadStaysTheOwners` (native, valgrind) and `TestBorrowedComparePayloadSurvivesTheHeapSanitizer` (interpreter); the existing scrutinee-release census covers the `@copy` row from the other side. | RV2-DEBT-146 | NONBLOCKING ingestion duplication compensated at selection time. `core/intrinsics.sg` is ingested under more than one module path, so `string` genuinely carries several `@intrinsic fn __clone` records that differ only in `ModulePath`. Left alone they are equally specific rivals and every `string` clone reports SEM3185; ten `testdata/golden/core_stdlib/*.sg` fixtures did. `collapseBuiltinOperations` (`internal/sema/clone_canonicality.go`) folds them by requiring both records to be Builtin and to agree on name, receiver, full signature and attributes — safe, since user code is never Builtin, and stable, since the input is body-key ordered. It is still a compensation: the duplicate records are created at ingestion and every whole-program selector that looks at builtin callables has to know about them. Found by the B3A clone review (P3-1, 2026-08-05). CLOSED 2026-08-06. A census of the merged catalog first corrected the row's own mechanism: TWO ingestion shapes produce the duplicates, not one. The stdlib prelude reads `core/intrinsics.sg` under the module path `core` while a build rooted inside that directory walks the very same physical file under its own path — and, separately, `testdata/golden/core_stdlib/` is a COPY of `core/`, whose records no physical-file identity could ever fold. Compiling `testdata/golden/core_stdlib/string.sg` carried 996 builtin records for 498 operations: every builtin operation in the program duplicated, not only `__clone`. The row's own framing (records "differ only in `ModulePath`") is exact, and explains why only builtin RECEIVERS conflict: a nominal type ingested twice gets two type identities, so its two records dispatch on different receivers and were never rivals, while a builtin type has one program-wide identity that both records claim. FIX: `dedupeBuiltinOperations` (`internal/sema/builtin_operations.go`) drops a builtin record whose operation is already present, at `MergeInstantiationGraphs` — the merge that first brings records from two modules together, which is where the duplicates are created. A builtin declaration carries no body; it names an operation the compiler implements, so however many modules spell it out there is one operation to select. Records fold only when they agree on receiver, signature and attributes — the exact agreement `sameBuiltinOperation` required — so the 123 records whose types genuinely differ between ingestions all survive, and a user declaration is never builtin. `collapseBuiltinOperations` and `sameBuiltinOperation` are deleted. Post-fix census: 621 records, exactly one `string.__clone`, pinned by `TestBuiltinOperationIsIngestedOnce` over both ingestion shapes; the golden corpus regenerates byte-identically (tree hash unchanged across the fix). | Closed | Epic 23b B3B (operation-plan registry) | Done: builtin extern blocks are deduped where they are ingested, so one compiler operation produces one callable record; `collapseBuiltinOperations` is then deleted and the core_stdlib golden fixtures stay byte-identical without it. |
| RV2-DEBT-147 | NONBLOCKING pre-existing hole in "one canonical `__clone` body per concrete T". The alias-override dispatch tier ranks a declaration on an alias as strictly more specific than one on the alias target (`compareCallableSpecificity` orders by receiver distance first, pinned by `TestDeferredCallableResolverPrefersAliasOverride`), so if BOTH an alias and its target declare `__clone`, the alias and the target bind DIFFERENT bodies. Selection is still deterministic and program-wide for each of the two types, so nothing miscompiles; the hole is that an alias is otherwise presented as transparent, and a reader who believes that will expect one body. The tier predates B3A and is shared with every deferred method, so B3A neither introduced nor could narrow it alone. Found by the B3A clone review (P3-2, 2026-08-05). CLOSED 2026-08-08 by the owner's ruling: an alias may not declare a magic method its target already declares. The divergence was first reproduced end to end — `type Handle = Leaf` with `__eq` on both prints "Leaf says equal" and "Handle says different" for one type — which also corrected the row's own reach: the tier bites every compiler-invoked hook, not `__clone` alone, and for `__clone` specifically the alias body is DEAD in the `clone(&x)` form (canonical selection resolves the receiver first), so the visible divergence runs through operators, `to`, indexing, `for in`, `abs`, `len` and boolean context. FIX: `checkAliasMagicDeclarations` (`internal/sema/alias_magic_methods.go`) refuses the alias's declaration where it is written, before any use site exists, with the new `SemaAliasMagicRedeclared` (3187), a note naming the rival declaration and a manual-review `surge fix` that deletes the alias's body. The refusal is scoped two ways. It covers only hooks the COMPILER reaches for (`isCompilerInvokedMagicName`), so an ordinary method overridden on an alias stays legal and `TestDeferredCallableResolverPrefersAliasOverride` still pins the tier unchanged; and two declarations rival each other only when their non-receiver operands agree through the alias chain, so `__to` declarations converting to different targets coexist and the `docs/LANGUAGE.md` §6.6 `Meters`/`Feet` example still compiles. The rule is stated in `docs/LANGUAGE.md` §6.9 and pinned by seven cases in `internal/sema/alias_magic_methods_test.go`. | Closed | Language decision: alias transparency for magic methods | Done: refused at declaration time with SEM3196, notes and a `surge fix`; the alias-override tier stays intact for ordinary methods and is documented in `docs/LANGUAGE.md` §6.9. |
| RV2-DEBT-148 | Post-merge finalization publishes decisions per source file, and its single-file fallback is gated on `res.File != nil` (`internal/driver/finalization_publication.go`). `finalizeParallelFileResults` leaves `File` nil when `fileSet.HasFile` is false, and then nothing publishes for that result at all — silently, for BOTH entrypoint bindings and direct clone bindings. The clone symptom is now fail-closed: HIR refuses to lower a `clone` whose request sema recorded and whose answer never arrived (`internal/hir/lower_expr_calls.go`, `TestLowerRefusesACloneThatWasNeverAnswered`), so a lost publication is an internal compiler error instead of an ordinary call to a function named `clone`. The publication gap itself is untouched, and the entrypoint side has no equivalent guard. Predates B3A; found by the B3A clone review (P2-3, 2026-08-05). FIXED 2026-08-06 by settling the id space at its source: `DiagnoseDirResult` names its file twice, once per id space, and `finalizeParallelFileResults` derived `File` from the source-space `FileID` while deriving `DiagnoseResult.FileID` from `ASTFile`, so the two could name different files. The AST file is the one tied to the semantic result being finalized, so `parallelResultOwner` now settles both fields from its span — the same span the module-record path already reads — and publication asks `finalizationOwnerSource` for the owning identity instead of gating on whether a `*source.File` handle happens to be present. An identity no record and no file set can answer is an error naming the id, the id space, and what was searched, on both sides of the seam. Measured on the unfixed tree, the defect shape published no clone symbols at all AND left the entrypoint binding on the non-owning file of the module. | Closed | Finalization publication id space (driver) | Done: `TestParallelPublicationSurvivesADriftedSourceFileID` drives `finalizeParallelFileResults` with a source-space `FileID` the file set never held and asserts the owning file receives both an entrypoint binding and a direct clone binding while the non-owning file receives neither, and `TestPublicationRefusesAnUnknownOwningSourceIdentity` asserts an identity unknown to every record and file set fails closed naming both id spaces and publishes nothing. |

On the production path the scan is dead: `rewriteCloneCall` refuses to rediscover an implementation when a closure exists (`TestCloneRewriteRefusesToRediscoverAnImplementation`), because a symbol-table scan could pick a different body than the program-wide canonical selection. Deferred from the B3A clone workstream so closure-free callers kept working; recorded by the B3A final range review (P3-1, 2026-08-06). CLOSED 2026-08-06: with the operation-plan registry published on every production pipeline (`m.Meta.Operations`) and clone decisions arriving through sema's `CloneSymbols` and the closure, the scan had no legitimate caller left on any path. `rewriteCloneCall` now refuses rediscovery on EVERY builder — closure-backed or closure-free — and `findCloneSymbol`, `cloneSymbolForType`, and their helpers are deleted. | Closed | Epic 23b B3B (operation-plan registry) | Done: both symbols are absent from the tree; `TestCloneRewriteRefusesToRediscoverAnImplementation` pins the refusal on both the closure-backed and the closure-free builder, asserting the call is never rewritten. |
| RV2-DEBT-154 | BLOCKER-CLASS soundness hole found underneath RV2-DEBT-153 and confirmed independently by the lead: sema never refuses a store through a SHARED reference, anywhere. `fn poke(r: &Leaf) { r.x = 99; }` compiles with zero diagnostics; the VM traps at runtime with `panic VM2102: store through non-mutable reference`, and the native binary silently writes through into the caller's value. Measured on the reproducer: VM exits 1 by trapping, the native binary exits 1 because the caller's `base.x` became 99 — the same exit code reached through opposite behaviours. `handleAssignment` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go:484`) only ever asks `isWriteThroughMutRef` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_binding.go:182`); no branch refuses when the base binding is a shared reference. The VM's runtime check exists because every `Location` carries `IsMut` (`internal/vm/place.go:323-325`); the native backend lowers `&T` to a bare `ptr` with no mutability bit anywhere, so there is no native check to add and the fix must be static. On the native backend `&T` therefore carries no guarantee whatsoever today. The inferred-`let` shape in RV2-DEBT-153 is one route into this hole, not the hole itself. **CLOSED 2026-08-09 by `aecd98d3`.** Sema reports SEM3187 at the store, with a note and a `surge fix` that widens the parameter to `&mut`; the VM's runtime VM2102 is now unreachable from well-typed source and is kept only as defence in depth. The row was left reading Open after the fix landed, and the stale status was caught by the pre-deletion audit of the lane worktrees rather than by anything in the tree — a row whose status nothing checks is a gate nothing runs. | Closed | Epic 23b defect lane (landed 2026-08-09) | Sema refuses a store whose base binding is a shared reference, diagnosed at the stage that can name the cause, with notes and — where the author has a real choice, such as widening a parameter to `&mut` — a fix suggestion that is not applied automatically because it changes every call site. VM and native agree by refusal on the whole matrix: a shared-reference parameter, an inferred indexed binding, a nested field store through a shared base, and a shared reborrow. The VM's runtime `VM2102` becomes unreachable from well-typed source and is retained only as a defence in depth. |
| RV2-DEBT-169 | BLOCKER REGRESSION introduced by the Wave C native cutover and FIXED 2026-08-08: the anchored channel send handed the runtime an address in the sending frame's own storage, and the matching receive freed it. `far-channel-composite` aborted on every run with `free(): invalid pointer`; Valgrind named the site precisely — `rt_free` (`rt_alloc.c:63`) called from generated code under `__surge_poll_call`, on an address "on thread 5's stack, in frame #2". Mechanism: `ch.send(v)` inside an `on ch { }` body lowers to an ordinary named runtime call (`internal/mir/lower_expr_calls.go:286`, `Callee{Kind: CalleeValue, Name: "rt_anchored_channel_send"}`) rather than to a MIR instruction of its own, and no backend emitter claimed that name, so it fell through `emitCall`'s intrinsic chain to the generic runtime-argument marshaller (`emit_call_site.go:78`), which appends the operand exactly as emitted. Since Wave C made composites inline, that operand is the value's own alloca. The receive leg (`emitAnchoredChanRecv` → `emitI64ToValue` → `emitAdoptFromTransportAllocation`) was correct and did the matching adopt: memcpy-out plus `rt_free` — of a stack address. In other words, of the box-on-transport TRIPLE, the copy-in leg was simply missing at this one site while its partner was present. The contrast that proves it: `far-task-composite` and `far-immediate-composite` publish through `emitValueToI64` and box, and both pass; `far-channel-scalar` passes because the whole bridge is gated on inline storage, which an `int` does not have. The payload was also WRONG before it was freed — the fixed row reports checksum 2368, which is the value the manifest has recorded as `expected_checksum` since Wave A. WHY NO GATE SAW IT: no test in the tree sends a struct over a far channel. Every far-channel fixture uses `far Channel<int>` (`internal/mir/crossing_representation_test.go:338`, `internal/buildpipeline/crossing_backend_test.go:70,150`, `crossing_payload_guard_test.go:49,58`) or `far Channel<string>` (`internal/vm/runtime_v2_far_channel_noncopy_e2e_test.go:44`, a refcounted pointer rather than inline storage). The carrier benchmark is the only material that builds one, and it had never completed a full sweep since integration. | Closed | Epic 23b Wave C tail (native backend) | CLOSED by `emitAnchoredChannelSend` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_crossing_anchored_on.go`), emitted next to the receive that completes the pair and dispatched from `emit_calls.go` ahead of the generic path; the new carrier site is recorded in the migration array against RV2-DEBT-151 rather than left for the ratchet to discover. Three payload-drop gates that asked `typeOwnsHeap` — correct only while a composite WAS its heap box — now ask `payloadNeedsRuntimeRelease` (owns heap OR has inline storage) at `emit_crossing_select.go`, `emit_channel.go` (`channelPayloadDropID`) and `emit_crossing_channel_create.go`. Evidence: 8/8 runs exit 0 with checksum 2368 matching the manifest, Valgrind reports zero bytes lost and zero invalid frees, and `internal/backend/llvm`, `internal/mir`, `internal/crossinggate`, `internal/buildpipeline` and `internal/carriergate` are green. A far-channel row carrying a COMPOSITE payload is owed where an ordinary run can see it, since the gap that hid this was fixture coverage rather than gate design. |
| RV2-DEBT-173 | NOTHING in the repository's automated gates executes a compiled Surge program, so no gate can observe a wrong runtime answer. Three independent facts, each verified 2026-08-08. (1) `make golden-check` regenerates FRONTEND sidecars only: `scripts/golden_update.sh:245` explicitly excludes `*.out`, `*.code`, `*.args`, `*.stdin` and `*.flags` from the regeneration sweep, so `.out` files are frozen inputs that are content-hashed but never re-derived. (2) The fifteen behavioural runners that DO execute `.out`/`.code` fixtures are all behind `//go:build golden` (`internal/vm/vm_async_golden_test.go:1` and fourteen siblings), and no target, script or CI job anywhere invokes `go test -tags golden` — grep over `Makefile`, `scripts/` and `.github/workflows/` returns zero hits. (3) CI's only behavioural step, `.github/workflows/ci.yml:181` → `scripts/run_testdata.sh --backend vm`, is a `# TEMPORARY STUB` (`:27-29`) that echoes `OK (stub)` and exits 0 unconditionally; 228 of that script's 279 lines are commented out. What `golden-check` genuinely proves is worth stating so it is not over-credited again: the frontend did not drift, and no lane silently reblessed a recorded output. It cannot prove behaviour, and Wave C's "golden-check ×2 with zero drift" was recorded in the wave's evidence as though it could — the cutover's actual behavioural evidence was the ordinary-storage proving corpus (15/15 on both harnesses, `known_red/` emptied) and the crossing gate, both of which do run programs. This row is the ninth instance of the pattern the wave has been recording all along: a gate reporting green while not exercising what its name claims. **REFINEMENT 2026-08-09, with a dated instance.** One clause above is too harsh on `golden-check` and the correction matters, because it locates the actual hole: `Check` (`internal/goldencheck/runner.go:53-69`) DOES re-run the generator and DOES `Diff` the regenerated corpus against the recorded one, so it is fully capable of seeing a frontend fixture drift away from what the compiler emits. What it cannot do is run: `make check` (`Makefile:278-285`) is `test` + `lint` + `c-check` + file sizes and never invokes it, so the only place it fires is CI, which is not gating this branch. The consequence was found the hard way while renumbering diagnostics: `testdata/golden/mir/compare_guard_await_release.mir` had been stale since `853aafba` (2026-08-08) changed a coroutine to write its suspension state in place — the fixture still recorded the old `L4 = struct_lit {__pc=..., __payload=...}` where the compiler now emits `L4.__pc = ...` / `L4.__payload = ...`, and `853aafba` touched no `.mir` fixture at all. Proven pre-existing rather than caused by the renumbering: the compiler rebuilt from `2d5bfb83` via `git archive` emits the new shape while that commit's own recorded fixture holds the old one. It was the only fixture in this state. So the gate that could have caught it exists and is correct; it is simply not wired to anything a commit has to pass. Whatever step 3 builds must be reachable from `make check`, not only from CI. **PARTIALLY CLOSED 2026-08-10.** Eleven of the fifteen runners now execute compiled Surge programs inside `make check`, on every commit, and `internal/vm` takes about 47s of its 90s package budget doing it. The tag turned out not to be a mode switch at all: `internal/vm/test_helpers_test.go` was `//go:build !golden` and defined helpers that some fifty unconstrained test files use, so setting the tag removed the helpers from files that still referenced them and the package did not COMPILE — the runners were not merely uninvoked, they were unrunnable. The cause was duplication: `repoRoot`, `envWithStdlib`, `buildSurgeBinary`, `runSurgeWithInput` and three package variables existed in two byte-identical copies, and the two tags were what kept them from colliding. Deleting the duplicates let the tag come off.
