Run for the first time, the runners failed 20 of 122 fixtures, all pre-existing; fourteen are closed (`inspect.go`'s three deleted bodies, the `Erring` envelope at `exit`, two unfinished halves of the fixed-array storage flip, the double drop of a refcounted scalar, a cancelled task denied a ready result, tuple types never interned structurally, and four recorded expectations that were wrong rather than the code). Four runners keep the tag, each with a comment naming its remaining red case and what that case needs, so the tag now marks known redness instead of hiding it. STILL OWED, and this row stays Open until all of it lands: the last six cases; the runners running on the NATIVE backend as well as the VM; `scripts/run_testdata.sh` un-stubbed or deleted with CI pointed at the real target; a clean-tree target for `TestGoldenUpdateDeterminism`, which cannot live in a pre-commit hook because its preflight demands a committed corpus and a hook runs on exactly the opposite; and the proof by breaking — change one recorded answer, watch the gate go red, restore it, watch it go green. **2026-08-10, second pass — everything in this row except the native lane being GREEN is now done.** The corpus runs on both backends: the thirteen near-identical copies of "run a recorded program and compare its output" are one `runBehaviourCorpus` taking the backend as a parameter, `make behaviour-check` is the VM lane the pre-commit hook already runs, and `make behaviour-check-all` adds the native lane. The native lane is a separate target by owner ruling rather than part of the hook, because it costs ~6s per fixture against the VM lane's ~0.26s — full tests at every important step, not at every commit. A requested lane whose toolchain is missing FAILS rather than skipping, since a silently skipped lane is the shape of gate this corpus replaces. `scripts/run_testdata.sh` is DELETED rather than un-stubbed — its commented-out body did what the Go runners now do, and a second implementation of the same comparison in bash only guarantees the two drift; CI runs the make targets instead, and the artifact-upload step that only the deleted script fed is gone. `make golden-corpus-determinism` gives `TestGoldenUpdateDeterminism` the clean-tree target it needs (471s, green) and CI runs it. **The proof by breaking is done and recorded here so it is not asked for again:** `TestVMTuplesGolden` green, `single=42` changed to `single=43` in the recorded `.out`, the gate went red naming the fixture, the line was restored, the gate went green. Also fixed on the way, and worth naming because the refactor is what exposed both: a `.flags` sidecar was silently dropped by the first draft of the shared helper (`async_fuzz_seed_123` runs the fuzzed scheduler), and the `_`-prefix convention that marks a recorded-but-not-run fixture was not honoured. WHAT REMAINS is the native lane going green, which is no longer this row's work: the VM lane has zero failures and the native lane's 25 are filed as RV2-DEBT-184 (eight the native backend will not compile), 185 (three wrong answers), 186 (a silent abnormal exit), 187 (panics without span or backtrace, owner ruling taken) and 188 (eight scheduler-order differences, owner decision required). This row closes when those five do. **CLOSED 2026-08-11 by owner ruling, with the reading stated so it is not re-litigated.** The last piece — the native lane going green — landed today: `make behaviour-check-all` reports 0 failures on both backends and `make behaviour-check-mt` is green. The close condition as written asks for a gate that runs "on every commit" on "BOTH backends", and what exists is the VM lane inside `make check` (so on every commit, since the hook refuses a red tree) plus `make behaviour-check-all` as a named target. **The owner reads the condition as met**: full tests belong at important milestones rather than at every commit, which is the same policy that put the native lane in its own target in the first place. The obligation that comes with that reading, and the reason it is written down here: **the native and MT lanes have to actually be run at those milestones.** A target nobody invokes is the failure mode this row was opened about. Before a wave lands, before an epic closes, and before any claim of the form "behaviour did not change", run `make behaviour-check-all` and `make behaviour-check-mt` and quote the result. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 gate integrity; native lane tracked by RV2-DEBT-184..188 | A gate that runs on every commit executes compiled Surge programs and compares their real output against the recorded `.out`, on BOTH backends, and fails when the output differs. Concretely: the `golden` build tag is either dropped or invoked by a target that `make check` reaches; `scripts/run_testdata.sh` is un-stubbed or deleted rather than left echoing success; and the new gate is proven by breaking it — change one recorded answer, watch the gate go red, restore it, watch it go green. Until that exists, no claim of the form "behaviour did not change" may cite `golden-check` as its evidence.  |
| RV2-DEBT-177 | The VM cannot iterate or range-index a FIXED array, so `for x in a` and `a[[i..j]]` over `T[N]` are LLVM-only shapes that panic at run time rather than being refused. `evalIterInit` (`internal/vm/eval_iter.go:35-54`) switches on the iterable's value kind and accepts only `VKHandleArray` and `VKHandleRange`; under ordinary storage a fixed array is a composite with no handle, so a loop head panics `VM1003: expected iterable, got composite` (reproduced on a five-line `int[3]` loop). A range index never reaches a slice path at all: `evalIndex` (`internal/vm/eval_data.go:73`) now routes a fixed array to `evalStorageIndex` (`:491`), which takes an integer and nothing else, so the range falls through to `arrayIndexFromValue` and is rejected at `internal/vm/array_utils.go:114` with `expected int, got range`. Before the VM's composite-storage flip a fixed array was a heap `OKArray` object and both paths worked; the flip removed the handle these paths dispatch on. The gap is not one missing branch — a VM array view is an `OKArraySlice` whose base must be an `OKArray`, so there is nothing for a fixed array's slice to point at. Sema accepts both shapes as readily as for dynamic arrays (`internal/sema/type_array_view.go:83-99` falls back to `arrayFixedInfo`), so nothing refuses them earlier. WHY NO GATE CAUGHT IT: the only fixed-array-slice gate is an IR-shape assertion (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_array_range_test.go:27-44`) that checks the emitted IR contains a call and never runs the program; the differential row that would have caught the VM half, `TestLLVMParity/array_range_indexing`, was already red because the NATIVE half segfaulted on the same lines — with both halves broken the mismatch named neither. The native half is fixed now, so that row is a live VM-only failure. **CLOSED 2026-08-10.** A fixed array in ordinary storage now has both. The VM had two element addressing models — a heap array object whose elements are whole Values, and an arena extent whose elements are layout-encoded bytes — and no single view that could be either, which is why every range and slice path dispatched on a heap handle that 75b56918 had taken away. `arrayView` now carries either base and five accessors are the only code that knows which, so a rename of its object field made the Go compiler enumerate all 23 readers rather than leaving one to dereference nil. A slice over a fixed array names the array's own extent, so writing through the slice lands in the bytes the base reads: the ref IS the alias. A range needs no arena awareness at all, because `for x in f` and `f.__range()` first take a slice header over the extent and then iterate that. One trap was load-bearing and worth recording: `for x in f` lowers to `iter_init copy L0`, and a copying operand DUPLICATES a composite into scratch, so an iterator built the obvious way would name an extent that the deferred drop releases and the instruction-boundary rewind invalidates — the next `iter_next` would read a stale reference. `evalIterInit` therefore resolves the iterable through `EvalPlace` and owns nothing, the same way a move-out field access does. Verified: `arrays_slice_view` prints its aliased write, `array_range_panics` prints all four rows, and `for x in f` over `int[3]` sums correctly. NOTE for KNOWN_LIMITATIONS, recorded there: a slice or range that OUTLIVES the fixed array it names is now refused by the VM with a stale-reference panic, where the native backend silently dangles — sema accepts the shape on both. The VM being stricter here is an improvement, not a divergence to paper over. | Closed | Runtime V2 VM container storage | A fixed array iterates and range-indexes on the VM, or the language refuses both shapes at sema with a diagnostic naming the edit — a run-time `VM1003` is neither. `TestLLVMParity/array_range_indexing` green is the evidence, plus a row that iterates `T[N]` where `T` is a composite. |
| RV2-DEBT-182 | **CLOSED 2026-08-09 in the same change that found it.** Nine diagnostic numbers were claimed by two different diagnostics each, so nine distinct errors rendered as the same `SEMxxxx` id. `Code.ID()` (`internal/diag/codes.go:635`) is purely range-based, so the number IS the identity a user sees, the key `Title()` looks a description up by, and the seed `fix.MakeFixID` derives a quick-fix identity from; the second claimant answered for the first at all three at once. Eight were the ownership block `codes.go` added at 3136-3143 (2026-07-14) landing on the crossing block `codes_crossing.go` had reserved at 3136-3143 (2026-07-07); the ninth was `SemaStoreThroughSharedRef` (`aecd98d3`) landing on `SemaAliasMagicRedeclared` (`d757ac61`), two lanes independently taking the next free number 3187. The golden corpus recorded BOTH readings of the same id — `sema/invalid/borrow_into_owned_method_param.diag` and `crossing/block01/invalid/far_negative_remote_own.diag` both assert SEM3137 — and nothing objected. WHY NOTHING OBJECTED, which is the part worth keeping: duplicate constant keys in a map literal are already a compile error in Go, so the one table in `codes.go` was self-policing all along; the five sibling code files defeated it by merging their descriptions in through `init()` at run time instead of writing them into the literal, and a map assignment silently overwrites. The split into sibling files was itself done to keep `codes.go` under the LOC ceiling, so a soft style limit bought a real defect class. | Closed | Diagnostics registry | Done, and the guarantee is the compiler rather than a test: every description now lives in the single `codeDescription` literal, the five `init()` merges are deleted, and reintroducing a collision fails the build with `duplicate key 3187 in map literal` (verified by reintroducing it). Two tests keep that reachable — `TestEveryDiagnosticCodeHasADescriptionEntry` refuses a code with no entry, since an unlisted code is one the literal cannot police, and `TestEveryDiagnosticCodeNumberIsClaimedOnce` reads the declarations out of the sources to name both claimants and the highest number in use. Both were proven by breaking them. The crossing block moved to 3188-3195 and the alias-magic code to 3196, because `codes.go` is the registry of record and the claimant outside it yields. Standing rule: new codes are declared in `codes.go`; if the LOC gate objects, `codes.go` goes on its exception list rather than the codes going into a new file. |
| RV2-DEBT-184 | The NATIVE backend refuses to compile eight programs the VM runs, so the behavioural corpus cannot be green on both backends. Two distinct emit gaps, found 2026-08-10 by running the corpus on the native lane for the first time. (1) `unknown external function` for a monomorphised intrinsic the emitter never declared — `__add::<uint>` in `strings_std`/`strings_rope_std` (`llvm emit levenshtein bb6 instr[4]`), and the same shape in `abi_string_bytesview`, `arrays_drop_nested`, `array_range_panics`, `range_for_in`, `strings_rope`. (2) `call has destination but returns void: fn.3` in `map_index_set` (`llvm emit main bb0 instr[2]`), where the call has a destination place but the emitted function signature returns nothing. Both are compile-time refusals: the program never runs, so this is not a wrong answer but an unimplemented one. **CLOSED 2026-08-11. There were FOUR gaps, not the two this row filed, and the miscount came from reading a monomorphised name as if it described the operation.** `unknown external function "__add::<uint>"` looks like scalar arithmetic on a `uint` and is not: it is `@intrinsic fn __add(self: &Array<T>, other: &Array<T>)` from `core/intrinsics.sg:757`, with the ELEMENT type in the suffix. The emitter's numeric check correctly declined it — it is not numeric — and the generic call path then could not resolve an intrinsic that is not a function, so one missing lowering presented under a name that named its element. This is the same defect shape recorded in [[asking-a-type-for-a-container-answer]]: the answer came from the element type, and the question was about the container. The four gaps and what closed each: (a) the void-call destination, by typing an index-set call by what it returns (`3447cd40`); (b) a missing `rt_string_force_flatten` symbol (`1c5b7486`); (c) array concatenation, lowered to a new `rt_array_concat` mirroring `rt_array_free_elems` and reusing the existing clone glue rather than a second copy machine (`0a550ad4`); (d) an explicit `Range<T>.next()`, which never compiled at all although `for x in 0..5` did, because a `Range<T>` handle names two different runtime objects and `iter_next` only ever saw one of them (`5ebb0cec`). VERIFIED by a full `make behaviour-check-all` at `5ebb0cec`: all eight named fixtures compile and match their recorded `.out` on `--backend=llvm`. The only `/llvm` failures left in the whole corpus are four panic fixtures held by RV2-DEBT-187's backtrace and one held by RV2-DEBT-186. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 native backend | Each of the eight compiles and runs on `--backend=llvm` and produces the recorded `.out`, verified by `make behaviour-check-all` reporting no `/llvm` failures for them. Group the fix by emit gap rather than by fixture: one missing declaration path and one signature mismatch account for all eight. |
| RV2-DEBT-185 | The NATIVE backend gives three WRONG ANSWERS the VM gets right, each a different mechanism, all found 2026-08-10 when the behavioural corpus first ran on the native lane. (1) `vm_int8_overflow_add`: the VM traps the overflow and exits 1; native exits 0 with no output, so a program that must fail silently succeeds. (2) `arrays_view_push_panics`: where the recorded answer is `1`, `1`, `2`, native prints 300-digit garbage (`-79843148642387849365584091149302591932883272793223952679927584496378351251319912547917764444...`), which is a read of memory that holds something else. (3) `async_cancel_propagation`: native prints only `parent=cancelled` and loses `child=cancelled` — the SAME defect fixed on the VM side in this branch, where the poll asked "am I cancelled?" before "is the awaited task done?", so a cancelled awaiter unwound before collecting a result that was already waiting. The VM fix is the model; check whether the native poll has the same ordering. **(3) IS FIXED 2026-08-10.** The native poll did have that ordering — `rt_task_poll` answered a cancelled caller before asking the target anything — and the check is now made after the DONE delivery instead. It is moved rather than removed, because what it guards is the join-waiter registration below it. Measured at `SURGE_THREADS=1`: 0 correct out of 20 before, 20 out of 20 after. Recorded because it nearly went the other way: measuring the same change at the default worker count says the opposite, since none of the answers at 32 workers is promised. **(1) is a SEPARATE problem and stays open by owner ruling 2026-08-10: the native backend has NO overflow trap for fixed-width arithmetic at all** — the checks in `emit_intrinsics_numeric.go` cover `to` conversions only, so `a + b` on `int8` wraps silently where the VM raises VM1101. Note the fixture cannot go green on this row alone: its recorded answer is a panic WITH span and backtrace, so it also needs RV2-DEBT-187. **(1) IS FIXED 2026-08-10/11, and this row now has no work of its own left.** Add, sub and mul on `int8..int64` and `uint8..uint64` go through the checked LLVM intrinsics and panic on the overflow bit; division gets a divisor-zero guard, signed `MIN / -1` traps as the VM does, and signed `MIN % -1` is steered to yield the VM's `0` rather than LLVM's undefined behaviour (`0a550ad4`). Checked against a matrix of 21 generated programs over every width and operation — 21 of 21 agree with the VM on exit code and message text — and against 33 lines of arithmetic that must NOT change, all identical. A second commit was then needed for the fixture, because the trap announced itself under the wrong number: `rt_panic_numeric` hardcoded `VM3202`, so `rt_panic_code` now takes the code from the caller and arithmetic overflow says `VM1101` (`ec46ba40`). MEASURED at `5ebb0cec` by a full `make behaviour-check-all`: `vm_int8_overflow_add` differs from its recorded answer by EXACTLY the backtrace — the message, the code and the `at <path>:<line>:<col>` line match the VM byte for byte. **The row therefore stays Open only because its close condition is written on the fixture, and the fixture is held by RV2-DEBT-187; there is nothing left to do here.** Deliberately NOT covered and needing their own decision: shifts and unary negation both still wrap on the native backend, scoped out of `0a550ad4` rather than half-done — see RV2-DEBT-192. **CLOSED 2026-08-11 the moment its blocker landed.** This row had no work of its own left and said so; what stood in the way was RV2-DEBT-187's backtrace, and with that built `vm_int8_overflow_add` matches its recorded answer on `--backend=llvm`. Verified by a full `make behaviour-check-all`: 0 failures on both backends. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 native backend; no work of its own remains, blocked only on RV2-DEBT-187's backtrace | All three produce the recorded `.out` on `--backend=llvm`. The garbage read in (2) is the one to treat as urgent: printing another value's bytes is a memory defect, not a formatting one.  |
| RV2-DEBT-186 | The NATIVE runtime terminates ABNORMALLY AND SILENTLY when a scope exits holding an unreclaimable reference cycle. `testdata/golden/vm_rc/vm_rc_leak_panics.sg` builds two arrays that point at each other; adding a `print` before `return` shows the body completes on both backends. The VM then reports `panic VM3302: heap leak detected: 2 objects still alive` and exits 1. Native exits **255 with empty stdout and empty stderr** — no message, no code, nothing to act on. Narrowed 2026-08-10: a borrow plus deref-copy alone, an `@drop` of the borrow alone, and a one-way assignment each exit 0 on native, so it takes the completed two-way cycle to trigger. Distinguish this from "native has no leak detector", which is true — `heap leak detected` exists only in `internal/vm/drop.go:186` — but is NOT what this row is about: a runtime with no detector should leak and exit 0, not die without a word. **MEASURED 2026-08-10, and the measurement removes most of the option space.** First, the crash is precise: `valgrind` reports `Stack overflow in thread #1 ... at drop.type1518`. The native drop glue is generated PER TYPE and memoised by `requireDropGlue`, so a recursive type produces one mutually recursive pair, `drop.typeN` and `drop_elem.typeN`, trampolining through `rt_array_free_elems`. The compiler's own cycle guard over the TYPE graph (`typeOwnsHeapRec`'s seen-set) is already correct; what runs away is the VALUE graph, for which the glue carries no object identity. Second, and decisively: a thread-local in-progress guard was written and tried at `array_free_base_storage`, and it DOES make the walk finite - the stack overflow disappears. What it exposes underneath is a double free, and the trace names the reason: `fn.1` drops BOTH arrays, and the walk started by dropping the first already frees the second, so the second drop reads a header that is gone. That is not a traversal defect. It is the program having TWO owners for each object, which is exactly what the ownership verifier already flags here as `OWN-001`/`OWN-002`. Native arrays carry no reference count at all - `rt_array_internal.h`'s header is `{len, cap, data}` - so no traversal order can arbitrate between two owners. THEREFORE: "reclaim the cycle" is unsound, "report a leak" has nothing to detect one with, and "leak and exit 0" cannot know which owner is redundant. The guard was reverted rather than kept, because its only effect is on a program the compiler already rejects and it costs a check on every array free. The remaining answers are (a) refuse the shape in the compiler - the check exists and already fires on these two lines under `--dev`, though promoting it interacts with the whole Epic 25 ownership-corpus subsystem and is a decision at that scale - or (b) give native arrays a reference count, which is a runtime-model change. **CLOSED 2026-08-11 by option (a), the one this row already named: the shape is refused in the compiler.** `vm_rc_leak_panics.sg` built its two-way cycle out of `*a_ref`, and RV2-DEBT-191 now refuses exactly that, so the program does not compile and the silent 255 is unreachable from Surge source. This is not a workaround for the exit code — it is the answer the row's own analysis arrived at, having established that reclaiming the cycle is unsound with no refcount on native arrays, that reporting a leak needs a detector native does not have, and that leaking and exiting 0 cannot know which of two owners is redundant. Measured while closing 191: `*r` was the ONLY expression in the language that could alias a heap array, so there is no second way to reach this state. What is NOT claimed: the native runtime still has no leak detector, and a future feature that reintroduces aliasing deliberately would reopen the question of what it does when a scope exits holding one. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 native runtime | Native either reclaims the cycle, or reports something a caller can act on, and exits deliberately. Whatever it does, it does not exit 255 in silence. Whether native grows a leak report at all is a separate question and may be answered No; this row is satisfied by an exit that is explained.  |
| RV2-DEBT-189 | A compare arm that gave away PART of its payload binding freed that part twice on the native backend. Eleven lines reproduce it with no array and no call: `type Foo = { name: string, n: int }`, `let v: Foo? = Some(Foo { name = "x", n = 1 })`, then `compare v { Some(x) => print(own x.name); _ => print("<nothing>"); }`. The VM prints `x` then `done` and exits 0; the native binary prints `x` and dies with `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`. The binding genuinely owns the payload — it is lowered as a move-out of the envelope — and `own x.name` gives the string away, but the arm then dropped the binding WHOLE and the generated glue freed the string the callee had already taken. CAUSE, one missing question: a compare arm's obligations come from `liveDroppables`, which excludes a binding only when it moved ENTIRELY, so a binding that lost one field is still owed a drop but a NARROWED one. Every sibling obligation channel — scope end, block-expression end, early exit — follows its drop list with `recordResidualDrops`; the compare-arm channel recorded its list and its one-sided plans and never asked for the residual, so the lowering looked up a plan that had never been written and emitted the un-narrowed drop. The plan has to be read INSIDE the arm loop: it is a question about that arm's moved-set, and the recording loop runs after the arms are merged, so asking there answers for the union. Two things about the shape are worth keeping. It is the same defect the OTHER branch channel already had — a moved place folded up to its whole binding — fixed there and never carried across when this channel was added. And it was invisible from the VM, which consumes the identical MIR and survives only because its move-out physically empties the slot (`takeMember` → `storageZero`), so a wrong obligation produced a right answer on one backend; the native backend never reads `MoveOut` at all. It was also invisible from the corpus until 2026-08-10, because the only fixture that reaches it, `arrays_drop_nested`, was refused at emit time for an unrelated missing symbol and never ran that far. | Closed 2026-08-10 | Ownership obligations; sema | Fixed where obligations are decided rather than by teaching the native backend to empty moved slots, which would make the nulling load-bearing and leave the wrong obligation in place. `arrays_drop_nested` matches its recorded answer on both backends. |
| RV2-DEBT-190 | A `select` over an unbuffered channel and a timer HANGS on the native backend at more than one worker. `testdata/golden/vm_async_suite/t22_select_wait_recv.sg` makes a capacity-0 channel, spawns a producer that sleeps and then sends, and selects between `ch.recv()` and `sleep(100).await()`. Built once and run repeatedly, the binary finishes in milliseconds and prints `v=7` almost always, and roughly one run in twenty never finishes at all: measured 0/20 hung at `SURGE_THREADS=1`, 1/20 at 2, 0/20 at 4, 1/20 at 8. A hung run produces no output and no error - it simply never exits, which is the signature of a wakeup that was posted and not observed rather than of a crash. Found 2026-08-10 by the multi-worker lane on its first run, which is the argument for that lane existing: the same fixture is byte-exact and stable at one worker, so every single-worker gate in the repository agrees it is fine. Worth noting how it first arrived, because the lane has since been changed to prevent a repeat: it presented as the whole `internal/vm` package timing out after an hour with the fixture name recoverable only from a goroutine dump. The lane now builds each fixture once and bounds each RUN at 20 seconds, so a hang is a named failure and the whole lane finishes in about four minutes. **CLOSED 2026-08-11.** The rate measurement is finally worth something: 3500 runs at 8 workers and 3500 at 16 — the two configurations the re-measurement used — give 0 hangs out of 7000. State the power with the number, because that is the lesson this row already taught once: at the previously measured 0.06% residual the chance of seeing zero in 7000 runs is about 1.5%, so this sweep had roughly 98.5% power to find the defect that was there, and did not. The earlier 0-in-840 had about 60% and a 0-in-400 about 79%, which is why neither meant anything. Closed on the deterministic proof FIRST and the sweep second: the proof says the window existed and is shut, the sweep says nothing of that rate remains. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 native scheduler; select / unbuffered channel / timer interaction | The fixture completes on every run at every worker count the lane exercises. The suspected shape is a lost wakeup between a select arm's registration and a same-instant readiness - the producer's send and the select's park are the two sides - so the proof should be a deterministic sync-point reproduction rather than a repetition count, in the style of the existing cancelled-join-waiter control. A repetition count can only ever show the rate falling. **ROOT CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-11, AND IT REFUTES THIS ROW'S OWN HYPOTHESIS.** It is not a lost wakeup between a select arm's registration and a same-instant readiness. A gdb dump of a caught hang shows there is NO `CHAN_RECV` waiter in the waiter store at all: `now_ms=100`, the sleep task is DONE, the select task is parked on `JOIN/2`, and the producer is parked on `CHAN_SEND` still holding the 7 — the select had already completed on its TIMEOUT arm, so nothing was ever waiting to be woken. The mechanism is a PREMATURE VIRTUAL-CLOCK ADVANCE. The clock only moves when an observer judges the executor idle: `advance_time_to_next_timer` (`runtime/native/rt_async_state.c:494`) CAS-advances `ex->now_ms` to the next deadline, gated on `rt_sched_idle_sample_locked`, whose predicate (`runtime/native/rt_ready_queue.c:34-46`) is `running_count > 0 \|\| scheduler runnable non-empty \|\| transport inbound > 0`. The worker turn violates that predicate: it decremented `running_count` and only THEN called `apply_poll_outcome`, which is the sole thing that publishes the turn's successor (`mark_done` → join drain → `ready_push`). Between the two, the finished task is in no queue, `running_count` is 0, and the successor is not yet enqueued — so an idle sample taken in the gap reads idle and jumps the clock straight past the producer's already-due 5ms sleep to the select's 100ms deadline. The two halves are separate critical sections on the same shard lock, so the lock does not serialise them. Proven by probe instrumentation rather than inference: a hung run logs `ADVANCE from=5 to=100` with no `APPLIED task=4` between the poll and the advance, and a healthy run of the same binary differs by exactly that one line. **PARTIALLY FIXED, AND THE ROW STAYS OPEN — the first patch was measured as a closure and adversarial verification refuted that.** Publishing before decrementing at all four sites took the hang rate from 19/240 to 0/840 in the original measurement, which was then read as closed. An independent re-measurement at higher worker counts and larger run counts still finds hangs: roughly 4 in ~6900 post-fix runs (~0.06%), including one caught and confirmed to be a genuine deadlock — 0 CPU ticks over a 2-second sample, all 18 threads in `futex_do_wait` — not a slow run. The original 0/840 is statistically CONSISTENT with a 0.06% residual (P(0 in 840) ≈ 60%); it was underpowered, not wrong, and the conclusion drawn from it is what failed. Record the lesson with the number: a rate this low needs thousands of runs and more than 8 workers before absence means anything. **A SECOND FALSE-IDLE WINDOW IS NAMED AS THE LIKELY RESIDUAL, by code reading and NOT causally confirmed:** `rt_sleep_fire_due_on_shard` (`runtime/native/rt_async_sleep.c:146-168`) takes the shard lock, pops due sleepers OUT of the sleep store into a local batch, RELEASES the lock, and only then calls `wake_task` to enqueue them. In that gap a fired sleeper is in no sleep store, no ready queue and no `running_count`, so the idle sample reads idle while `rt_next_sleep_deadline` no longer reports the deadline just fired — precisely a 5→100 skip. Its racy caller is a worker at `rt_worker_turn.c:131-133`, which drops the shard lock around the call. The collect-then-wake there is deliberate, so closing it needs a counted in-flight batch rather than a reordering. An instrumented A/B on it was VACUOUS — adding the counter perturbed timing until the control arm stopped hanging too — which is itself the argument for the sync-point control this row asks for. **The invariant to state once and apply everywhere: no observer may conclude the executor is idle while work is in flight, and that covers every popped-but-not-yet-republished item — poll outcomes, fired sleepers, drained waiters — not just the poll-outcome case.** **THE SECOND WINDOW IS CONFIRMED AND CLOSED 2026-08-11, and this time by a deterministic proof rather than a rate.** It was named by code reading and could not be confirmed by measurement — the instrumented A/B was vacuous — so it is confirmed the way this project confirms a race: a sync point in the real runtime, held open, with a negative control to show the proof is not empty. `SP_SLEEP_FIRED_BEFORE_WAKE` sits in `rt_sleep_fire_due_on_shard` after the due batch has been popped and the shard lock released, before the first wake. A harness mode holds the firing thread there and asks `rt_sched_idle_sample_locked` whether the executor is idle. With the fix it answers BUSY; with `RV2_DEBT_190_NEGATIVE_CONTROL` — which makes the in-flight claim inert — it answers IDLE and the harness fails with `idle while a fired sleeper was in flight`. Both halves green at 2, 4 and 8 workers. At `SURGE_THREADS=1` the window cannot be constructed at all: the single-runner path fires the batch on the same thread that would sample it, so there is no concurrent observer — which is why the fixture hung 0 times in 20 at one worker and roughly one in six at two. **The fix is the invariant expressed ONCE rather than a second reordering.** `rt_scheduler` gains `publishing_count`, shard-lock-guarded like `running_count`: a collect-then-wake site claims its batch in the SAME critical section that removed it, releases the claim after republishing, and `rt_sched_idle_sample_locked` — the runtime's only idle observer — asks it alongside the queues. A batch on somebody's stack is now work the executor can see. **A THIRD SITE OF THE SAME CLASS IS CONFIRMED BY READING AND NOT YET COVERED:** `wake_key_all_with_policy` (`runtime/native/rt_task_park.c`) collects matching waiters out of the store under the shard lock, releases it, and only then wakes them — the identical shape, for the identical stated reason (waking takes the target's owner lock, which can be the same mutex). It has not been measured as a cause and is not claimed as one; the counter it would need is the one that now exists.  |
| RV2-DEBT-191 | `*r` on a borrow of a heap-owning value produces a SECOND OWNER rather than a copy, and the native backend then frees the value twice. Six lines, no arrays and no cycles: `let a: string = "hello"; let r = &a; let b: string = *r; @drop r;` then printing `b`, `a` and `"done"`. The VM prints `hello`, `hello`, `done` and exits 0; the native binary prints `hello` and dies with `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`, exit 255. Verified pre-existing at `ea674471`. That `*r` ALIASES rather than copies is provable without any crash: `let mut b: int[] = *a_ref;` followed by `b[0] = 99` makes `a[0]` read 99, on BOTH backends. So the language today lets a program hold two owning bindings for one heap value, and the VM survives that only because its heap values are reference counted while native strings and arrays carry no count at all. **RV2-DEBT-186 is a consequence of this row, not a separate defect**: its cycle is what happens when the aliasing is closed into a loop, and the stack overflow is only the first symptom, ahead of the double free underneath. The `--dev` ownership verifier does NOT catch the general shape - it fires on a STORE into a projection (`checkAssign`, `internal/mir/ownership_verify.go`, sink `projected_assign`), which is what the cycle fixture does and what the plain `let b = *r` does not. **OWNER RULING 2026-08-10: refuse it. And the rule ALREADY EXISTS — `*r` walks past it rather than being missed by it.** `SemaBorrowIntoOwnedParam` (`internal/sema/type_expr_calls.go:441`) refuses passing a borrow where an owned value is wanted, and its own note states this exact defect in as many words: "an owned value is freed by ...; binding a borrow to it would free the borrowed value twice", with the hint "pass 'r' itself to give the value away, or keep yours and pass a copy: r.__clone()". Demonstrated side by side: `takes(r)` where `r: &string` and `fn takes(s: string)` is REFUSED by that diagnostic, while `takes(*r)` is ACCEPTED and double-frees natively. So the hole is not a missing rule but a laundering operation: `internal/sema/type_expr_ops.go`'s `ast.ExprUnaryDeref` case types `*r` as `elementType(operandType)` and returns it — no ownership check, nothing consumed, no move recorded, unlike the neighbouring `ast.ExprUnaryOwn` which calls `consumeTempCandidate`. By the time any ownership check runs, the operand's type is `string` and the evidence that it came from a borrow is gone. **ROOT CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-10, and it is one uncommented early return.** The rule is not missing and is not a family of separate checks: it is ONE gate with ONE entry point, `observeMove` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go:12`), which every consuming position funnels into — let, return, ret, block tail, call argument, struct-literal field, compare scrutinee, arm result, crossing capture, blocking capture, async capture, select send, assignment RHS, array/map/tuple element. `PlaceSegmentDeref` is ALREADY on its refused list (`placeSegmentEnumerable` admits only Field and TupleIndex), `resolvePlace` ALREADY emits a Deref segment for `*r` (`borrow_runtime.go:151`), and `reportUnnameableResidual` ALREADY carries prose written for exactly this shape: "a reference borrows what it points at, so there is no value here to give away" (`borrow_lints.go:203-207`). The gate is simply never reached, because `observeMove` early-returns at `borrow_runtime_ops.go:81` on `tc.isSharedRefDeref(expr)`, whose last line is `return !tt.Mutable` — true exactly for a deref of a SHARED reference. Demonstrated in three programs: `let b: string = own *r` is REFUSED with "cannot take `r.*` out of `r`: it names a place behind a reference"; `let b: string = *rm` where `rm: &mut string` is REFUSED with the same words; `let b: string = *r` where `r: &string` is ACCEPTED. Blame says this is a layering accident rather than a decision: the early return dates from 2025-12-24, when `observeMove` recorded borrow-table moves and nothing else, and the ownership gate was added underneath it on 2026-07-30 by `1dd820ca` — whose own message says a path "through a reference" is refused. Neither the function nor the early return carries a comment, in a file where every other decision carries a paragraph. Also established: sema hands MIR ONE owner, not two — it marks the binding aliased with no drop obligation — and the second release appears at the first consuming sink, where the callee parameter is `owes_release`. The VM survives because `internal/vm/eval_ops.go:234` retains on every deref, manufacturing the reference MIR never recorded; native emits a bare load. And MIR's own ownership verifier already catches the shape (`call_arg of L4`), but runs only under `--dev`. **BLAST RADIUS MEASURED 2026-08-11, AND IT REFUTES THE FIX THIS ROW IMPLIES.** Deleting the early return is not a small change with a countable cost; it is a change that stops the language compiling. Measured twice independently on the same tree: the ownership corpus goes from `attempted=1052 mir_success=566` to `attempted=1052 mir_success=0`, and 556 of the 556 new unrecorded failures carry the SAME site, `SEM3143@core/format.sg:61:20`. A four-line program with no strings and no `print` stops building. The site is `return compare *arg {` in `fn append_fmt_arg(out: string, arg: &FmtArg) -> string`, which every compilation analyses. (Honesty note carried from the measurement: the corpus gate is ALREADY RED at `5ebb0cec` — 15 unrecorded non-invalid compile failures and 7 stale allowances — so read 566→0 as the delta, not as green→red.) A compile-only sweep of all 1014 `.sg` files under `testdata/golden`, `stdlib` and `core` changes exactly 15 files, which are exactly 5 distinct source expressions. **Only 2 of the 5 are the defect.** GENUINE: both `let X_copy: Node[] = *X_ref;` in `vm_rc_leak_panics.sg` — the built binary segfaults with a valgrind stack overflow and 8 allocs / 0 frees. FALSE POSITIVES, each proven by running both backends under valgrind rather than by reading: `core/format.sg:61` (12 allocs / 12 frees, 0 errors), `stdlib/json/json.sg:53` in the deep-clone routine `json_clone` (no double free; a pre-existing 224-byte UNDER-release instead, the opposite failure mode, worth its own row), and `recursive_handles.sg:19`, whose scrutinee is `*n.next` at `uint?` and owns no heap at all — it is dragged in only because `isCopyType` (`internal/sema/type_expr_utils.go:281`) never recurses into a union, so an Option over a Copy payload is not Copy. A SIXTH site lives in Go test source where no `.sg` sweep can see it: five `compare *pair` snippets in `TestCompareBorrowedTupleBindingsAreAliases`, whose doc comment PINS the opposite semantics as a decision — a borrowed-tuple decomposition must emit zero drops. **THE DISCRIMINATOR IS NOT THE DEREF.** `compare *arg { TStr(s) => out + s; }` is clean on both backends; `compare *arg { TStr(s) => s; }` — the arm handing its payload OUTWARD — double-frees natively at `5ebb0cec` today. So refusing the compare scrutinee is neither necessary nor sufficient: `observeMove` is POSITION-BLIND (21 call sites funnel into it, the compare scrutinee at `internal/sema/type_expr_flow.go:20`, carrying no position), and the rule has to name the consuming position, refusing the value-producing ones (let initialiser, return, ret, block tail, call argument, struct-literal field, assignment RHS, element, captures) while admitting the compare scrutinee — and then, as a second half of the same rule, refusing an ARM that lets its payload escape. Landing only the first half leaves a live double free the corpus happens not to contain. Also established: `isSharedRefDeref` returns true only for IMMUTABLE derefs, so `*rm` on `&mut T` never reached the deleted gate and already flows through the move machinery; a position-based rule must not assume that gate covered every deref. **TWO THINGS MUST LAND IN THE SAME CHANGE OR THE TREE IS RED EITHER WAY.** `OWN-001`/`OWN-002` do not lapse quietly — a stale allowance is a `t.Error` — so they come out of `internal/ownershipgate/testdata/allowlist.json` with the rule. And the VM3302 positive control needs a replacement that does not exist in Surge source: `clone(a_ref)` is refused (`type [Node] is not clonable`), inlining `clone(&a)` leaves the borrow live so the cycle-closing assignment is refused, and hiding the deref in a helper reproduces the identical VM3302 and is refused by the new rule there too. `*r` is currently the ONLY expression in the language that aliases a heap array, so the control has to be built in a VM-level unit test, or the change has to add a deliberately-priced aliasing primitive. **DIAGNOSTIC: do not reuse `SEM3143`.** Its headline says "what would remain after this move cannot be named", which is the residual-enumerability rationale for a partial move out of an aggregate — but nothing is being taken out of an aggregate here and there is no residual. It labels the expression `r.*`, a spelling the language does not have and the author did not write. Its hint — "move the value that `r` refers to, at the place that owns it" — is actively WRONG for this shape: in the six-line reproducer `a` is read on the next line, so following the hint turns the error into a use-after-move. And it attaches no quick-fix, unlike its sibling `reportPartialMoveNeedsOwn`, against the project's own rule that a new surface ships with one. This condition needs its own code in `internal/diag/codes.go` naming what is true — that reading a heap-owning value through a shared reference would make a second owner — with `r.__clone()` as the hint and as a `surge fix` suggestion, verified to work natively at `5ebb0cec`. **THIS ROW THEREFORE NEEDS AN OWNER DECISION BEFORE IMPLEMENTATION, on a question the original ruling did not reach:** the ruling settled that `*r` producing a second owner is refused; it did not settle what a `compare` over a borrowed union means, and the measurement says that is where the whole cost sits. **IMPLEMENTED AND CLOSED 2026-08-11, by position, exactly as the measurement said it had to be.** The rule is `observeMove` refusing a shared-reference deref of a heap-owning type in every position EXCEPT the compare scrutinee, which is flagged for one expression at `type_expr_flow.go` and nowhere else; plus the second half, `rejectArmHandingOutBorrowedPayload`, which refuses an arm that ANSWERS with a payload binding when the subject is borrowed. Both report the new `SemaMoveOutOfSharedBorrow` (SEM3197) rather than the partial-move code, because nothing is taken out of an aggregate here and no residual has to be named — and because only its own message can name `r.__clone()`, which the partial-move hint cannot: that hint says to move the value at the place that owns it, and the six-line reproducer reads that owner on the very next line. A `surge fix` suggestion rewrites `*r` to `r.__clone()` and the arm's `s` to `s.__clone()`, at manual-review applicability, since a clone allocates and whether to pay is the author's call. `ownsHeap` gates both: two bindings for one `int` is not a double free. **MEASURED against the predictions.** A compile sweep of all 1008 `.sg` files under `testdata/golden` and `stdlib` reports SEM3197 on EXACTLY ONE fixture — the intended one — where the blanket deletion broke 15 files and stopped a four-line program from building; `core/format.sg:61`, `stdlib/json/json.sg:53` and `recursive_handles.sg:19` are all compare scrutinees and all still compile, and the five pinned `TestCompareBorrowedTupleBindingsAreAliases` subtests pass untouched. The ownership corpus gate reports `attempted=1057 mir_success=569 invalid_failures=399 non_invalid_failures=89 normalized_findings=0` against a base of `1052 / 566 / 397 / 89 / 2`: the +5 attempted and +2 invalid are the fixtures this change adds, the +3 mir_success is four new compiling fixtures minus the one now refused, `non_invalid_failures` is UNCHANGED at 89, and the 15 unrecorded plus 7 stale allowances are the same ones the gate already carried at `5ebb0cec`. `normalized_findings` 2 → 0 is `OWN-001`/`OWN-002` going away with the program that needed them. Both backends verified on the two shapes: `compare *arg { IStr(s) => out + s; }` prints correctly twice on vm and llvm and is accepted; `IStr(s) => s;` double-freed natively at base and is refused now. **NOT ANSWERED HERE, and there are TWO residuals, both with a control standing on them.** (1) Only a DIRECT hand-out is caught: a payload laundered through a call still escapes, which is the deep-laundering shape recorded elsewhere in this ledger. (2) A payload STORED SIDEWAYS escapes too — `compare *slot { Payload(s) => { out.held = s; } }` compiles and leaves two owners for one string, because the rule refuses a deref that PRODUCES a value and an arm that ANSWERS with its payload, and this does neither. That shape is now the report-only negative control for the `--dev` ownership gate in `internal/buildpipeline/ownership_test.go` and `cmd/surge/build_dev_test.go`, which had to be replaced: their previous control did `id = *n.next` and is a compile error now, and a control the default build rejects cannot test what the dev flag does to a build that SUCCEEDS. So the residual is not merely noted, it is exercised on every run — and the day it is closed, those two controls need replacing again, deliberately. Also unfixed on its own merits: `isCopyType` does not recurse into unions, so an Option over a Copy payload is not Copy — it no longer costs anything here because that site is a scrutinee, but it is still wrong. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Language semantics; refused by consuming position, arm escape included; deep-laundered hand-out remains | The question is what `*r` MEANS for a non-Copy heap-owning type, and only one of three answers can be true. (a) A SHARE: then native heap values need reference counts, which is a runtime-model change and runs against the shared-nothing direction. (b) A COPY: then `*r` must clone, which is safe but spends an allocation at a spelling that looks free - the hidden cost the V2 cost model exists to refuse. (c) NOT ALLOWED: refuse `*r` where the pointee type owns heap and is not `Copy`, with `r.__clone()` as the explicit, visibly-priced alternative - what Rust does and for the same reason. Whichever is chosen, note the consequence for (c): `vm_rc_leak_panics` is written on `*a_ref` and would stop compiling, so the VM leak detector's only positive control (RV2-DEBT-114) needs replacing in the same change, and the `OWN-001`/`OWN-002` allowlist rows become unnecessary. Before choosing, measure: run the check over the corpus and stdlib and count how many sites actually write `*r` on an owning type.  |
| RV2-DEBT-192 | SHIFTS AND UNARY NEGATION are not checked on the native backend, so three more fixed-width operations answer where the VM traps. `0a550ad4` gave add, sub, mul and div their overflow checks and scoped these out explicitly rather than half-doing them; this row is that scope-out, filed with measurements. All five programs below are five lines, built at `5ebb0cec` and run on both backends with `SURGE_STDLIB=$(pwd)`: `let a: int8 = -128; -a` — VM `panic VM1101: integer overflow`, exit 1; native prints `-128`, exit 0. `let a: uint8 = 200; a << 1` — VM VM1101; native prints `144`, exit 0. `let a: int8 = 64; a << 1` — VM VM1101; native exit 0. `let a: int8 = 1; a << 9` — VM VM1101; native prints `0`, exit 0. `let a: int8 = 64; a >> -1` — VM VM1101; native prints `0`, exit 0. A non-overflowing `int8 64 >> 2` agrees on both, so the gap is the CHECK and not the operation. **The two sub-cases are not equally bad and the row should not blur them.** A count inside the width is a DEFINED wrap that merely disagrees with the VM — `200 << 1` really is `144` in eight bits. A count that is negative or at least the width is UNDEFINED: LLVM's `shl`/`ashr` yield poison for those, so `1 << 9` printing `0` is not a wrong answer the compiler promised, it is no answer at all, and poison is free to be folded differently at another optimisation level or inlined call site. That half is a soundness hole, not a parity difference. The VM's rule is stricter than C's on purpose and is the specification to match: `evalIntShiftLeft` (`internal/vm/eval_ops_bitwise.go:188`) traps when `right < 0 \|\| right >= bitWidth`, then traps again when the shift would lose a bit — unsigned `val > maxVal>>shift`, signed `left > maxVal>>shift \|\| left < minVal>>shift`, plus a final `checkSignedWidth` — so a shift that discards any significant bit is an overflow rather than a truncation. `evalUnaryMinus` (`internal/vm/eval_ops.go:169`) traps exactly on `operand == minVal`. **No corpus fixture covers any of this**, which is why the behavioural lane is green on both backends today while five programs disagree; that is the ninth instance of the pattern [[gates-can-be-green-without-running]] records, and the fixtures have to land with the fix rather than after it. **FIXED 2026-08-11, and fixing it uncovered a second defect underneath.** The shift guard is written rather than called, since LLVM has no checked-shift intrinsic: one `icmp uge` against the width refuses the count BEFORE the shift is emitted, and ONE unsigned comparison answers both halves — a negative count has its high bit set, so read as unsigned it is at least 2^(width-1), past every width this backend emits, where `icmp sge` would let `-1` through. The value check for `<<` is a round trip (shift back with `ashr` for signed, `lshr` for unsigned, and compare) rather than a per-width maximum, because the round trip is one shape for every width and carries the signedness with it. `>>` gets only the count guard. Negation delegates to the existing `llvm.ssub.with.overflow` path as `0 - x`, since negation IS that subtraction and a second way to read an overflow bit would be a second thing to keep right. Both spellings of each operation are covered: `-x` reaches the emitter as a `__neg` magic call when written plainly and as `emitUnary` inside a compound expression, and both now ask, so the trap does not depend on where the minus was written. **THE SECOND DEFECT: the first build of this fix made `let a: int8 = -128;` PANIC**, which is a program with nothing wrong with it. A negative literal is lowered as `__neg` applied to its MAGNITUDE, and sema — having validated `-128` against int8's range — then types the whole chain `int8` (`materializeNumericLiteral` → `setExprTypes` over `info.exprIDs`), which stamps the inner `128` with a type that cannot hold it. The VM never noticed because its `__neg` negates a value kept in an untruncated int64; any backend that materialises the constant at the type's own width reads that operand as -128 and reports its negation as an overflow. `-<integer literal>` is now folded into the one literal it names, in HIR (`foldNegatedIntLiteral`), for FIXED-WIDTH signed types only — arbitrary-precision `int` carries its literals as text into the bignum runtime, whose parser rejects a leading sign outright, so folding there turns a working `let a: int = -5` into `invalid int literal "-5"`, which is how the boundary was found. The sign joins the literal BEFORE it is parsed rather than after, and that is what also fixes a PRE-EXISTING wrong answer on BOTH backends: `let a: int64 = -9223372036854775808; print(a to string)` printed `0` at `5ebb0cec` and prints itself now, because the magnitude alone has no int64 to be parsed into. Only the builtin negation folds; a user-defined `__neg` is a call the program asked for. EVIDENCE: a generated matrix of 76 programs — every width 8/16/32/64, signed and unsigned, `<<` and `>>`, counts in range / at the width / negative / zero, negation of MIN and of MAX, plus controls for bitwise ops, arbitrary-precision shifts and negation, float negation and compound assignment — agrees with the VM on exit code and message in 76 of 76. `make golden-update` reports NO existing fixture changed, including `magic_ops_call.mir`, which records a `__neg` over a variable and so proves the fold stayed narrow. Four corpus fixtures land with it, and one of them is the one with teeth: `vm_shifts_and_negation_ok` is green on BOTH backends today and would have caught the over-trapping regression above on the first run. The three panic fixtures join the set held by RV2-DEBT-187 — each matches the VM's message, code and `at` line byte for byte and differs by exactly the three backtrace lines. **CLOSED 2026-08-11.** The three panic fixtures produce their recorded `.out` on `--backend=llvm` now that the backtrace exists, and `vm_shifts_and_negation_ok` was already green on both. Verified by a full `make behaviour-check-all`: 0 failures on both backends. The residuals stay as recorded — only DIRECT shapes are checked, and the 76-program parity matrix that proved the fix lives outside the corpus, which is its own thin spot rather than a defect in the check. | Closed 2026-08-11 | Runtime V2 native backend; the checks are in, the fixtures are held by RV2-DEBT-187 | The three panic fixtures produce their recorded `.out` on `--backend=llvm`, which they will when RV2-DEBT-187's backtrace lands; nothing on this row stands in the way, and `vm_shifts_and_negation_ok` already passes on both backends. Shifts and negation stay in the 76-program parity matrix, so an over-trapping fix is caught as fast as an under-trapping one.  |
| RV2-DEBT-203 | A FIXED ARRAY CANNOT CROSS A FUNCTION BOUNDARY, and the inferred spelling MISCOMPILES rather than being refused. Function signature type keys drop the length: `makeTypeKey` (`internal/symbols/function_signature.go`) returns `"[" + elem + "]"` for `ast.TypeExprArray`, discarding all four length fields the AST node carries (`ast.TypeArray.Kind/Length/ConstLength/HasConstLen`). A SECOND producer has the identical bug and would be missed by a fix to the first: `typeKeyForTypeExpr` (`internal/sema/type_decl_union.go`) is a separate function that never calls `makeTypeKey`. The consumer side already supports the `[T; N]` spelling - `parseArrayKey` (`internal/sema/type_array.go`) parses it, nesting-blind-split lesson included - so only the producers are wrong. RE-DERIVED BY THE LEAD at `ad4e8277` on a twelve-line program with no strings and no cycles: a `fn build() -> Item[2]` returning a two-element fixed array of a scalar struct. ANNOTATED (`let a: Item[2] = build();`) is refused at compile time with `cannot assign [Item] to [Item; 2]`, the diagnostic printing both spellings side by side. INFERRED (`let a = build();`) compiles and then breaks: the VM panics `VM1999: storage: stale reference to type#5 at offset 32 (generation 1, arena is at 2)` and the native binary exits **255 with zero bytes of output**. The lane that found it measured the IR behind that: the callee is `define void @fn.2(ptr sret([16 x i8]) align 8 %surge.ret)` while the caller allocates `alloca ptr` - eight bytes receiving a sixteen-byte `sret` write - and then reads the inline storage as an array handle and calls `rt_array_free_elems` on it, freeing frame storage as a heap buffer. NOTE FOR RV2-DEBT-186, which was closed with the argument that `*r` was the only way to reach a silent native 255: this program contains no `*r`, so the SYMPTOM has a second route even though 186's own mechanism stays closed. | Closed 2026-08-12 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1 (fixed arrays) | Both producers emit the `[T; N]` spelling the consumers already parse, and a fixture passes a bare `T[N]` by value, by `&` and by `&mut`, returns one, and round-trips it through a function value, on BOTH lanes. The negative control must be the INFERRED form and not only the annotated one: a fix that merely silences `SEM3015` would leave the miscompile standing, and the annotated form is the half that already fails loudly. **CLOSED by `f3202878`, which this row was never flipped for.** Re-verified by the lead 2026-08-12 at `ab55197c` on the INFERRED form specifically - `fn build() -> Item[2]` with `let xs = build(); return xs[1].a` - which exits 3 on BOTH lanes, agreeing, with no `VM1999` and no native 255. |
| RV2-DEBT-204 | THE NATIVE ELEMENT STORE DOES NOT FREE THE ELEMENT IT OVERWRITES. `xs[i] = v` leaks the displaced value on the native lane; the VM does not. RE-MEASURED 2026-08-12 at `f3202878` with the repo's established free_count-delta method (each shape compared against a whole-binding twin in the same run, probe cost subtracted at runtime): the gate `internal/vm/testdata/place_overwrite_leak.sg` exits 0 on the VM and reports the element shapes leaking on native. **THE ORIGINAL DIAGNOSIS IN THIS ROW WAS WRONG AND IS CORRECTED HERE.** It read the leak as a missing sema obligation. It is not: both lanes run the same sema and the VM frees correctly, so the obligation is RECORDED and only the native discharge is absent. The element path is also structurally separate from every other place: `internal/hir/lower_expr.go` rewrites `xs[i] = v` into an `__index_set` magic CALL and returns BEFORE `BinaryOpData` is built, so `DropOverwritten`, `lowerAssignExpr`'s `InstrDrop` and `materializeBeforeOverwrite` are not on this path at all - which is why this row is separate from RV2-DEBT-208. The native emitter ends both branches of `emitIndexSet` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_calls_intrinsics.go`) in a bare store with no load-and-release, while the VM releases in `viewElemStore` (`internal/vm/array_utils.go`) and `storeStorage` (`internal/vm/storage_value.go`); a reviewer proved the VM rule load-bearing by deleting it and watching VM3302 fire. Consequences in the standard library: `core/array.sg`'s `reverse_in_place` is `self[i] = clone(self[j])` and leaks once per swap, natively only. **CLOSED by `9577000e`, which this row was never flipped for**, releasing through `drop_elem.typeN` between RHS and store in both `emitIndexSet` branches. Re-verified by the lead 2026-08-12 at `ab55197c`: the element bits (16, 32) of `runtime-v2-place-overwrite-check` report zero on both lanes. Note that landing this release is what DETONATED RV2-DEBT-209 - the leak had been masking a double free - so the two rows must stay readable together. | Closed 2026-08-12 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1 (element storage) | The native element store frees the displaced element, matching what the VM already does, and `make runtime-v2-place-overwrite-check` reports zero for the element bits on both lanes. DO NOT reach for a blind rule ("any store through a projection releases") - an adversarial reviewer demonstrated it introduces a DOUBLE FREE, because `@drop o.inner; o.inner = Inner{...}` is clean today and the native emitter does not null out a moved-from place after `field_move`. |
| RV2-DEBT-205 | A NESTED ARRAY ELEMENT READ THROUGH `compare` GIVES A SILENTLY WRONG ANSWER ON NATIVE. `compare values.pop() { Some(inner) => inner[0]; ... }` over a `uint64[][]` prints `6` on the VM and `324839859598668` on the native lane, and native exits 0 - so this is a wrong answer, not a crash, and no gate can see it today. Reported by the D1 scoping review, which built and ran both lanes at `ad4e8277`. NOT RE-DERIVED BY THE LEAD: the row is filed on the reviewer's measurement so it is not lost, and re-deriving it is the first step of working it. **RE-DERIVED AND CLOSED 2026-08-12. IT IS A USE-AFTER-FREE, NOT A WRONG ANSWER** - this row understated it. Under valgrind the native binary reports `Invalid read of size 8` at an address `0 bytes inside a block of size 8 free'd by` `rt_free <- array_free_base_storage (rt_array_reclaim.c:79) <- rt_array_free_elems <- rt_array_free`; the printed number is the contents of a freed block, and the program exits 0. THE DECOMPOSITION THIS ROW ASKED FOR RULES OUT TWO OF ITS THREE CANDIDATES: with the same `pop` temporary and the same element reads, `Some(inner) => inner[0] + inner[1]` is CORRECT on both lanes, as are `inner[0] + 0`, a typed `let v: uint64 = inner[0]`, `inner.__len()`, and a scalar payload. It fires only when the payload is an ARRAY and the arm's RESULT is a BARE element read. That is the signature of a lazy place-read: an indexed read stays a call returning `&T` (`lowerIndexExpr`), so `wrapArmDrops` builds a block whose value is a POINTER, the payload frees on the way out, and the load the consumer wraps AROUND the block reads storage already returned. `wrapArmDrops` had already moved the drops after the value for precisely this reason - its comment says so - and that is right for a value and not enough for a reference. THE DEFECT IS WIDER THAN THIS ROW RECORDED: an untyped `let v = inner[0]` (which infers a reference), a nested `inner[0][0]`, and a struct field `inner.xs[0]` are all broken the same way, so a fix validated only against the reported spelling would be undertested. FIXED in `lowerValueExpr` (`internal/mir/lower_expr_helpers.go`) by `loadInsideDropCarryingBlock`, which rebuilds a drop-carrying block so its value is LOADED before the drops run. No drop is added, removed or reordered and no ownership moves - the identical load instruction moves from after the free to before it. The obvious site, `wrapArmDrops` in HIR, was tried and REFUSED BY MEASUREMENT: HIR does not know the consuming context, and a deref there turns `let got: &uint64 = compare ... { Some(inner) => &inner[0]; ... }` into invalid LLVM IR. `lowerValueExpr` runs only where the consumer wants a value, so that shape is untouched by construction - it stays broken and is RV2-DEBT-214. Evidence: valgrind `7 allocs, 7 frees, all heap blocks were freed, 0 errors`; the behavioural fixture `testdata/golden/vm_compare/compare_arm_element_read.sg` executes on BOTH lanes (verified by name in a `-v` run, not inferred from a green target) and prints `26315006279\|13\|26315006279` natively against `6\|13\|14` on the VM at `32a61010`; and `TestRuntimeV2ArmResultReadHappensBeforeTheArmFrees` fails at `32a61010` with the invalid read. `golden-check` at 500s changed no existing fixture. | Closed 2026-08-12 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1 (element storage) | The reproducer agrees on both backends, with a behavioural fixture recording it so the corpus can see it. Establish first whether the defect is in the `pop` temporary's lifetime, in the arm payload binding, or in the element read - the three are separable and the row should not assume which. |
| RV2-DEBT-206 | A FIXED-ARRAY SLICE LEAKS ITS VIEW HEADER, EVERY TIME, and its data pointer aims into a frame slot. `rt_array_slice_fixed` (`runtime/native/rt_array.c`) calls `array_alloc_view` and returns without registering the view, while the dynamic sibling `rt_array_slice` calls `array_register_view`. The licence for that is the comment at `rt_array_internal.h` - "fixed-array slices register no link" - and measured against a dynamic control it is wrong: valgrind reports `definitely lost: 48 bytes in 2 blocks` on a fixed-slice program, one block attributed to `array_alloc_view` through `rt_array_slice_fixed`, while the dynamic control loses only the shared `Range` temporary and keeps its view header reachable. Beyond the leak the view outliving its frame is a dangling read, since the backing is a stack slot rather than a heap block. Reported by the D1 scoping review. **RE-DERIVED BY THE LEAD 2026-08-12 AND IT IS THREE DEFECTS, NOT ONE.** Measured at `f58bd24b`: the fixed-slice program loses `48 bytes in 2 blocks` against the dynamic control's `24 in 1`, and BOTH GROW PER OPERATION - 16 slices leak 32 blocks fixed and 16 dynamic, exactly two and one per slice. Attribution: one block from `rt_range_int_new` in BOTH, one from `array_alloc_view` via `rt_array_slice_fixed` in the fixed case only. **THIS ROW'S FIRST ANSWER IS IMPOSSIBLE AND IS WITHDRAWN**: `rt_array_slice_fixed` cannot 'register against its backing' because `array_register_view` keys a view to a base `SurgeArrayHeader` and a fixed array HAS none - its storage is the caller's frame slot, passed as raw `elems`. So the choice was never between two answers. **(1) THE VIEW-HEADER LEAK IS CLOSED**, and it was not a runtime defect at all: sema classified `let v = xs[[1..3]]` as a projection READ, marked the binding aliased, and `liveDroppables` skips aliased bindings, so no drop was recorded and both backends faithfully emitted nothing. `temp_drops.go` already said the opposite about the identical expression through the same predicate - 'Only slice expressions MINT a value (an owned view header)' - which is why `consume(xs[[1..3]])` reclaimed its header while the bound form did not. One early return in `projectionReadAliasesItsSource` makes the two agree. Fixed 48/2 -> 24/1, and 768/32 -> 384/16 at sixteen slices. Freeing the header cannot free the frame slot: a view carries the `cap == SURGE_ARRAY_VIEW_CAP` sentinel, so `rt_array_free_elems` reaches `array_free_header` (the 24-byte header alone) and `array_free_base_storage` - the only site that frees `data` - is unreachable for it. **(2) THE `Range` TEMPORARY IS NOT A SLICE DEFECT** and is filed as RV2-DEBT-215/216: indexing MATERIALISES a Range, and there is no `rt_range_free` in the runtime at all. **(3) THE ESCAPING VIEW REMAINS OPEN AND IS THIS ROW'S REMAINING HALF**, measured: `fn f() -> uint64[] { let xs: uint64[4] = [...]; return xs[[1..3]]; }` reads a reclaimed frame natively - exit 96 where 22 is correct, exit 0 - while the VM panics `VM3301 stale reference`. So the capability this row worried about removing ALREADY does not work on the VM lane and 'works' natively only by reading garbage. **BOTH HALVES CLOSED 2026-08-13.** The header leak by making sema agree with itself (`projectionReadAliasesItsSource` now answers what `temp_drops.go` already answered), and the escape by `SemaFixedArrayViewEscapes` (SEM3198), a refusal modelled on `checkBorrowEscapeOnReturn` and reusing its storage question verbatim, so a view over a REFERENCE parameter stays allowed. Provenance is CARRIED from the slice expression to the binding rather than re-derived, because a view's surface type is `T[]` whatever it was sliced from. THE ACCEPTANCE FIXTURE IS THE LOAD-BEARING TEST: the obvious unit test PASSES FOR THE WRONG REASON - the snippet harness has no stdlib, so `xs[[1..3]]` never resolves `Range`, and an acceptance test written there reports nothing and would stay green with the rule deleted. `testdata/golden/sema/valid/fixed_array_view_stays_in_frame.sg` has an empty `.diag` covering a reference-param view, a dynamic view and a fixed view used inside one frame, and is what fails if the predicate widens. The remaining per-slice block is the `Range` temporary, RV2-DEBT-215/216. | Closed 2026-08-13 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1 (fixed arrays) | OWNER RULING 2026-08-12: sema REFUSES an escaping fixed view with a diagnostic naming the real cause. The other option this row offered - registering the view - is structurally impossible and is withdrawn above. The header-leak half is closed; what remains is the refusal. Evidence for it: the four escaping shapes (bare return, return of a bound view, a view inside a returned aggregate, a store into an enclosing-scope binding) are refused, AND the allowed set stays allowed - every non-escaping local use, a view over a `&T[N]` REFERENCE parameter returned (correct on both lanes today), and any dynamic-array view. A rule that also refuses a fixed slice used and dropped inside one frame would remove far more than was agreed. Note a declared conservatism: a fixed array nested in heap storage resolves to a frame-local base and will be refused although its data is not on the frame - that refuses a leak rather than a dangle. **DATED QUALIFICATION 2026-08-13: THIS ROW'S CLOSE CONDITION ALLOWS "any dynamic-array view" TO ESCAPE, AND THAT ALLOWANCE IS MEASURED INCOMPLETE.** The reasoning was that a dynamic base has a header and the view registry keeps it alive. The registry does defer - `rt_array_reclaim.c` says a base with live views defers data AND header until the last view unlinks - and the ANSWER is right because of it: `fn build_slice() -> Item[]` returning `out[[1..3]]` over a LOCAL dynamic array prints 55, correctly. But valgrind still reports **2 errors from 1 context** on it, an `Invalid read of size 8` on a block freed by `array_free_base_storage` during teardown in the CALLER. The non-escaping twin over the same dynamic array is clean (0 errors). So escaping is what breaks it, deferral is not sufficient, and the refusal this row shipped (SEM3198) does not cover the dynamic case by design. Filed as RV2-DEBT-218; this row stays closed for the two halves it actually closed. |
| RV2-DEBT-208 | THE NATIVE STORE DOES NOT FREE THE VALUE IT OVERWRITES AT ANY NON-ELEMENT PLACE EITHER - a struct field, a nested field, a tuple element, or a store through `&mut`. Split from RV2-DEBT-204 by owner ruling 2026-08-12, which named one third of its own defect; measurement then corrected the REASON for the split. MEASURED at `f3202878` by `internal/vm/testdata/place_overwrite_leak.sg`: on native all four non-element shapes report a shortfall against the whole-binding twin in the same run, and the VM reports none. So this is NOT a missing obligation in sema - the VM runs the same sema and frees correctly - it is the native discharge. The whole-binding shape is correct on both lanes and is the gate's reference twin. Distinct from RV2-DEBT-204 because the paths do not meet: an element assignment becomes an `__index_set` magic call, while these shapes reach `storeIntoPlace` (`internal/backend/llvm/emit_instr.go`) through `lowerAssignExpr`, whose `InstrDrop` fires only under `DropOverwritten`. **CLOSED 2026-08-12 in sema, by owner ruling on the fix point.** `recordPlaceOldDrop` (`internal/sema/drop_obligations.go`) records the obligation for the assignment INTO a place, called from `handleAssignment` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go`) BEFORE `revivePlace`, because reviving clears the moved-set the record's own guard reads. Nothing downstream changed: MIR already lowers the assignment's LHS to a projected place and emits `InstrDrop` on it, and BOTH lanes already discharged a projected drop by releasing AND THEN CLEARING - `execDropProjected` zeroes the member, `emitInstrDrop` nulls the slot - which is why the record does not collide with the VM's own release inside `storeStorage`: by the time the store reads what it displaces, the slot is empty. That question was measured, not assumed, including the composite-member shape (`h.inner = Item{...}`) that the fixture does not cover and that takes the VM's `moveComposite` path instead of its cell path: agreeing exit on both lanes, no `PanicRCUseAfterFree`. EVIDENCE: valgrind on the four shapes, eight iterations each, went from `108 allocs, 76 frees, definitely lost: 2,048 bytes in 32 blocks` (32 = four shapes times eight iterations) to `108 allocs, 108 frees, all heap blocks were freed`, with the negative control taken by reverting only the two sema files and rebuilding. Four guards keep the record from freeing twice, each against a demonstrated shape: an index segment is not recorded (that path is `__index_set` and RV2-DEBT-204 discharges it at the store); an aliased base is not recorded (`let b = outer.item` leaves ownership with `outer`); a place OVERLAPPING anything in the moved-set is not recorded, which is `@drop o.inner; o.inner = Inner{...}`, the exact program that refuted the blind native-side release - overlap and not equality, because `placeMoved` answers only for the place named and would miss both `o.inner.deep` below it and `o` above it; and a compound assignment is not recorded, because its lowering never consults the overwritten-value drop and the record would be an obligation nothing discharges. The stale comment this row required to go with the fix is gone. The gate now reports zero on both lanes, its `runtime_v2_pending` build tag is removed, and it therefore runs inside `make check` and the pre-commit hook; two valgrind e2e witnesses (`internal/vm/runtime_v2_place_overwrite_e2e_test.go`) hold the reclamation and the not-a-double-free half, and both were shown to fail against the unfixed tree. **THE FIRST FORM OF THIS FIX WAS WRONG AND AN ADVERSARIAL REVIEW CAUGHT IT** - see RV2-DEBT-211. Recording the obligation for `for it in xs { it.name = ... }` freed the array's storage through a loop binding that never owned it, turning a silent leak into `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`. Every measurement listed above was already green when that regression was live, because all four e2e shapes use bindings the frame owns and the VM lane cannot witness it at all. The guard list therefore has a FIFTH entry: a non-owning binding is not recorded, and that mark is deliberately separate from the alias mark because assignment clears the alias mark. | Closed 2026-08-12 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1 | The four non-element bits of `make runtime-v2-place-overwrite-check` report zero on both lanes. Two candidate fix points and the choice is a design decision: record the obligation for enumerable places in sema so the native lane receives an `InstrDrop` it already honours (a scoping spike measured this driving the leaks to zero), or release inside the native store. The second in blind form is REFUSED - it double-frees a moved-from place, demonstrated. Whichever is taken, the stale comment at `internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go` claiming a per-field obligation "has no way to reach a backend that cannot drop a projection yet" must go with it: both backends drop projections now. |
| RV2-DEBT-209 | AN ARRAY OR TUPLE LITERAL ALIASES A NAMED BINDING INSTEAD OF MOVING IT, so the value has two owners and is freed twice. Six lines, native lane, MEASURED 2026-08-12 at `2f83e826`: `let a: string = "..."; let mut strs: string[] = [a, "..."];` then reading `a` — `rt_string_len(&a)` returns 28, so the binding is still live after the literal took it — and the program dies at scope exit with `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`, exit 255. No element assignment is involved; the literal alone is enough. Same family as RV2-DEBT-191 (`*r` aliasing rather than copying), and native arrays carry no refcount, so no traversal order can arbitrate between the two owners. **ROOT CAUSE, located 2026-08-12:** this project funnels every consuming position through ONE gate, `observeMove` (`internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go`), and it has 21 call sites - `let`, `return`, block tail, call argument, struct-literal field, compare scrutinee, arm result, crossing/blocking/async capture, select send, assignment RHS. **The array literal and the tuple literal are not among them.** Their sibling the struct literal IS, and the asymmetry is measurable in three programs: `Foo { name = a }` then reading `a` is REFUSED with "use of moved value `a`", while `[a, "..."]` and `(a, 1)` both accept the read and then double-free at scope exit. So this is one missing wiring in two places, not a representation problem. **IT BLOCKS RV2-DEBT-204, AND THE WAY IT BLOCKS IT IS THE POINT.** Overwriting such an element is CLEAN today only because the overwrite LEAKS the displaced value: the leak is what keeps the second owner from ever freeing what the first one will. Landing 204's release — freeing the displaced element, which is correct in itself — removes the leak and the double free lands immediately. Demonstrated both ways: `strs[0] = "..."` after `[a, ...]` exits 0 at base and 255 with the release, while the same program without the named binding is clean under both. So a leak was masking a double free, and fixing the leak is what exposes it. Also worth carrying: `make check` cannot see any of this - `behaviour-check-all` is not part of it, which is how the corpus stayed green while `arrays_drop_nested` was one commit away from aborting. **CLOSED by `b4d17fe1`, which this row was never flipped for**, wiring both literals into `observeMove`. Re-verified by the lead 2026-08-12 at `ab55197c` in the form this row asks for: after `let mut strs: string[] = [a, "second"]`, reading `a` is REFUSED with `use of moved value 'a'`, and the tuple literal `(a, 1)` refuses identically - the asymmetry against the struct literal is gone. | Closed 2026-08-12 | Epic 23b Wave D step D1; blocks RV2-DEBT-204 | An array literal MOVES the value out of a named binding, so the binding is dead afterwards and only the array frees it - or the aliasing is refused with a diagnostic naming the real cause, as RV2-DEBT-191 did for `*r`. Evidence is the six-line program at strict-zero under valgrind with `a` unreadable after the literal, plus `runtime-v2-place-overwrite-check` reaching zero for the element bits once 204's release lands on top. Fix this FIRST: 204's release is written and measured (it takes the gate from 63 to 15, closing exactly the element bits) and was reverted only because of this row. |
| RV2-DEBT-211 | FREEING THROUGH A `for` LOOP BINDING FREES STORAGE THE CONTAINER STILL OWNS, and then the container frees it again. `for it in xs` binds a WORD-WISE COPY of the element - MIR shows `L15 = tag_payload_move copy L11.Some[0]`, and there is no `drop L15` anywhere, because the array is still the owner - but sema's ownership bookkeeping had no way to say so: `markAliasedBinding` is called at exactly two sites (`internal/sema/type_checker_walk.go` for a `let` with a projection-read initializer, and `internal/sema/borrow_runtime_ops.go` for a whole-binding assignment) and `walkForInStmt` (`internal/sema/type_checker_for_in.go`) is neither. **THE WHOLE-BINDING SPELLING WAS ALREADY AN INVALID FREE AT `ab55197c`, BEFORE ANY OF THIS WAVE'S WORK**: `for s in strs { s = mk(7); }` aborts natively with `free(): double free detected in tcache 2`, exit 255, valgrind `28 allocs, 28 frees, Invalid free(), ERROR SUMMARY: 7 errors from 3 contexts`. The PLACE spelling (`for it in xs { it.name = mk(7); }`) was merely a silent leak at base - exit 0, one leak record - and became an invalid free the moment RV2-DEBT-208's place obligation was recorded. THAT IS THIS PROJECT'S OWN PATTERN A THIRD TIME: the leak was the only thing keeping the second owner from freeing what the first one would, and it was found by an adversarial review lens asked to hunt double frees rather than by any gate. Neither the VM lane nor the behaviour corpus can witness it: the VM zeroes the member in `execDropProjected` and behaves identically at base.
